, Sec. 3.3]
. As for
\mathcal{I}_{2}
, since
\overline{q}_{0}<1,
then taking
\varepsilon_{0}
and
a_{0}
small enough, the integrand inside (
4.12
) is not singular. This gives the desired regularity in
r
with respect to the desired function spaces (loss of only one derivative). For the parity property, it is obtained by immediate changes of variables. Thus, it remains to proves that the quantities (
4.11
)-(
4.12
) are actually not singular in
\varepsilon q.
From the identities

\displaystyle\log(|R(\theta)e^{{\rm i}\theta}-R(\eta)e^{{\rm i}\eta}|)

\displaystyle=\log\big(\big|e^{{\rm i}\theta}-e^{{\rm i}\eta}\big|\big)+\log\big(\big|1+\tfrac{(R(\theta)-1)e^{{\rm i}\theta}-(R(\eta)-1)e^{{\rm i}\eta}}{e^{{\rm i}\theta}-e^{{\rm i}\eta}}\big|\big),

\displaystyle\forall|z|<1,\quad\log|1+z|

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{k+1}}{k}\textnormal{Re}\big\{z^{k}\big\},

we obtain the following decomposition

\mathcal{I}_{1}=\mathcal{I}_{1,1}+\mathcal{I}_{1,2}+\mathcal{I}_{1,3},

(4.13)

with

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{1,1}(\varepsilon,q)(\theta)

\displaystyle\triangleq\frac{1}{\varepsilon q}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\log(|e^{{\rm i}\theta}-e^{{\rm i}\eta}|)\sin(\theta-\eta)d\eta,

(4.14)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{1,2}(\varepsilon,q,r)(\theta)

\displaystyle\triangleq\frac{1}{\varepsilon q}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\log(|e^{{\rm i}\theta}-e^{{\rm i}\eta}|)\partial^{2}_{\theta\eta}\big[\big(R(\theta)R(\eta)-1\big)\sin(\theta-\eta)\big]d\eta,

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{1,3}(\varepsilon,q,r)(\theta)

\displaystyle\triangleq\frac{1}{\varepsilon q}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{k+1}}{k}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\textnormal{Re}\Big\{\Big(\tfrac{(R(\theta)-1)e^{{\rm i}\theta}-(R(\eta)-1)e^{{\rm i}\eta}}{e^{{\rm i}\theta}-e^{{\rm i}\eta}}\Big)^{k}\Big\}\partial^{2}_{\theta\eta}\big[R(\theta)R(\eta)\sin(\theta-\eta)\big]d\eta.

First observe that by parity argument,

\mathcal{I}_{1,1}(\varepsilon,q,r)=0.

(4.15)

Moreover, expanding the second term we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{1,2}(\varepsilon,q,r)(\theta)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon q\int_{\mathbb{T}}\log(|e^{{\rm i}\theta}-e^{{\rm i}\eta}|)\frac{\partial_{\theta}r(\theta)\partial_{\eta}r(\eta)}{R(\theta)R(\eta)}\sin(\theta-\eta)d\eta

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{T}}\log(|e^{{\rm i}\theta}-e^{{\rm i}\eta}|)\left(\frac{\partial_{\theta}r(\theta)}{R(\theta)}R(\eta)-\frac{\partial_{\eta}r(\eta)}{R(\eta)}R(\theta)\right)\cos(\theta-\eta)d\eta

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\varepsilon q}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\log\big(\big|e^{{\rm i}\theta}-e^{{\rm i}\eta}|)\big(R(\theta)R(\eta)-1\big)\sin(\theta-\eta)d\eta.

Remark that one may write

R(\theta)R(\eta)-1=\big(R(\theta)-1\big)\big(R(\eta)-1\big)+\big(R(\theta)-1\big)+\big(R(\eta)-1\big)

and, by Taylor formula,

R(\theta)-1=\varepsilon q\int_{0}^{1}\frac{(\partial_{\theta}r)(s\theta)}{R(s\theta)}ds.

(4.16)

Therefore, we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{1,2}(\varepsilon,q,r)(\theta)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon q\int_{\mathbb{T}}\log(|e^{{\rm i}\theta}-e^{{\rm i}\eta}|)\frac{\partial_{\theta}r(\theta)\partial_{\eta}r(\eta)}{R(\theta)R(\eta)}\sin(\theta-\eta)d\eta

(4.17)

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{T}}\log(|e^{{\rm i}\theta}-e^{{\rm i}\eta}|)\left(\frac{\partial_{\theta}r(\theta)}{R(\theta)}R(\eta)-\frac{\partial_{\eta}r(\eta)}{R(\eta)}R(\theta)\right)\cos(\theta-\eta)d\eta

\displaystyle+\varepsilon q\int_{\mathbb{T}}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\log\big(\big|e^{{\rm i}\theta}-e^{{\rm i}\eta}|)\frac{(\partial_{\theta}r)(s_{1}\theta)}{R(s_{1}\theta)}\frac{(\partial_{\eta}r)(s_{2}\eta)}{R(s_{2}\eta)}\sin(\theta-\eta)ds_{1}ds_{2}d\eta

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{T}}\int_{0}^{1}\log\big(\big|e^{{\rm i}\theta}-e^{{\rm i}\eta}|)\frac{(\partial_{\theta}r)(s_{1}\theta)}{R(s_{1}\theta)}\sin(\theta-\eta)ds_{1}d\eta

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{T}}\int_{0}^{1}\log\big(\big|e^{{\rm i}\theta}-e^{{\rm i}\eta}|)\frac{(\partial_{\eta}r)(s_{2}\eta)}{R(s_{2}\eta)}\sin(\theta-\eta)ds_{2}d\eta.

This implies that
\mathcal{I}_{1,2}
is not singular in
\varepsilon q
and actually smooth with respect to
\varepsilon
and
q
. In addition, one obtains from (
4.17
) that

\mathcal{I}_{1,2}(0,q,0)=0.

(4.18)

As for the last term in (
4.13
) we use the identity

\displaystyle\textnormal{Re}\Big\{\Big(\tfrac{(R(\theta)-1)e^{{\rm i}\theta}-(R(\eta)-1)e^{{\rm i}\eta}}{e^{{\rm i}\theta}-e^{{\rm i}\eta}}\Big)^{k}\Big\}

\displaystyle=\textnormal{Re}\Big\{\Big(R(\theta)-1+\tfrac{R(\theta)-R(\eta)}{e^{{\rm i}\theta}-e^{{\rm i}\eta}}e^{{\rm i}\eta}\Big)^{k}\Big\}

\displaystyle=\textnormal{Re}\Big\{\Big(R(\theta)-1+\tfrac{R(\theta)-R(\eta)}{2{\rm i}\sin(\frac{\theta-\eta}{2})}e^{{\rm i}\frac{\eta-\theta}{2}}\Big)^{k}\Big\}.

Using one more time Taylor formula, we can write

\displaystyle R(\theta)-R(\eta)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon q\big(r(\theta)-r(\eta)\big)\mathtt{I}(\varepsilon,q,r,\theta,\eta),

(4.19)

\displaystyle\mathtt{I}(\varepsilon,q,r,\theta,\eta)

\displaystyle\triangleq\int_{0}^{1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+2\varepsilon q\left(r(\eta)+s(r(\theta)-r(\eta))\right)}}ds.

Combining (
4.16
), (
4.19
) and (
4.14
), we infer

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{1,3}(\varepsilon,q,r)(\theta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\tfrac{(-1)^{k+1}}{k}(\varepsilon q)^{k-1}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\textnormal{Re}\left\{\left(\int_{0}^{1}\tfrac{(\partial_{\theta}r)(s\theta)}{R(s\theta)}ds+\tfrac{r(\theta)-r(\eta)}{2{\rm i}\sin\left(\frac{\theta-\eta}{2}\right)}\mathtt{I}(\varepsilon,q,r,\theta,\eta)\right)^{k}\right\}\partial_{\theta\eta}^{2}\big[R(\theta)R(\eta)\sin(\theta-\eta)\big]d\eta.

Therefore,
\mathcal{I}_{1,3}
is not singular in
\varepsilon q
and actually smooth with respect to
\varepsilon
and
q.
In addition,

\mathcal{I}_{1,3}(0,q,0)=0.

(4.20)

The previous calculations show that
\mathcal{I}_{1}
is not singular in
\varepsilon q
and can actually been prolongated in
\varepsilon
on an interval of the form
(-\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{0})
and in
q
in an interval of the form
(\overline{q}_{0}-a_{0},\overline{q}_{0}+a_{0}).
Moreover, gathering (
4.13
), (
4.15
), (
4.18
) and (
4.20
) gives

\mathcal{I}_{1}(0,q,0)=0.

(4.21)

For
\mathcal{I}_{2}(\varepsilon,q,r)
we write

\mathcal{I}_{2}=\mathcal{I}_{2,1}+\mathcal{I}_{2,2}+\mathcal{I}_{2,3},

with

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{2,1}(r)(\theta)

\displaystyle\triangleq-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial_{\theta}r(\theta)}{R^{2}(\theta)},

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{2,2}(\varepsilon,q,r)(\theta)

\displaystyle\triangleq\frac{1}{\varepsilon q}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\textnormal{Re}\Big\{\Big(\big(\varepsilon R(\theta)e^{-{\rm i}\theta}+q\big)\big(\varepsilon R(\eta)e^{{\rm i}\eta}+q\big)\Big)^{k}\Big\}\sin(\theta-\eta)d\eta,

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{2,3}(\varepsilon,q,r)(\theta)

\displaystyle\triangleq\frac{1}{\varepsilon q}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\textnormal{Re}\Big\{\Big(\big(\varepsilon R(\theta)e^{-{\rm i}\theta}+q\big)\big(\varepsilon R(\eta)e^{{\rm i}\eta}+q\big)\Big)^{k}\Big\}\partial^{2}_{\theta\eta}\Big[\Big(\tfrac{R(\eta)}{R(\theta)}-1\Big)\sin(\theta-\eta)\Big]d\eta.

First observe that

\mathcal{I}_{2,1}(0)=0.

(4.22)

Notice that for any
k\in\mathbb{N}^{*},

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{k}(\varepsilon,q,r)(\theta)

\displaystyle\triangleq\frac{1}{\varepsilon q}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\textnormal{Re}\Big\{\Big(\big(\varepsilon R(\theta)e^{-{\rm i}\theta}+q\big)\big(\varepsilon R(\eta)e^{{\rm i}\eta}+q\big)\Big)^{k}\Big\}\sin(\theta-\eta)d\eta

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\varepsilon q}\sum_{n=0}^{k}\sum_{m=0}^{k}\binom{k}{n}\binom{k}{m}q^{2k-m-n}\varepsilon^{n+m}R^{n}(\theta)\textnormal{Re}\Big\{e^{-{\rm i}n\theta}\int_{\mathbb{T}}R^{m}(\eta)e^{{\rm i}m\eta}\sin(\theta-\eta)d\eta\Big\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\varepsilon q}\sum_{n=0}^{k}\sum_{m=0}^{k}\binom{k}{n}\binom{k}{m}q^{2k-m-n}\varepsilon^{n+m}R^{n}(\theta)\textnormal{Re}\Big\{e^{-{\rm i}n\theta}\int_{\mathbb{T}}e^{{\rm i}m\eta}\sin(\theta-\eta)d\eta\Big\}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\varepsilon q}\sum_{n=0}^{k}\sum_{m=0}^{k}\binom{k}{n}\binom{k}{m}q^{2k-m-n}\varepsilon^{n+m}R^{n}(\theta)\textnormal{Re}\Big\{e^{-{\rm i}n\theta}\int_{\mathbb{T}}(R^{m}(\eta)-1)e^{{\rm i}m\eta}\sin(\theta-\eta)d\eta\Big\}.

Also, straightforward computations give

\textnormal{Re}\Big\{e^{-{\rm i}n\theta}\int_{\mathbb{T}}e^{{\rm i}m\eta}\sin(\theta-\eta)d\eta\Big\}=\begin{cases}0,&\textnormal{if}\quad m\neq 1,\\
-\tfrac{1}{2}\sin\big((n-1)\theta\big),&\textnormal{if}\quad m=1.\end{cases}

Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{k}(\varepsilon,q,r)(\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{k}{2}q^{2(k-1)}\sin(\theta)+\mathcal{J}_{k,1}(\varepsilon,q,r)(\theta),

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{k,1}(\varepsilon,q,r)(\theta)

\displaystyle\triangleq-\frac{k}{2}\sum_{n=2}^{k}\binom{k}{n}q^{2(k-1)-n}\varepsilon^{n}R^{n}(\theta)\sin\big((n-1)\theta\big)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\varepsilon q}\sum_{n=0}^{k}\sum_{m=0}^{k}\begin{pmatrix}k\\
n\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}k\\
m\end{pmatrix}q^{2k-m-n}\varepsilon^{n+m}R^{n}(\theta)\textnormal{Re}\Big\{e^{-{\rm i}n\theta}\int_{\mathbb{T}}(R^{m}(\eta)-1)e^{{\rm i}m\eta}\sin(\theta-\eta)d\eta\Big\}.

Observe that

R^{m}(\eta)-1=\big(R(\eta)-1\big)\sum_{\ell=0}^{m-1}R^{m-1-\ell}(\eta).

Hence, making appeal to (
4.16
), we infer

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{k,1}(\varepsilon,q,r)(\theta)=-\frac{k}{2}\sum_{n=2}^{k}\binom{k}{n}q^{2(k-1)-n}\varepsilon^{n}R^{n}(\theta)\sin\big((n-1)\theta\big)

\displaystyle+\sum_{n=0}^{k}\sum_{m=0}^{k}\sum_{\ell=0}^{m-1}\begin{pmatrix}k\\
n\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}k\\
m\end{pmatrix}q^{2k-m-n}\varepsilon^{n+m}R^{n}(\theta)\textnormal{Re}\Big\{e^{-{\rm i}n\theta}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\int_{0}^{1}\tfrac{(\partial_{\eta}r)(s\eta)}{R(s\eta)}dsR^{m-1-\ell}(\eta)e^{{\rm i}m\eta}\sin(\theta-\eta)d\eta\Big\}.

We have removed the singularity and it is immediate that

\mathcal{J}_{k,1}(0,q,0)=0.

(4.23)

Thus, we have the following decomposition

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{2,2}(\varepsilon,q,r)(\theta)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}q^{2(k-1)}\sin(\theta)+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\tfrac{1}{k}\mathcal{J}_{k,1}(\varepsilon,q,r)(\theta)

\displaystyle\triangleq\frac{1}{2(1-q^{2})}\sin(\theta)+\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{2,2}(\varepsilon,q,r)(\theta).

From what preceeds,
\mathcal{I}_{2,2}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{2,2}
are smooth in
\varepsilon
and
q
. Moreover, (
4.23
) implies

\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{2,2}(0,q,0)=0.

(4.24)

Besides, another use of (
4.16
) yields

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{2,3}(\varepsilon,q,r)(\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\varepsilon q}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\textnormal{Re}\Big\{\Big(\big(\varepsilon R(\theta)e^{-{\rm i}\theta}+q\big)\big(\varepsilon R(\eta)e^{{\rm i}\eta}+q\big)\Big)^{k}\Big\}\partial^{2}_{\theta\eta}\Big[\Big(\tfrac{R(\eta)}{R(\theta)}-1\Big)\sin(\theta-\eta)\Big]d\eta

\displaystyle=-\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\textnormal{Re}\Big\{\Big(\big(\varepsilon R(\theta)e^{-{\rm i}\theta}+q\big)\big(\varepsilon R(\eta)e^{{\rm i}\eta}+q\big)\Big)^{k}\Big\}\partial_{\theta}\Big[\int_{0}^{1}\tfrac{(\partial_{\eta}r)(s\eta)}{R(\theta)R(s\eta)}ds\cos(\theta-\eta)\Big]d\eta

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\textnormal{Re}\Big\{\Big(\big(\varepsilon R(\theta)e^{-{\rm i}\theta}+q\big)\big(\varepsilon R(\eta)e^{{\rm i}\eta}+q\big)\Big)^{k}\Big\}\partial_{\theta}\Big[\tfrac{(\partial_{\eta}r)(\eta)}{R(\theta)R(\eta)}\sin(\theta-\eta)\Big]d\eta.

One readily sees that
\mathcal{I}_{2,3}
is smooth and that

\mathcal{I}_{2,3}(0,q,0)=0.

(4.25)

Combining the foregoing calculations, we find that

\mathcal{G}(\varepsilon,q,\Omega,r)=\varepsilon^{2}\Omega\partial_{\theta}r(\theta)+\Omega\partial_{\theta}\big[R(\theta)\cos(\theta)\big]+\frac{1}{2(1-q^{2})}\sin(\theta)+\mathcal{I}_{3}(\varepsilon,q,r)(\theta),

(4.26)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{3}

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathcal{I}_{1}+\mathcal{I}_{2,1}+\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{2,2}+\mathcal{I}_{2,3}

is a smooth function. Finally, putting together (
4.21
), (
4.22
), (
4.24
) and (
4.25
), we get

\mathcal{I}_{3}(0,q,0)=0.

(4.27)

(ii)
From (
4.26
) and (
4.27
), we find

\mathcal{G}(0,q,\Omega,0)=-\Big[\Omega-\tfrac{1}{2(1-q^{2})}\Big]\sin(\theta).

(4.28)

This gives the desired equivalence.

(iii)
The linearized operator of
\mathcal{G}
with respect to
(\Omega,r)
at
(\varepsilon,\Omega,r)=(0,\Omega_{0},0)
is given by

d_{(\Omega,r)}\mathcal{G}(0,q,\Omega_{0},0)[(\widehat{\Omega},h)]=-\widehat{\Omega}\sin(\theta)-\tfrac{1}{2}\big[\partial_{\theta}-\mathbf{H}\big]h(\theta).

Given
g\in\mathbb{Y}_{\textnormal{\tiny{odd}}}^{s}
in the form

g(\theta)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}g_{n}\sin(n\theta),\quad g_{n}\in\mathbb{R}.

Then, we choose

\widehat{\Omega}=-g_{1}\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad\forall n\geqslant 2,\quad h_{n}=-\frac{g_{n}}{nB_{n}},\qquad B_{n}\triangleq\frac{n-1}{2n}

so that setting

h(\theta)=\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}h_{n}\cos(n\theta),

we find

d_{(\Omega,r)}\mathcal{G}(0,q,\Omega_{0},0)[(\widehat{\Omega},h)]=g.

Moreover, since for any
n\geqslant 2,
we have
B_{n}\in(\tfrac{1}{4},\tfrac{1}{2}),
we immediately get from Cauchy-Schwarz and Bessel inequalities

\|h\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\lesssim\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\frac{|g_{n}|}{n}\lesssim\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\lesssim\|g\|_{C^{s}(\mathbb{T})}.

We can also write

h^{\prime}(\theta)=\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\frac{g_{n}}{B_{n}}\sin(n\theta)=(\widetilde{g}\ast g)(\theta)+2\big[g(\theta)-g_{1}\sin(\theta)\big],

where

\widetilde{g}(\theta)\triangleq-\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\frac{2}{n-1}\cos(n\theta),\qquad(\widetilde{g}\ast g)(\theta)\triangleq 2\int_{\mathbb{T}}\widetilde{g}(\theta-\eta)g(\eta)d\eta.

Notice that
\widetilde{g}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T})\subset L^{1}(\mathbb{T}).
Thus, using that
L^{1}(\mathbb{T})\ast C^{s}(\mathbb{T})\to C^{s}(\mathbb{T})
, we obtain
\widetilde{g}\ast g\in C^{s}(\mathbb{T}).
Hence
h^{\prime}\in C^{s}(\mathbb{T})
. This proves that
h\in\mathbb{X}_{\textnormal{\tiny{even}}}^{1,s}
and therefore that
d_{(\Omega,r)}\mathcal{G}(0,q,\Omega_{0},0):\mathbb{X}_{\textnormal{\tiny{even}}}^{1,s}\to\mathbb{Y}_{\textnormal{\tiny{odd}}}^{s}
is an isomorphism.

(iv)
It follows from the previous points by applying the Implicit Function Theorem.

The proof of Theorem
4.1
is now complete.
∎

5
Applications of Corollary
3.3

In this section, we focus on the application of Corollary
3.3
to specific domains. Since the domains we will consider are convex, the first assumption of this corollary is automatically satisfied. Therefore, we only need to verify the second assumption in Corollary
3.3
for the admissibility of these domains. This condition requires knowledge of the critical point and the conformal mapping.
We have two strategies to check this condition. The first strategy involves providing the conformal mapping that satisfies the required constraint, from which we can derive the geometry. The second strategy involves defining the geometry first and then verifying the constraint. This second approach that we will develop here, which is particularly challenging due to the complex structure of the conformal mapping, even for simple geometries.
We will focus on ellipses, rectangles, and more generally, polygonal domains. All these examples are convex bounded domains making Corollary
3.3
applicable whenever its second assumption holds true, that is,

{\left|\tfrac{F^{(3)}(0)}{F^{\prime}(0)}\right|\not\in\Big\{2\sqrt{1-\tfrac{1}{n^{2}}},\,\,n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}\Big\}}.

(5.1)

with
F:\mathbb{D}\to\mathbf{D}
the conformal mapping such that
F(0)=\xi_{0}
, is the critical point of the Robin function. In terms of the conformal mapping
\Phi=F^{-1}:\mathbf{D}\to\mathbb{D}
, this condition is equivalent to

{\left|\tfrac{\Phi^{(3)}(0)}{(\Phi^{\prime}(0))^{3}}\right|\not\in\Big\{2\sqrt{1-\tfrac{1}{n^{2}}},\,\,n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}\Big\}}.

(5.2)

To check one of these conditions, we need to get access to the conformal mapping structures.

5.1
Elliptic domains

We want to check the validity of the Corollary
3.3
with one of the most simplest domain shapes, namely ellipses. As we have mentioned before, since ellipses are convex, the first assumption of this corollary is automatically satisfied. Thus it remains to check the second assumption reformulated in (
5.1
). Consider
\mathbf{D}=\mathbf{E}_{a}
the domain delimited by the renormalized ellipse with semi-axis
a>1
and
b=1,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{a}\triangleq\Big\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\quad\textnormal{s.t.}\quad\tfrac{x^{2}}{a^{2}}+y^{2}<1\Big\}.

Using the symmetry of this ellipse, we can show that the critical point of Robin function will be given by
\xi_{0}=0.
On the other hand, it is well known
[
72
]
(see also
[
83
, p. 296]
, that the conformal mapping
\Phi:\mathbf{E}_{a}\to\mathbb{D}
is given by

\Phi(z)=\sqrt{k}\,\mathtt{sn}\left(\frac{2K(k)}{\pi}\sin^{-1}\left(\frac{z}{\sqrt{a^{2}-1}}\right);k\right),

(5.3)

where
K:[0,1)\to[\tfrac{\pi}{2},\infty)
is the complete Legendre elliptic integral of first kind defined by

K(x)\triangleq\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\tfrac{d\varphi}{\sqrt{1-x^{2}\sin^{2}(\varphi)}}.

(5.4)

The application
\mathtt{sn}
is the Jacobi elliptic sinus amplitudinus function and
k
is defined through

G(k)=\frac{2}{\pi}\sinh^{-1}\left(\frac{2a}{a^{2}-1}\right),

(5.5)

with
G:(0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
the strictly decreasing function given by

G(x)\triangleq\frac{K\big(\sqrt{1-x^{2}}\big)}{K(x)}\cdot

(5.6)

The previous relation gives a bijective correspondance between
k\in(0,1)
and
a>1
, with

\displaystyle a\to 1\,\,\leftrightarrow\,\,k\to 0\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad a\to\infty\,\,\leftrightarrow\,\,k\to 1.

Our result reads as follows.

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
a>1
and
k=k(a)\in(0,1)
defined through (
5.5
). Introduce the function

\displaystyle g(k)\triangleq\left(1-\frac{1}{4k^{2}}\left(\big(1+k^{2}\big)-\left(\frac{\pi}{2K(k)}\right)^{2}\right)^{2}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

The function
g:[0,1)\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
is continuous, strictly increasing and satisfies

\displaystyle g(0)=1\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad\lim_{k\to 1}g(k)=\infty.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*},
there exists a unique
k_{n}\in[0,1)
(and therefore a unique
a_{n}>1
) such that

\displaystyle g(k_{n})=n.

Finally, for any
a\in(1,\infty)\setminus\{a_{n},\,n\in\mathbb{N}\}
, the non-degeneracy condition (
5.2
) holds.

Figure 3:
Graph of the function
g
.

Proof.

First, we recall the following power series expansions, see for instance
[
1
, p. 81]
,

\displaystyle\sin^{-1}(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(2k-1)!!}{(2k)!!}\frac{z^{2k+1}}{2k+1}=z+\tfrac{1}{6}z^{3}+\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\frac{(2k-1)!!}{(2k)!!}\frac{z^{2k+1}}{2k+1}

and, see
[
90
]
,

\displaystyle\mathtt{sn}(z,k)=z-\tfrac{1}{6}\big(1+k^{2}\big)z^{3}+\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}b_{n}(k)z^{2n+1},\qquad b_{n}(k)\in\mathbb{R}.

Inserting this into (
5.3
), we find

\displaystyle\Phi(z)=a_{1}(k)z+\sum_{n=3}^{\infty}a_{n}(k)z^{n},

and in particular, we have

\displaystyle a_{1}(k)=\frac{2K(k)\sqrt{k}}{\pi\sqrt{a^{2}-1}},\qquad a_{3}(k)=\frac{K(k)\sqrt{k}}{3\pi(a^{2}-1)^{\frac{3}{2}}}\left[1-\frac{4K^{2}(k)}{\pi^{2}}\big(1+k^{2}\big)\right].

Recall that the mapping
x\mapsto K(x)
is continuous increasing on
[0,1)
with

K(0)=\tfrac{\pi}{2},\qquad\lim_{x\to 1}K(x)=\infty.

(5.7)

In particular,

\displaystyle K(k)>\tfrac{\pi}{2}\cdot

Hence,

\displaystyle\frac{|a_{3}(k)|}{a_{1}^{3}(k)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi^{2}}{24kK^{2}(k)}\left(\frac{4K^{2}(k)}{\pi^{2}}\big(1+k^{2}\big)-1\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{6k}\left(\big(1+k^{2}\big)-\left(\frac{\pi}{2K(k)}\right)^{2}\right).

Denote by
M
the arithmetic-geometric mean function defined on
\{(\alpha,\beta)\in(0,\infty)^{2}\}
by

\displaystyle M(\alpha,\beta)\triangleq\lim_{n\to\infty}\alpha_{n}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\beta_{n},

where the sequences
(\alpha_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(\beta_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
are given by

\displaystyle(\alpha_{0},\beta_{0})=(\alpha,\beta)\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad\forall n\in\mathbb{N},\quad(\alpha_{n+1},\beta_{n+1})=\left(\tfrac{\alpha_{n}+\beta_{n}}{2},\sqrt{\alpha_{n}\beta_{n}}\right).

According to
[
82
, p. 66-67]
, we have

K(k)=\frac{\pi}{2M(1,\sqrt{1-k^{2}})}\cdot

(5.8)

Now, we turn to condition (
5.2
) which is equivalent to

\displaystyle\frac{|a_{3}(k)|}{a_{1}^{3}(k)}\neq\frac{1}{3}\sqrt{1-\tfrac{1}{n^{2}}}\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle\quad n\neq\left(1-\frac{1}{4k^{2}}\left(\big(1+k^{2}\big)-\left(\frac{\pi}{2K(k)}\right)^{2}\right)^{2}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\triangleq g(k).

(5.9)

The function
K
admits the following power series expansion, see
[
1
, p. 591]

\displaystyle K(k)=\frac{\pi}{2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{(2n)!}{2^{2n}(n!)^{2}}\right)^{2}k^{2n}.

Consequently, the map
g:[0,1)\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
is continuous and satisfies, in view of (
5.7
),

g(0)=1\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad\lim_{k\to 1}g(k)=\infty.

(5.10)

Now, let us turn to the monotonicity of
g
. The derivative of
g
is

\displaystyle g^{\prime}(k)

\displaystyle=\frac{g^{3}(k)}{2}\left(\big(1+k^{2}\big)-\left(\frac{\pi}{2K(k)}\right)^{2}\right)\left[\frac{\left(k+\frac{\pi^{2}K^{\prime}(k)}{4K^{3}(k)}\right)}{k^{2}}-\frac{\big(1+k^{2}\big)-\left(\frac{\pi}{2K(k)}\right)^{2}}{2k^{3}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{g^{3}(k)}{4k^{3}}\left(\big(1+k^{2}\big)-\left(\frac{\pi}{2K(k)}\right)^{2}\right)\left[k^{2}-1+\left(\frac{\pi}{2K(k)}\right)^{2}\left(1+\frac{2kK^{\prime}(k)}{K(k)}\right)\right].

From (
5.8
), we deduce that

\displaystyle K(k)<\frac{\pi}{2\sqrt{1-k^{2}}},

which implies

(1-k^{2})\left(\frac{2K(k)}{\pi}\right)^{2}<1.

Since
K
is positive and strictly increasing, we have proved that

(1-k^{2})\left(\frac{2K(k)}{\pi}\right)^{2}<1+\frac{2kK^{\prime}(k)}{K(k)}\cdot

(5.11)

Finally, (
5.11
) implies
g^{\prime}(k)>0
, and therefore
g
is strictly increasing on
[0,1)
. Combined with (
5.10
), we obtain that
g:[0,1)\to[1,\infty)
is a bijection. Hence, from (
5.9
) we get the proof of Proposition
5.1
.
∎

5.2
Polygonal domains

The main goal in this section is to explore some polygonal shapes that satisfy the assumption (
5.1
). This will be implemented through the Schwarz-Christoffel mapping. The Schwarz-Christoffel transformation is a powerful tool in complex analysis that describes the conformal mapping of unit disc onto the interior of a polygon. This mapping provides a useful framework for examining the geometric properties and behaviors of various polygonal domains, particularly in the context of the non-degeneracy condition (
5.1
).
We begin this section with the following remark showing that for highly symmetric polygons, the condition (
5.1
) fails. This observation underscores the necessity of exploring less symmetric, more irregular polygonal shapes to satisfy the given assumption.

Remark 5.2
.

Let
m\geqslant 3
be an integer and consider a regular polygon
\mathbf{P}_{m}
with
m
sides, centered at
0
and with length side

\displaystyle\frac{2^{1-\frac{4}{m}}\Gamma^{2}\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{m}\right)}{m\Gamma\left(1-\frac{2}{m}\right)}\cdot

It is well-known, see for instance
\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{N52}{}{}, p. 196]},
that the associated conformal mapping
F:\mathbb{D}\to\mathbf{P}_{m}
is given by

F(z)=\int_{0}^{z}\frac{d\xi}{(1-\xi^{m})^{\frac{2}{m}}}\cdot

(5.12)

From this we deduce that

\displaystyle F(0)=F^{\prime\prime}(0)=F^{(3)}(0)=0,\qquad F^{\prime}(0)=1.

Consequently,
0
is the unique (since
\mathbf{P}_{m}
is convex) critical point of the Robin function
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{P}_{m}}
and

\displaystyle S(F)(0)=0.

Thus, the condition (
5.1
) fails.

The previous remark teaches us that we need to look for less symmetric configurations. This is what we will explore in the next subsections. Before entering into details, let us discuss the general theory of conformal mapping for polygonal domains discovered by Schwarz and Christoffel. We refer the reader to
[
83
, p. 189]
for the theory. The Schwarz-Christoffel mapping provides a way to map the unit disk conformally onto the interior of the polygon
\mathbf{P}
. This transformation is essential for understanding the geometric properties of the polygonal domain.
Consider
\mathbf{P}
a polygon with
m\geqslant 3
vertices labelled
z_{1},

z_{2},
…,
z_{m}.
We denote
\pi\alpha_{1},

\pi\alpha_{2},
…,
\pi\alpha_{m}
as its interior angles, see Figure
4
. The associated exterior angles
\pi\mu_{1},

\pi\mu_{2}
, …,
\pi\mu_{m}
are defined by

\displaystyle\forall k\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket,\quad\mu_{k}\triangleq 1-\alpha_{k}\in(-1,1)

and they satisfy the identity

\sum_{k=1}^{m}\mu_{k}=2.

(5.13)

Then, there exist
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{C},
determining respectively the size and the position of the polygon
\mathbf{P}
, and distinct angles
\theta_{1}
,
\theta_{2}
, … ,
\theta_{m}\in[0,2\pi)
such that the following application maps conformally the unit disc
\mathbb{D}
onto the interior of the polygon
\mathbf{P}

F(z)=\alpha\mathop{\text{\Large$\int_{\text{\normalsize$\scriptstyle\kern-1.63885pt0$}}^{\text{\normalsize$\scriptstyle z$}}$}}\nolimits\prod_{k=1}^{m}\big(\xi-e^{{\rm i}\theta_{k}}\big)^{-\mu_{k}}d\xi+\beta,

(5.14)

with the additional property

\displaystyle\forall k\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket,\quad F\big(e^{{\rm i}\theta_{k}}\big)=z_{k}.

\bullet
e^{{\rm i}\theta_{1}}
\bullet
e^{{\rm i}\theta_{2}}
\bullet
e^{{\rm i}\theta_{3}}
\bullet
e^{{\rm i}\theta_{4}}
\bullet
e^{{\rm i}\theta_{5}}
\mathbb{D}
F
z_{1}
z_{2}
z_{3}
z_{4}
z_{5}
\pi\alpha_{1}
\pi\alpha_{2}
\pi\alpha_{3}
\pi\alpha_{4}
\pi\alpha_{5}
\mathbf{P}
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet

Figure 4:
Schwarz Christoffel conformal mapping for polygonal domains.

Observe that the map in (
5.12
) is nothing but the Schwarz-Christoffel conformal mapping with

\displaystyle\alpha=1,\qquad\beta=0\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad\forall k\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket,\quad\theta_{k}=\tfrac{2k\pi}{m}\cdot

It is readily seen from the expression of
F
that

\frac{F^{\prime\prime}(z)}{F^{\prime}(z)}=-\sum_{k=1}^{m}\frac{\mu_{k}}{z-e^{{\rm i}\theta_{k}}}\cdot

(5.15)

Differentiating (
5.15
), we get

\displaystyle\frac{F^{(3)}(z)}{F^{\prime}(z)}-\left(\frac{F^{\prime\prime}(z)}{F^{\prime}(z)}\right)^{2}=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\frac{\mu_{k}}{(z-e^{{\rm i}\theta_{k}})^{2}}\cdot

Therefore, by vitue of (
2.16
), the Schwarzian derivative of
F
is given by

S(F)(z)=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\frac{\mu_{k}}{(z-e^{{\rm i}\theta_{k}})^{2}}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{m}\frac{\mu_{k}}{z-e^{{\rm i}\theta_{k}}}\right)^{2}.

(5.16)

Notice that the convexity of the polygon
\mathbf{P}
is equivalent to require

\forall k\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket,\quad\mu_{k}>0.

5.2.1
Symmetric convex polygons

Here we will explore the non-degeneracy condition (
5.1
) for symmetric polygons and see a concrete application for rectangles. We consider a polygon
\mathbf{P}
with
2m
vertices (
m\geqslant 2
) denoted
(z_{k})_{1\leqslant k\leqslant 2m}
subject to the following configuration,

1.

(Symmetry with respect to
0
) The vertices satisfy the following properties

\displaystyle\forall k\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket,\quad\,z_{m+k}=-z_{k},\qquad\textnormal{Im}(z_{k})\geqslant 0.

(5.17)

2.

(Convexity) The exterior angles satisfy

\forall k\in\llbracket 1,2m\rrbracket,\quad\mu_{k}>0.

(5.18)

\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
z_{1}
z_{4}=-z_{1}
z_{2}
z_{5}=-z_{2}
z_{3}
z_{6}=-z_{3}
\mathbf{P}
0

Figure 5:
Symmetric convex polygons.

The symmetry property (
5.17
) imposes that

\displaystyle\forall k\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket,\quad\mu_{m+k}=\mu_{k}.

(5.19)

Putting together (
5.19
) and (
5.13
) (with
m
replaced by
2m
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{m}\mu_{k}=1.

(5.20)

Recall from (
5.14
) that the Schwarz-Christoffel mapping satisfying
F(0)=0
and
F^{\prime}(0)>0
takes the form

F(z)=\alpha\mathop{\text{\Large$\int_{\text{\normalsize$\scriptstyle\kern-1.63885pt0$}}^{\text{\normalsize$\scriptstyle z$}}$}}\nolimits\prod_{k=1}^{2m}\big(\xi-e^{{\rm i}\theta_{k}}\big)^{-\mu_{k}}d\xi,\qquad\alpha=\widetilde{\alpha}\prod_{k=0}^{2m-1}\left(-e^{{\rm i}\theta_{k}}\right)^{\mu_{k}},\qquad\widetilde{\alpha}>0.

Due to the symmetry, we can impose to the angles
\theta_{k}
the constraints

\displaystyle 0\leqslant\theta_{1}<\theta_{2}<...<\theta_{m}<\pi\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad\forall k\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket,\quad\theta_{k+m}=\theta_{k}+\pi

(5.21)

leading, with (
5.17
), to

F(z)=\widetilde{\alpha}\mathop{\text{\Large$\int_{\text{\normalsize$\scriptstyle\kern-1.63885pt0$}}^{\text{\normalsize$\scriptstyle z$}}$}}\nolimits\prod_{k=1}^{m}\big(\xi^{2}-e^{2{\rm i}\theta_{k}}\big)^{-\mu_{k}}d\xi.

(5.22)

Next, we will show the following result.

Proposition 5.3
.

Consider
\mathbf{P}
a symmetric convex polygonal domain associated with the Schwarz-Christoffel conformal mapping (
5.22
). Assume that

\sum_{k=1}^{m}{\mu_{k}}\cos(2\theta_{k})\notin\left\{\sqrt{1-\tfrac{1}{n^{2}}},\,n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}\right\}.

(5.23)

Then, the origin
0
is the unique critical point of the Robin function
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{P}}
and the condition (
5.1
) is satisfied.

Proof.

Recall that by construction
F(0)=0
and
F^{\prime}(0)>0.
Moreover, a direct differentiation in (
5.22
) gives

\displaystyle F^{\prime\prime}(0)=0.

(5.24)

It follows that
z=0
is a solution to Grakhov’s equation (
2.14
). Therefore
z=0
is a critical point to the Robin function associated with the domain
\mathbf{P}.
As the domain
\mathbf{P}
is convex, then this critical point is unique. Now, using (
5.16
), (
5.15
), (
5.24
), (
5.19
) and (
5.21
) yield

\displaystyle S(F)(0)=\frac{F^{(3)}(0)}{F^{\prime}(0)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{2m}{\mu_{k}}{e^{-2{\rm i}\theta_{k}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{m}{\mu_{k}}{e^{-2{\rm i}\theta_{k}}}+\sum_{k=1}^{m}{\mu_{m+k}}{e^{-2{\rm i}\theta_{m+k}}}

\displaystyle=2\sum_{k=1}^{m}{\mu_{k}}\cos(2\theta_{k}).

(5.25)

Thus the condition (
5.1
) is equivalent to (
5.23
). This concludes the proof of Proposition
5.3
.
∎

5.2.2
Rectangles

As an application of the previous proposition, we intend to study rectangular domains for which the condition (
5.1
) is explicitly described. In this context, the non-degeneracy condition (
5.1
) will be related to the aspect ratio which should avoid a discrete set of values. Our main result reads as follows.

Proposition 5.4
.

Let
\mathbf{D}
be a rectangle with sides
0<l<L
. Then the condition (
5.1
) is satisfied if and only if

\tfrac{l}{L}\notin\big\{G_{n},\,n\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}\big\},\qquad G_{n}\triangleq G\left(\sqrt{\tfrac{1+\sqrt{1-\frac{1}{n^{2}}}}{2}}\right),

(5.26)

where
G
has been introduced in (
5.6
).

Figure 6:
Graph of the function
G
and the sequence
(G_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we can shall chose the rectangle
\mathbf{D}
to be symmetric with respect to the real and imaginary axis,

\theta_{1}\in(0,\tfrac{\pi}{4}],\qquad\theta_{2}=\pi-\theta_{1},\qquad\theta_{3}=\theta_{1}+\pi,\qquad\theta_{4}=\theta_{2}+\pi.

(5.27)

Moreover,

\mu_{1}=\mu_{2}=\frac{1}{2},

(5.28)

implying in turn

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{2}{\mu_{k}}\cos(2\theta_{k})

\displaystyle=\cos(2\theta_{1}).

Therefore, the condition (
5.23
) becomes

\displaystyle\cos(2\theta_{1})\notin\left\{\sqrt{1-\tfrac{1}{n^{2}}},\,n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}\right\},

which is equivalent to

\displaystyle\cos(\theta_{1})\notin\left\{\sqrt{\tfrac{1+\sqrt{1-\tfrac{1}{n^{2}}}}{2}},\,n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}\right\}.

(5.29)

According to (
5.22
), (
5.27
) and (
5.28
), we have

\displaystyle F(z)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\alpha}\int_{0}^{z}\frac{d\xi}{\sqrt{(\xi^{2}-e^{2{\rm i}\theta_{1}})(\xi^{2}-e^{-2{\rm i}\theta_{1}})}},\qquad\widetilde{\alpha}>0.

The length of the side joining
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
is given by

\displaystyle L=\int_{\theta_{1}}^{\pi-\theta_{1}}|F^{\prime}(e^{{\rm i}t})|dt.

(5.30)

From straightfoward computations we infer

\displaystyle|F^{\prime}(e^{{\rm i}t})|

\displaystyle=\frac{\widetilde{\alpha}}{\sqrt{|e^{2{\rm i}t}-e^{2{\rm i}\theta_{1}}||e^{2{\rm i}t}-e^{-2{\rm i}\theta_{1}}|}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\widetilde{\alpha}}{2\sqrt{|\sin(t-\theta_{1})||\sin(t+\theta_{1})|}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\widetilde{\alpha}}{\sqrt{2}\sqrt{|\cos(2\theta_{1})-\cos(2t)|}}\cdot

Therefore, by change of variables, we get with
\beta=\pi-2\theta_{1}
,

\displaystyle\frac{L}{\widetilde{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\int_{\theta_{1}}^{\pi-\theta_{1}}\frac{dt}{\sqrt{|\cos(2\theta_{1})-\cos(2t)|}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\int_{2\theta_{1}}^{\pi}\frac{dt}{\sqrt{|\cos(2\theta_{1})-\cos(t)|}}+\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\int_{\pi}^{2\pi-2\theta_{1}}\frac{dt}{\sqrt{|\cos(2\theta_{1})-\cos(t)|}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\beta}\frac{dt}{\sqrt{2\cos(t)-2\cos(\beta)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi}{2}{P_{-\frac{1}{2}}}\big(\cos(\beta)\big)=\frac{\pi}{2}{P_{-\frac{1}{2}}}\big(-\cos(2\theta_{1})\big),

where
P_{\nu}
is the Legendre functions of degree
\nu
related to the hypergeometric function
\mathtt{F}
through

\displaystyle P_{\nu}(z)=\mathtt{F}\left(-\nu,\nu+1;1;\tfrac{1-z}{2}\right).

We refer the reader to
[
1
, p. 337 and 561]
for the justification of the previous identities involving special functions. Hence

\displaystyle\frac{L}{\widetilde{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi}{2}\mathtt{F}\left(\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2};1;\tfrac{1+\cos(2\theta_{1})}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi}{2}\mathtt{F}\left(\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2};1;\cos^{2}(\theta_{1})\right)

\displaystyle=K\big(\cos(\theta_{1})\big),

with
K
the complete elliptic function defined in (
5.4
). The last equality comes from
[
1
, p. 591]
. The length
l
of the side
[z_{1},z_{4}]
satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{l}{\widetilde{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\int_{-\theta_{1}}^{\theta_{1}}\frac{dt}{\sqrt{|\cos(2\theta_{1})-\cos(2t)|}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{2\theta_{1}}\frac{dt}{\sqrt{2\cos(t)-2\cos(2\theta_{1})}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi}{2}{P_{-\frac{1}{2}}}\big(\cos(\theta_{1})\big).

Hence

\displaystyle\frac{l}{\widetilde{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi}{2}\mathtt{F}\left(\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2};1;\tfrac{1-\cos(2\theta_{1})}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi}{2}\mathtt{F}\left(\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2};1;\sin^{2}(\theta_{1})\right)

\displaystyle=K\big(\sin(\theta_{1})\big).

Therefore for
\theta_{1}\in(0,\tfrac{\pi}{4}]

\displaystyle\frac{l}{L}

\displaystyle=\frac{K\big(\sin(\theta_{1})\big)}{K\big(\cos(\theta_{1})\big)}=G\big(\cos(\theta_{1})\big),

(5.31)

where
G
has been introduced in (
5.6
). We can easily check that
G
is strictly decreasing. It follows that (
5.29
) is satisfied if and only if

\displaystyle\tfrac{l}{L}\notin\big\{G_{n},n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}\big\},\qquad G_{n}\triangleq G\left(\sqrt{\tfrac{1+\sqrt{1-\frac{1}{n^{2}}}}{2}}\right).

This concludes the proof of Proposition
5.4
.
∎

6
Vortex duplication

The aim of this section is to explore the duplication method achievable through a standard reflection scheme. This mechanism will be employed to construct multi-vortex configurations from a single point vortex, ultimately leading to vortex synchronization.
To illustrate this, we will discuss two cases. The first case involves a rectangular domain divided into several identical rectangular cells, each filled with a time-periodic vortex patch and exhibiting alternating circulations. The second case involves a disc divided into several identical sectors.

6.1
Duplication method

Let
\mathbf{D}
be a bounded simply connected domain such that its boundary
\partial\mathbf{D}
contains a non-trivial segment
[z,w]
with
z\neq w
and

\displaystyle\partial\mathbf{D}\cap(z,w)=[z,w],

(6.1)

where
(z,w)
denotes the infinite line joining the points
z
and
w.
Now, denote by
\mathtt{S}
the reflection mapping through the line
(z,w)
, namely

\displaystyle\forall\,x\in\mathbb{C},\quad\mathtt{S}(x)=2\textnormal{Re}\big\{x(\overline{z-w})\big\}\frac{z-w}{|z-w|}-x.

Define

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}^{\prime}\triangleq\mathtt{S}(\mathbf{D})=\big\{\mathtt{S}(x),\,x\in\mathbf{D}\big\}.

Notice that
\mathbf{D}\cap\mathbf{D}^{\prime}=[z,w].
We set

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}^{\star}\triangleq\textnormal{Int}\big(\overline{\mathbf{D}}\cup\overline{\mathbf{D}^{\prime}}\big).

Let
\omega_{0}\in L^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbf{D})
and
\omega_{0}^{\star}\in L^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbf{D}^{\star})
and consider Euler equations,

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}{\omega}+u\cdot\nabla\omega=0,\quad x\in\mathbf{D},\\
u=\nabla^{\perp}(\Delta_{\mathbf{D}})^{-1}{\omega},\\
\omega(0,x)=\omega_{0}\end{cases}

(6.2)

and

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}{\omega}^{\star}+u^{\star}\cdot\nabla\omega^{\star}=0,\quad x\in\mathbf{D}^{\star},\\
u^{\star}=\nabla^{\perp}(\Delta_{\mathbf{D}^{\star}})^{-1}{\omega}^{\star},\\
\omega^{\star}(0,x)=\omega_{0}^{\star}.\end{cases}

(6.3)

For a function
f:\mathbf{D}\to\mathbb{R}
compactly supported in
\mathbf{D}
, we define its reflection
f^{\#}:\mathbf{D}^{\star}\to\mathbb{R}
as

f^{\#}(x)\triangleq\begin{cases}f(x),&\textnormal{if }x\in\mathbf{D},\\
-f(\mathtt{S}x),&\textnormal{if }x\in\mathbf{D}^{\prime}.\end{cases}

(6.4)

We intend to prove the following result.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
\mathbf{D}
be a simply connected bounded domain satisfying (
6.1
). Given
\omega_{0}\in L^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbf{D})
such that the system (
6.2
) admits a global weak solution
\omega\in L^{\infty}\big(\mathbb{R};L^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbf{D})\big)
. Let
\omega_{0}^{\star}=\omega_{0}^{\#}
, then
the system (
6.3
) admits a weak global solution
\omega^{\star}\in L^{\infty}\big(\mathbb{R};L^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbf{D}^{\star})\big)
, such that,

\forall t\in\mathbb{R},\quad\omega^{\star}(t)=\omega(t)^{\#}.

In particular, if
\omega
is time periodic then
\omega^{\star}
is a time periodic counter-rotating pairs.

Remark 6.2
.

The existence of at least one solution to
(
6.2
)
satisfying
\omega\in L^{\infty}\big(\mathbb{R};L^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbf{D})\big)
, particularly with compact support for each time, can be guaranteed with a piecewise smooth boundary
\partial\mathbf{D}
that has a finite number of corners with angles smaller than
\pi
, see
[
78
]
. Uniqueness can be achieved in slightly smoother domains, as detailed in
\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Agrawal,GVL13,HZ21,HZ22,LZ19}{}{}]}.
It is important to note that in Theorem
3.1
and Corollary
\ref{convex-dom},
we do not impose any regularity conditions on the boundary. In fact, the time-periodic solutions we construct are inherently compactly supported in space due to the construction scheme.

Proof.

Let
\omega
be a compactly supported solution of (
6.2
) whose stream function is
\psi.
Let us show that the function
\omega^{\#}
is a solution to (
6.3
). Define

\displaystyle\forall x\in\mathbf{D}^{\star},\quad\psi^{\#}(t,x)

\displaystyle\triangleq\int_{\mathbf{D}^{\star}}G_{\mathbf{D}^{\star}}(x,y)\omega^{\#}(t,y)dy

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{D}}\big[G_{\mathbf{D}^{\star}}(x,y)-G_{\mathbf{D}^{\star}}(x,\mathtt{S}y)\big]\omega(t,y)dy.

(6.5)

Assume for a while that

\displaystyle\forall x,y\in\mathbf{D},\quad G_{\mathbf{D}^{\star}}(x,y)-G_{\mathbf{D}^{\star}}(x,\mathtt{S}y)=G_{\mathbf{D}}(x,y)

(6.6)

and

\displaystyle\forall x\in\mathbf{D}^{\prime},\quad\forall y\in\mathbf{D},\quad G_{\mathbf{D}^{\star}}(x,y)-G_{\mathbf{D}^{\star}}(x,\mathtt{S}y)=-G_{\mathbf{D}}(\mathtt{S}x,y).

(6.7)

Then, we deduce from (
6.5
) that

\displaystyle\forall x\in\mathbf{D},\quad\psi^{\#}(t,x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{D}}G_{\mathbf{D}}(x,y)\omega(t,y)dy

\displaystyle=\psi(t,x)

and

\displaystyle\forall x\in\mathbf{D}^{\prime},\quad\psi^{\#}(t,x)

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbf{D}}G_{\mathbf{D}}(\mathtt{S}x,y)\omega(t,y)dy

\displaystyle=-\psi(t,\mathtt{S}x).

Therefore, we get

\displaystyle\nabla^{\perp}\psi^{\#}(t,x)\cdot\nabla\omega^{\#}(t,x)=\begin{cases}(\nabla^{\perp}\psi\cdot\nabla\omega)(t,x),\quad x\in\mathbf{D},\\
-(\nabla^{\perp}\psi\cdot\nabla\omega)(t,\mathtt{S}x),\quad x\in\mathbf{D}^{\prime}.\end{cases}

Consequently, we find

\partial_{t}{\omega}^{\#}+\nabla^{\perp}\psi^{\#}\cdot\nabla\omega^{\#}=0,\quad\hbox{in}\quad\mathbf{D}^{\star}

implying that
{\omega}^{\#}
is a solution to (
6.3
). This gives the desired result. It remains to check the identities (
6.6
) and (
6.7
). Before checking these identities, we will first show

\displaystyle\forall\,x,y\in\mathbf{D}^{\star},\quad G_{\mathbf{D}^{\star}}(x,\mathtt{S}y)=G_{\mathbf{D}^{\star}}(\mathtt{S}x,y).

(6.8)

Its proof follows from the fact that

\displaystyle\Delta_{x}[G_{\mathbf{D}^{\star}}(\mathtt{S}x,\mathtt{S}y)]

\displaystyle=[\Delta_{x}G_{\mathbf{D}^{\star}}](\mathtt{S}x,\mathtt{S}y)

\displaystyle=\delta_{\mathtt{S}x}(\mathtt{S}y)

\displaystyle=\delta_{x}(y).

In addition, as
\mathtt{S}\mathbf{D}^{\star}=\mathbf{D}^{\star}
and for
x\in\partial\mathbf{D}^{\star}
we have
\mathtt{S}x\in\partial\mathbf{D}^{\star}
, then

\displaystyle\forall y\in\mathbf{D},\quad\forall\,x\in\partial\mathbf{D},\quad G_{\mathbf{D}^{\star}}(\mathtt{S}x,\mathtt{S}y)=0.

By uniqueness of the Green function, we find that

\displaystyle\forall\,x,y\in\mathbf{D}^{\star},\quad G_{\mathbf{D}^{\star}}(\mathtt{S}x,\mathtt{S}y)=G_{\mathbf{D}^{\star}}(x,y),

which implies (
6.8
) in view of the identity
\mathtt{S}^{2}=\hbox{Id}.

Let’s start with checking the identity (
6.6
). For this aim, we introduce the auxiliary function

\displaystyle\forall\,x,y\in\mathbf{D},\quad G_{1}(x,y)\triangleq G_{\mathbf{D}^{\star}}(x,y)-G_{\mathbf{D}^{\star}}(x,\mathtt{S}y).

(6.9)

First, for
x,y\in\mathbf{D}
, as
\mathtt{S}y\notin{\mathbf{D}}
, we have

\displaystyle\Delta_{x}[G_{1}(x,y)]

\displaystyle=\Delta_{x}G_{\mathbf{D}^{\star}}(x,y)-\Delta_{x}G_{\mathbf{D}^{\star}}(x,\mathtt{S}y)

\displaystyle=\delta_{x}(y)-\delta_{x}(\mathtt{S}y)

\displaystyle=\delta_{x}(y).

One may easily verify that

\displaystyle\partial\mathbf{D}=A\cup[z,w],

where
A\subset\partial\mathbf{D}^{\star}
. As
\mathtt{S}(\mathbf{D}^{\star})=\mathbf{D}^{\star}
, then for
y\in\mathbf{D}
, we obtain from (
6.9
) and (
2.2
)

\displaystyle\forall\,x\in A,\quad G_{1}(x,y)=0-0=0.

However, for
x\in[z,w]
, we have
\mathtt{S}x=x
and thus we find once again from (
6.9
) and (
6.8
)

\displaystyle\forall\,y\in\mathbf{D},\quad G_{1}(x,y)

\displaystyle=G_{\mathbf{D}^{\star}}(x,y)-G_{\mathbf{D}^{\star}}(\mathtt{S}x,y)

\displaystyle=0.

Therefore
G_{1}
satisfies the same elliptic equation as
G_{\mathbf{D}}
. By uniqueness, we find

\displaystyle\forall x,y\in\mathbf{D},\quad G_{1}(x,y)=G_{\mathbf{D}}(x,y).

Together with (
6.8
), it yields the identity (
6.6
). It remains to prove (
6.7
). We write by virtue of (
6.8
)

\displaystyle\forall x\in\mathbf{D}^{\prime},\quad\forall y\in\mathbf{D},\quad G_{\mathbf{D}^{\star}}(x,y)-G_{\mathbf{D}^{\star}}(x,\mathtt{S}y)

\displaystyle=G_{\mathbf{D}^{\star}}(\mathtt{S}^{2}x,y)-G_{\mathbf{D}^{\star}}(\mathtt{S}^{2}x,\mathtt{S}y)

\displaystyle=G_{\mathbf{D}^{\star}}(\mathtt{S}x,\mathtt{S}y)-G_{\mathbf{D}^{\star}}(\mathtt{S}x,y).

(6.10)

On the other hand, as
\mathtt{S}x\in\mathbf{D},
then applying (
6.6
)

\displaystyle G_{\mathbf{D}^{\star}}(\mathtt{S}x,y)-G_{\mathbf{D}^{\star}}(\mathtt{S}x,\mathtt{S}y)=G_{\mathbf{D}}(\mathtt{S}x,y).

(6.11)

Putting together (
6.11
) with (
6.10
) yields

\displaystyle\forall x\in\mathbf{D}^{\prime},\quad\forall y\in\mathbf{D},\quad G_{\mathbf{D}^{\star}}(x,y)-G_{\mathbf{D}^{\star}}(x,\mathtt{S}y)

\displaystyle=-G_{\mathbf{D}}(\mathtt{S}x,y).

This achieves the proof of (
6.7
).

∎

Combining Theorem
3.1
together with Proposition
6.1
allows to generate synchronized pairs of counter-rotating time periodic patches. More precisely, we obtain the following result.

Corollary 6.3
.

Let
\mathbf{D}
be a simply-connected bounded domain such that Theorem
3.1
holds true. Assume the existence of a non-trivial segment
[z,w]
such that

\displaystyle\partial\mathbf{D}\cap(z,w)=[z,w].

Then, counter-rotating time periodic vortex patches exist as solutions to Euler equations (
6.3
) in the domain
\mathbf{D}^{\star},
generated in the spirit of Proposition
6.1
by duplication.

In the remainder of this section, we shall explore how to iterate the duplication process when the boundary of the initial domain
\mathbf{D}
contains more than one segment.
Let
\mathbf{D}
be a domain such that its boundary
\partial\mathbf{D}
contains two non-trivial segments
[z_{1},w_{1}]
and
[z_{2},w_{2}]
and

\displaystyle\forall j\in\{1,2\},\quad\partial\mathbf{D}\cap(z_{j},w_{j})=[z_{j},w_{j}].

Notice that the line
(z_{1},w_{1})
will necessary intersect (at infinity when they are parallel) the line
(z_{2},w_{2})
at a point
I
outside the segment
[z_{1},w_{1}].
We denote by
\theta
the geometric angle
\widehat{z_{1}Iz_{2}}
, which belongs to
[0,\pi).
We denote by
\mathtt{S}_{j}
the reflection with respect to the axis
(z_{j},w_{j})
. Denote

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}_{\star,1}\triangleq\textnormal{Int}\big(\overline{\mathbf{D}}\cup\overline{\mathtt{S}_{1}\mathbf{D}}\big).

Then, for
\theta\in[0,\tfrac{\pi}{2})
we still get that

\displaystyle\forall j\in\{1,2\},\quad\partial\mathbf{D}_{\star,1}\cap(z_{2},w_{2})=[z_{2},w_{2}].

Therefore, we can generate a new duplication by introducing

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}_{\star,2}\triangleq\textnormal{Int}\big(\overline{\mathbf{D}_{\star,1}}\cup\overline{\mathtt{S}_{2}\mathbf{D}_{\star,1}}\big).

The new domain is simply connected. Notice that we have excluded
\theta=\frac{\pi}{2}
because in that case the resulting domain would not be simply connected and would contain a hole. However, we can reach
\theta=\tfrac{\pi}{2}
if we allow
w_{1}=w_{2}
which permits the duplication of rectangles.

\bullet
I

\bullet
\mathbf{D}

w_{2}

z_{2}
\theta
\bullet

\mathbf{D}
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\mathtt{S_{1}}(\mathbf{D})

w_{2}

z_{2}
\bullet
\bullet

\mathbf{D}_{\star,1}

Figure 7:
Duplication iteration : 1st step (
\theta=\tfrac{\pi}{3}
)

6.2
Application to vortex synchronization

Our aim here is to apply the duplication method explained in the Section
6
to study the desingularization of a highly symmetric system of
2^{m}
point vortices. Due to the symmetry, the problem reduces to the evolution a single point vortex within a primitive fundamental cell, in the application it will be a rectangle or an
m
-sector.

6.2.1
Time periodic vortex choreography within rectangular cells

Let us consider a rectangular fundamental domain
\mathbf{R}
with length
L
and width
l.
Assume that

\tfrac{l}{L}\not\in\{G_{n},\,n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}\},

where the sequence
(G_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
has been introduced in (
5.26
). Therefore, Proposition
5.4
applies and by virtue of Corollary
3.3

most
of the point vortex periodic orbits can be desingularized into periodic vortex patch solutions. Given such dynamics, one can apply and iterate the duplication method to construct a simply connected domain formed by
2^{m}
cells (copies of
\mathbf{R}
) within which time periodic multi-vortex patch motion occurs. Therefore one obtains time periodic symmetric choreography of
2^{m}
patches as illustrated in Figure
9
.

Figure 9:
Rectangle cells choreography

6.2.2
Duplication of
m
-sectors: application to the unit disc

Here, we aim to investigate time-periodic multi-vortex motion in sectors, similar to our previous discussion on rectangles.
Let
\mathbf{D}
be a simply connected bounded domain centered in
0
such that there exists
m\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
with

\mathbf{D}=\textnormal{Int}\left(\bigcup_{k=0}^{2m-1}e^{\frac{{\rm i}k\pi}{m}}\overline{\mathbf{D}_{m}}\right),

(6.12)

where
\mathbf{D}_{m}
is the fundamental cell given by the
m
-sector

\mathbf{D}_{m}\triangleq\big\{z\in\mathbf{D}\quad\textnormal{s.t.}\quad 0<\arg(z)<\tfrac{\pi}{m}\big\}.

(6.13)

As examples, we can take discs, ellipses with
m=2
or regular polygons of
2^{m}
sides.

Using the image method, we can establish a connection between the Green functions of the cell
\mathbf{D}_{m}
and the full domain
\mathbf{D}.

The result is as follows, with a proof analogous to the initial part of the Lemma.
6.5
.

Proposition 6.4
.

The Green function of the domain
\mathbf{D}_{m}
defined through (
6.13
) takes the form

\displaystyle G_{\mathbf{D}_{m}}(z,w)=\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\Big[G_{\mathbf{D}}\big(z,\omega_{m}^{k}w\big)-G_{\mathbf{D}}\big(z,\omega_{m}^{k}\overline{w}\big)\Big],\quad\omega_{m}\triangleq e^{\frac{2{\rm i}\pi}{m}}.

Now, we shall focus on the particular case of the unit disc
\mathbb{D}.

Define the sets

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}_{+}\triangleq\big\{z\in\mathbb{D}\quad\textnormal{s.t.}\quad\textnormal{Im}(z)>0\big\},\qquad\mathbb{H}_{+}\triangleq\big\{z\in\mathbb{C}\quad\textnormal{s.t.}\quad\textnormal{Im}(z)>0\big\}

(6.14)

and for
m\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
we define the unit
m
-sector

\mathbb{D}_{m}\triangleq\big\{z\in\mathbb{D}\quad\textnormal{s.t.}\quad 0<\arg(z)<\tfrac{\pi}{m}\big\}.

(6.15)

For the specific case of the disc, the computations are explicit.

Lemma 6.5
.

The Green function of the unit
m
-sector
\mathbb{D}_{m}
defined in (
6.15
) takes the form

G_{\mathbb{D}_{m}}(z,w)=\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\Big[G_{\mathbb{D}}\big(z,\omega_{m}^{k}w\big)-G_{\mathbb{D}}\big(z,\omega_{m}^{k}\overline{w}\big)\Big].

Its associated Robin function writes

\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{D}_{m}}(z)=-\log\left({1-|z|^{2}}\right)-\log\left|\tfrac{z-\overline{z}}{1-z^{2}}\right|+\sum_{k=1}^{m-1}\Big[G_{\mathbb{D}}\big(z,\omega_{m}^{k}z\big)-G_{\mathbb{D}}\big(z,\omega_{m}^{k}\overline{z}\big)\Big]

and admits a unique critical point
\xi_{m}\in\mathbb{D}_{m}
given by

\xi_{m}=t_{m}e^{\frac{{\rm i}\pi}{2m}},\qquad t_{m}\triangleq\left(2m+\sqrt{4m^{2}+1}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2m}}.

(6.16)

\bullet
\xi_{1}
\bullet
\xi_{2}
\bullet
\xi_{4}

Figure 10:
Position of the critical point
\xi_{m}
in the circular sector
D_{m}
for
m\in\{1,2,4\}.

Proof.

By the image method
[
75
]
, finding the Green function in
\mathbb{D}_{m}
amounts to solve in
\mathbb{D}
the equation

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\Delta_{z}G_{\mathbb{D}_{m}}(z,w)=\displaystyle 2\pi\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\delta_{\omega_{m}^{k}w}(z)-2\pi\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\delta_{\omega_{m}^{k}\overline{w}}(z),&\text{ in $\mathbb{D}$},\\
G_{\mathbb{D}_{m}}(z,w)=0,&\text{ for all $z\in\partial\mathbb{D}$}.\end{cases}

(6.17)

By a uniqueness argument, we conclude that

G_{\mathbb{D}_{m}}(z,w)=\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\Big[G_{\mathbb{D}}\big(z,\omega_{m}^{k}w\big)-G_{\mathbb{D}}\big(z,\omega_{m}^{k}\overline{w}\big)\Big].

By isolating the term
k=0
, we can write

\displaystyle G_{\mathbb{D}_{m}}(z,w)=\log\left|\tfrac{z-w}{1-z\overline{w}}\right|-\log\left|\tfrac{z-\overline{w}}{1-zw}\right|+\sum_{k=1}^{m-1}\Big[G_{\mathbb{D}}\big(z,\omega_{m}^{k}w\big)-G_{\mathbb{D}}\big(z,\omega_{m}^{k}\overline{w}\big)\Big].

Thus Robin Function takes the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{D}_{m}}(z)=-\log\left({1-|z|^{2}}\right)-\log\left|\tfrac{z-\overline{z}}{1-z^{2}}\right|+\sum_{k=1}^{m-1}\Big[G_{\mathbb{D}}\big(z,\omega_{m}^{k}z\big)-G_{\mathbb{D}}\big(z,\omega_{m}^{k}\overline{z}\big)\Big].

Straightforward computations yield for any
k\in\llbracket 1,m-1\rrbracket,

\displaystyle\partial_{z}\Big(G_{\mathbb{D}}\big(z,\omega_{m}^{k}z\big)\Big)=\frac{1}{2z}+\overline{z}\,\hbox{Re}\left(\tfrac{\omega_{m}^{k}}{1-\omega_{m}^{k}|z|^{2}}\right)

and for any
k\in\llbracket 0,m-1\rrbracket,

\displaystyle\partial_{z}\Big(G_{\mathbb{D}}\big(z,\omega_{m}^{k}\overline{z}\big)\Big)=\tfrac{1}{z-\omega_{m}^{k}\overline{z}}+\tfrac{\omega_{m}^{-k}{z}}{1-\omega_{m}^{-k}z^{2}}\cdot

Hence

\displaystyle\partial_{z}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{D}_{m}}(z)=\frac{m-1}{2z}+\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\overline{z}\,\hbox{Re}\left(\tfrac{\omega_{m}^{k}}{1-\omega_{m}^{k}|z|^{2}}\right)+\tfrac{1}{\omega_{m}^{k}\overline{z}-z}+\tfrac{\omega_{m}^{-k}{z}}{\omega_{m}^{-k}z^{2}-1}\cdot

The critical point
\xi_{m}\in\mathbb{D}_{m}
is unique since
\mathbb{D}_{m}
is convex and it solves the equation

\frac{m-1}{2\xi_{m}}+\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\overline{\xi_{m}}\,\hbox{Re}\left(\tfrac{\omega_{m}^{k}}{1-\omega_{m}^{k}|\xi_{m}|^{2}}\right)+\tfrac{1}{\omega_{m}^{k}\overline{\xi_{m}}-\xi_{m}}+\tfrac{\omega_{m}^{-k}{\xi_{m}}}{\omega_{m}^{-k}\xi_{m}^{2}-1}=0.

(6.18)

By symmetry, we expect

\xi_{m}=t_{m}\,\omega_{4m},\qquad t_{m}\in(0,1).

(6.19)

Notice that

\omega_{4m}=e^{\frac{{\rm i}\pi}{2m}},\qquad\omega_{4m}^{4}=\omega_{m},\qquad\omega_{4m}^{4m}=1.

(6.20)

Inserting (
6.19
) into (
6.18
) and using (
6.20
) yields

\displaystyle\frac{m-1}{2t_{m}}+\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}t_{m}\,\hbox{Re}\left(\tfrac{\omega_{4m}^{4k}}{1-\omega_{4m}^{4k}t_{m}^{2}}\right)+\tfrac{1}{t_{m}(\omega_{4m}^{4k-2}-1)}+\tfrac{\omega_{4m}^{2-4k}{t_{m}}}{\omega_{4m}^{2-4k}t_{m}^{2}-1}=0.

In addition, the third identity in (
6.20
) implies

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\hbox{Re}\left(\tfrac{\omega_{4m}^{4k}}{1-\omega_{4m}^{4k}t_{m}^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\tfrac{\omega_{4m}^{4k}}{1-\omega_{4m}^{4k}t_{m}^{2}}+\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\tfrac{\omega_{4m}^{-4k}}{1-\omega_{4m}^{-4k}t_{m}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\tfrac{\omega_{4m}^{4k}}{1-\omega_{4m}^{4k}t_{m}^{2}}

and

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\tfrac{\omega_{4m}^{2-4k}{t}}{\omega_{4m}^{2-4k}t_{m}^{2}-1}=\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\tfrac{\omega_{4m}^{2+4k}t_{m}}{\omega_{4m}^{2+4k}t_{m}^{2}-1}\cdot

Therefore,

\displaystyle\left(\frac{m-1}{2}+\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\tfrac{1}{\omega_{4m}^{4k-2}-1}\right)+\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\tfrac{\omega_{4m}^{4k}t_{m}^{2}}{1-\omega_{4m}^{4k}t_{m}^{2}}+\tfrac{\omega_{4m}^{2+4k}t_{m}^{2}}{\omega_{4m}^{2+4k}t_{m}^{2}-1}=0,

which is equivalent to

\displaystyle\left(\frac{m-1}{2}+\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\tfrac{1}{\omega_{4m}^{4k-2}-1}\right)+\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\tfrac{1}{1-\omega_{4m}^{4k}t_{m}^{2}}+\tfrac{1}{\omega_{4m}^{4k+2}t_{m}^{2}-1}=0.

Writing

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{1-\omega_{4m}^{4k}t_{m}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\omega_{4m}^{4kj}t_{m}^{2j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\sum_{j=\ell m}^{(\ell+1)m-1}\omega_{4m}^{4kj}t_{m}^{2j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}t_{m}^{2\ell m}\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}\omega_{4m}^{4kj}t_{m}^{2j}

\displaystyle=\frac{P_{k}(t_{m})}{1-t_{m}^{2m}},

with

\displaystyle P_{k}(x)\triangleq\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}\omega_{4m}^{4kj}x^{2j}.

Then

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{\omega_{4m}^{4k+2}t_{m}^{2}-1}=\tfrac{P_{k}(\omega_{4m}t_{m})}{\omega_{4m}^{2m}t_{m}^{2m}-1}=-\tfrac{P_{k}(\omega_{4m}t_{m})}{t_{m}^{2m}+1}\cdot

In addition, notice that from (
6.20
), we have

\displaystyle P_{k}\left(\omega_{4m}^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}\left(\omega_{4m}^{4k-2}\right)^{j}

\displaystyle=\frac{1-\omega_{4m}^{(4k-2)m}}{1-\omega_{4m}^{4k-2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{1-\omega_{4m}^{4k-2}}\cdot

Thus

\displaystyle\left(\tfrac{m-1}{2}-\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}P_{k}\left(\omega_{4m}^{-1}\right)\right)-\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\tfrac{P_{k}(t_{m})}{t^{2m}-1}+\tfrac{P_{k}(\omega_{4m}t_{m})}{t^{2m}+1}=0.

By exchanging finite sums, we can check that for any
x\in\mathbb{C}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}{P_{k}(x)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}\omega_{4m}^{4kj}x^{2j}

\displaystyle=m+\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}x^{2j}\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\omega_{4m}^{4kj}

\displaystyle=m,

since from (
6.20
) we have for any
j\in\llbracket 1,m-1\rrbracket,

\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\omega_{4m}^{4kj}=\frac{1-\omega_{4m}^{4jm}}{1-\omega_{4m}^{4j}}=0.

It follows that

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}+\tfrac{m}{t_{m}^{2m}-1}+\tfrac{m}{t_{m}^{2m}+1}=0.

Thus,
t_{m}
is solution to the equation

\displaystyle t_{m}^{4m}+4mt_{m}^{2m}-1=0.

Hence, under the constraint
t_{m}\in(0,1)
, we find

t_{m}=\left(2m+\sqrt{4m^{2}+1}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2m}}.

This achieves the proof of Lemma
6.5
.
∎

Now, we prove the following result.

Proposition 6.6
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and consider
\Phi_{m}:\mathbb{D}_{m}\to\mathbb{D}
the unique Riemann mapping such that

\Phi_{m}(\xi_{m})=0\qquad\Phi_{m}^{\prime}(\xi_{m})>0,

where
\xi_{m}\in\mathbb{D}_{m}
is the unique critical point of the Robin function
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{D}_{m}}
given in (
6.16
). Then, the
condition (
3.19
)
is satisfied, with
F=\Phi^{-1}_{m}
, and Corollary
3.3
holds true. As a consequence, for any
w\in\mathbb{D}_{m}
,
the initial configuration

\omega_{0}=\displaystyle\pi\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\delta_{w\omega_{m}^{k}}(z)-\pi\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\delta_{\overline{w}\omega_{m}^{k}}(z)

generates time-periodic solution to the point vortex system, which can be desingularized with time periodic vortex patches.

Proof.

It is known that the following mappings are conformal

\phi_{+}:\begin{array}[t]{rcl}\mathbb{D}_{+}&\to&\mathbb{H}_{+},\\
z&\mapsto&-\tfrac{1}{2}(z+\tfrac{1}{z})\end{array}\qquad\phi_{0}:\begin{array}[t]{rcl}\mathbb{H}_{+}&\to&\mathbb{D}\\
z&\mapsto&\tfrac{z-{\rm i}}{z+{\rm i}},\end{array}

where we refer to (
6.14
) for the definition of
\mathbb{D}_{+}
and
\mathbb{H}_{+}.

Therefore
\phi\triangleq\phi_{0}\circ\phi_{+}:\mathbb{D}_{+}\to\mathbb{D}
is conformal. One can check that

\phi(z)=\tfrac{z^{2}+2iz+1}{z^{2}-2iz+1}\cdot

On the other hand, the mapping
z\in\mathbb{D}_{m}\mapsto z^{m}\in\mathbb{D}_{+}
is conformal. Therefore the mapping

\phi_{m}:\begin{array}[t]{rcl}\mathbb{D}_{m}&\to&\mathbb{D}\\
z&\mapsto&\phi(z^{m})=\tfrac{z^{2m}+2iz^{m}+1}{z^{2m}-2iz^{m}+1}\end{array}

is conformal. We want to construct the unique conformal mapping
\Phi_{m}:\mathbb{D}_{m}\mapsto\mathbb{D}
such that

\Phi_{m}(\xi_{m})=0,\qquad\Phi_{m}^{\prime}(\xi_{m})>0,

where
\xi_{m}
is the critical point defined in (
6.16
). Now, set

a_{m}\triangleq\phi_{m}(\xi_{m})=\tfrac{t_{m}^{2m}+2t_{m}^{m}-1}{t_{m}^{2m}-2t_{m}^{m}-1}\in(-1,1)

and denote by
T_{m}:\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{D}
the conformal map (Moebius transform)

T_{m}(z)=e^{i\theta_{m}}\frac{z-a_{m}}{1-a_{m}\,z}

for some
\theta_{m}\in\mathbb{R}
that will be fixed later.
Then set

\Phi_{m}\triangleq T_{m}\circ\phi_{m}.

From direct computations we show that

\Phi_{m}(\xi_{m})=0

and, from (
6.16
),

\phi_{m}^{\prime}(\xi_{m})=\alpha_{m}e^{i(\pi-\frac{\pi}{2m})},\qquad\alpha_{m}\triangleq\frac{4mt_{m}^{m-1}(1+t_{m}^{2m})}{(1+2t_{m}^{m}-t_{m}^{2m})^{2}}>0.

Therefore

\Phi^{\prime}_{m}(\xi_{m})=\frac{\alpha_{m}}{1-a_{m}^{2}}e^{i(\theta+\pi-\frac{\pi}{2m})}.

By taking
\theta_{m}\triangleq\frac{\pi}{2m}-\pi
, we infer that

\Phi^{\prime}_{m}(\xi_{m})=\frac{\alpha_{m}}{1-a_{m}^{2}}>0.

By Faà di Bruno formula, we have that

\Phi_{m}^{\prime}=\phi_{m}^{\prime}\cdot T_{m}^{\prime}\circ\phi_{m},\qquad\Phi_{m}^{(3)}=(\phi_{m}^{\prime})^{3}\cdot T_{m}^{(3)}\circ\phi_{m}+3\phi_{m}^{\prime}\phi_{m}^{\prime\prime}\cdot T_{m}^{\prime\prime}\circ\phi_{m}+\phi_{m}^{(3)}\cdot T^{\prime}_{m}\circ\phi_{m}.

Helped by Mathematica, we find that

\left|\tfrac{F^{(3)}(0)}{F^{\prime}(0)}\right|^{2}=\left|\tfrac{\Phi_{m}^{(3)}(\xi_{m})}{\big(\Phi_{m}^{\prime}(\xi_{m})\big)^{3}}\right|^{2}=\mathtt{A}_{m}\sqrt{4m^{2}+1}+\mathtt{B}_{m},

where

\mathtt{A}_{m}\triangleq\frac{-8}{m^{8}}\big(2m^{6}+9m^{4}+6m^{2}+1\big)\in\mathbb{Q}^{*},\qquad\mathtt{B}_{m}\triangleq\frac{4}{m^{8}}\big(m^{8}+24m^{6}+38m^{4}+8m^{2}+2\big)\in\mathbb{Q}.

Notice that for any integer
m\geqslant 1
,

\sqrt{4m^{2}+1}\not\in\mathbb{Q}.

Hence,

\forall n\in\mathbb{N}^{*},\quad\mathtt{A}_{m}\sqrt{4m^{2}+1}+\mathtt{B}_{m}\neq 4\left(1-\tfrac{1}{n^{2}}\right).

This proves the condition (
3.19
), which concludes the proof of Proposition
6.6
.
∎

7
Proof of Theorem
3.1

The goal of this section is to prove Theorem
3.1
by employing a modified Nash-Moser scheme tin conjunction with KAM tools. Specifically, we will utilize the framework developed in
[
59
]
to address the leapfrogging phenomenon in the context of Euler equations involving two symmetric pairs of vortices. Our approach begins with an introduction to the functional setting, followed by a detailed presentation of the asymptotic structure of the linearized operator. Subsequently, we will construct a suitable approximate solution to the nonlinear equation (
3.14
). Next, we rescale the functional
\mathbf{G}
, defined via (
3.14
), and transform the new linear operator into a Fourier multiplier, with a regularizing remainder. This transformation enables us to identify an approximate right inverse of the linearized operator, which is a critical component of the Nash-Moser scheme.

7.1
Functional setting

We denote by
\mathbb{T}^{2}
the two-dimensional torus and any
h:\mathbb{T}^{2}\to\mathbb{C}
in the class
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2},\mathbb{C})
admits the Fourier expansion,

\displaystyle h=\sum_{(l,j)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}h_{l,j}\,\mathbf{e}_{l,j},\qquad\mathbf{e}_{l,j}(\varphi,\theta)\triangleq e^{{\rm i}(l\varphi+j\theta)},\qquad h_{l,j}\triangleq\big\langle h,\mathbf{e}_{l,j}\big\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2},\mathbb{C})}.

The Hilbert
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2},\mathbb{C})
is equipped with the scalar product

\displaystyle\big\langle h_{1},h_{2}\big\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2},\mathbb{C})}\triangleq\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}h_{1}(\varphi,\theta)\overline{h_{2}(\varphi,\theta)}d\varphi d\theta=\sum_{(l,j)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}h_{1,l,j}\overline{h_{2,l,j}}.

The following notation is of constant use in this work

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}}f(x)dx\triangleq\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}f(x)dx.

(7.1)

The Sobolev spaces of regularity
s\in\mathbb{R}
is given by

\displaystyle H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2},\mathbb{C})\triangleq\Big\{h\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2},\mathbb{C})\quad\textnormal{s.t.}\quad\|h\|_{H^{s}}<\infty\Big\},

where

\displaystyle\|h\|_{H^{s}}^{2}\triangleq\sum_{(l,j)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\langle l,j\rangle^{2s}|h_{l,j}|^{2},\qquad\langle l,j\rangle^{2}\triangleq 1+|l|^{2}+|j|^{2}.

The subspace with zero space average functions is denoted

\displaystyle H^{s}_{0}(\mathbb{T}^{2},\mathbb{C})\triangleq\left\{h\in H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2},\mathbb{C})\textnormal{\quad}\hbox{s.t.}\quad\forall\varphi\in\mathbb{T},\,\int_{\mathbb{T}}h(\varphi,\theta)d\theta=0\right\}.

Given a nonempty subset
\mathscr{O}
of
\mathbb{R}
and
\gamma\in(0,1]
, we define the Banach spaces

\displaystyle\textnormal{Lip}_{\gamma}(\mathscr{O},H^{s})

\displaystyle\triangleq\Big\{h:\mathscr{O}\rightarrow H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2},\mathbb{C})\quad\textnormal{s.t.}\quad\|h\|_{s}^{{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}}<\infty\Big\},

\displaystyle\textnormal{Lip}_{\gamma}(\mathscr{O},\mathbb{C})

\displaystyle\triangleq\Big\{h:\mathscr{O}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\quad\textnormal{s.t.}\quad\|h\|^{{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}}<\infty\Big\},

with

\displaystyle\|h\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}

\displaystyle\triangleq\sup_{\lambda\in{\mathscr{O}}}\|h(\lambda,\cdot)\|_{H^{s}}+\gamma\sup_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\in\mathscr{O}^{2}\atop\lambda_{1}\neq\lambda_{2}}\frac{\|h(\lambda_{1},\cdot)-h(\lambda_{2},\cdot)\|_{H^{s-1}}}{|\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}|},

\displaystyle\|h\|^{{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\sup_{\lambda\in{\mathscr{O}}}|h(\lambda)|+\gamma\sup_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\in\mathscr{O}^{2}\atop\lambda_{1}\neq\lambda_{2}}\frac{|h(\lambda_{1})-h(\lambda_{2})|}{|\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}|}\cdot

We emphasize that in Section
7.6
related to Nash-Moser scheme we find it convenient to use the notation

\displaystyle\|h\|_{s,\mathscr{O}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}=\|h\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}.

(7.2)

In the sequel, we consider the list of numbers with the constraints

\gamma\in(0,1],\qquad\tau>1,\qquad S\geqslant s\geqslant s_{0}>3.

(7.3)

Along this section, we will extensively use the following classical result related to the product law over weighted Sobolev spaces. Given
(\gamma,s_{0},s)
satisfying (
7.3
).
Let
h_{1},h_{2}\in\textnormal{Lip}_{\gamma}(\mathscr{O},H^{s}).
Then
h_{1}h_{2}\in\textnormal{Lip}_{\gamma}(\mathscr{O},H^{s})
and

\|h_{1}h_{2}\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\lesssim\|h_{1}\|_{s_{0}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\|h_{2}\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}+\|h_{1}\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\|h_{2}\|_{s_{0}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}.

(7.4)

We need some anisotropic spaces to describe the spatial smoothing effects of some non-local operators. The anisotropic Sobolev spaces of regularity
s_{1},s_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
is given by

\displaystyle H^{s_{1},s_{2}}(\mathbb{T}^{2},\mathbb{C})\triangleq\Big\{h\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2},\mathbb{C})\quad\textnormal{s.t.}\quad\|h\|_{H^{s_{1},s_{2}}}<\infty\Big\},

where

\displaystyle\|h\|_{H^{s_{1},s_{2}}}^{2}\triangleq\sum_{(l,j)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\langle l,j\rangle^{2s_{1}}\langle j\rangle^{2s_{2}}|h_{l,j}|^{2}.

The corresponding Lipschitz norm for external parameters

\displaystyle\|h\|_{s_{1},s_{2}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\triangleq\sup_{\lambda\in{\mathscr{O}}}\|h(\gamma,\cdot)\|_{H^{s_{1},s_{2}}}+\gamma\sup_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\in\mathscr{O}^{2}\atop\lambda_{1}\neq\lambda_{2}}\frac{\|h(\lambda_{1},\cdot)-h(\lambda_{2},\cdot)\|_{H^{s_{1}-1,s_{2}}}}{|\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}|}\cdot

To conclude this section, we will briefly recall the Hilbert transform on
\mathbb{T}
.
Let
h:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{R}
be a continuous function with a zero average, we define its Hilbert transform by

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}h(\theta)\triangleq\int_{\mathbb{T}}h(\eta)\cot\left(\tfrac{\eta-\theta}{2}\right)d\eta=-\partial_{\theta}\int_{\mathbb{T}}h(\eta)\log\left(\sin^{2}\left(\tfrac{\theta-\eta}{2}\right)\right)d\eta.

(7.5)

The integrals are understood in the principal value sense. It is a classical that
\mathbf{H}
is a Fourier multiplier with

\forall j\in\mathbb{Z}^{*},\quad\mathbf{H}\mathbf{e}_{j}(\theta)={\rm i}\,\hbox{sign}(j)\mathbf{e}_{j}(\theta).

7.2
Asymptotic structure of the linearized operator

Our aim here is to analyze the asymptotic behavior in
\varepsilon
of the differential
d_{r}\mathbf{G}(r)
at a small state
r
for the functional
\mathbf{G}
introduced in (
3.14
). The main result is stated in Proposition
7.2
below. As a preliminary step and in view of (
3.14
)-(
3.13
), we need to perform the
\varepsilon
-expansion, up to order 2, of the kernel

K(p+\varepsilon z,p+\varepsilon\xi)

using its representation via the conformal mapping (
2.5
).

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
V
be an open set such that
\overline{V}\subset\mathbf{D}
. Denote also
\mathbb{D}_{2}
the disc of radius
2
centered in the origin. There exist
\varepsilon_{0}>0
and
K_{3}\in C^{\infty}_{b}(V\times\mathbb{D}_{2}^{2})
such that for any
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
and
(p,z,\xi)\in V\times\mathbb{D}_{2}^{2}
, we have

K(p+\varepsilon z,p+\varepsilon\xi)=\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{D}}(p)+\varepsilon K_{1}(p,z,\xi)+\varepsilon^{2}K_{2}(p,z,\xi)+\varepsilon^{3}K_{3}(p,z,\xi),

(7.6)

with

\displaystyle K_{1}(p,z,\xi)

\displaystyle\triangleq\textnormal{Re}\Big\{\partial_{z}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{D}}(p)\,(z+\xi)\Big\},

\displaystyle K_{2}(p,z,\xi)

\displaystyle\triangleq\textnormal{Re}\Big\{\Big(\tfrac{1}{6}S(\Phi)(p)+\tfrac{1}{2}\big(\partial_{z}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{D}}(p)\big)^{2}\Big)(z^{2}+\xi^{2})+\tfrac{1}{6}S(\Phi)(p)z\xi+\tfrac{1}{r_{\mathbf{D}}^{2}(p)}z\overline{\xi}\Big\},

where we have used the notations (
2.8
), (
2.11
) and (
2.16
).

Proof.

Throughout the proof, we shall make use of the following formulae

\displaystyle\partial_{\varepsilon}\big(\log|1+g(\varepsilon)|\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textnormal{Re}\left\{\tfrac{\partial_{\varepsilon}g(\varepsilon)}{1+g(\varepsilon)}\right\},

(7.7)

\displaystyle\partial_{\varepsilon}^{2}\big(\log|1+g(\varepsilon)|\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textnormal{Re}\left\{\tfrac{(1+g(\varepsilon))\partial_{\varepsilon}^{2}g(\varepsilon)-(\partial_{\varepsilon}g(\varepsilon))^{2}}{(1+g(\varepsilon))^{2}}\right\}.

(7.8)

In general, we can write

\displaystyle\Phi(z)=bz+f(z),\qquad b>0,\qquad f\textnormal{ analytic in }\mathbf{D}.

As
\Phi
is univalent, then we infer that for any compact subset
\mathbf{K}\subset\mathbf{D},

\max_{\zeta\in\mathbf{K}}|f^{\prime}(\zeta)|<b.

Therefore, applying (
2.5
) we deduce that

\displaystyle K(z,w)

\displaystyle=-\log|1-\Phi(z)\overline{\Phi(w)}|+\log(b)+\log\left|1+\tfrac{1}{b}\tfrac{f(z)-f(w)}{z-w}\right|

\displaystyle\triangleq-\log|1+g_{1}(z,w)|+\log(b)+\log|1+g_{2}(z,w)|.

(7.9)

It follows that

K(p+\varepsilon z,p+\varepsilon\xi)=-\log|1+g_{1}(p+\varepsilon z,p+\varepsilon\xi)|+\log(b)+\log|1+g_{2}(p+\varepsilon z,p+\varepsilon\xi)|.

(7.10)

Define

\displaystyle g_{1}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\triangleq g_{1}(p+\varepsilon z,p+\varepsilon\xi)

\displaystyle=-\Phi\big(p+\varepsilon z\big)\overline{\Phi\big(p+\varepsilon\xi\big)}.

Then straightforward calculations based on Taylor expansion of the holomorphic function
\Phi
yield for small
\varepsilon

\displaystyle g_{1}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle{=}-|\Phi(p)|^{2}-\varepsilon\big(\Phi(p)\overline{\Phi^{\prime}(p)}\,\overline{\xi}+\overline{\Phi(p)}\Phi^{\prime}(p)z\big)

\displaystyle\quad-\tfrac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\Big(\overline{\Phi(p)}\Phi^{\prime\prime}(p)z^{2}+\Phi(p)\overline{\Phi^{\prime\prime}(p)}\,\overline{\xi}^{2}+2|\Phi^{\prime}(p)|^{2}z\overline{\xi}\Big)+O(\varepsilon^{3}).

This implies

\displaystyle g_{1}(0)

\displaystyle=-|\Phi(p)|^{2},

\displaystyle\partial_{\varepsilon}g_{1}(0)

\displaystyle=-\big(\Phi(p)\overline{\Phi^{\prime}(p)}\overline{\xi}+\overline{\Phi(p)}\Phi^{\prime}(p)z\big),

\displaystyle\partial_{\varepsilon}^{2}g_{1}(0)

\displaystyle=-\Big(\overline{\Phi(p)}\Phi^{\prime\prime}(p)z^{2}+\Phi(p)\overline{\Phi^{\prime\prime}(p)}\overline{\xi}^{2}+2|\Phi^{\prime}(p)|^{2}z\overline{\xi}\Big).

Combining these identities with (
7.7
) and (
7.8
) allow to get for small
\varepsilon

\displaystyle\log|1+g_{1}(\varepsilon)|

\displaystyle{\displaystyle=}\log\big(1-|\Phi(p)|^{2}\big)-\varepsilon\textnormal{Re}\left\{\tfrac{\Phi(p)\overline{\Phi^{\prime}(p)}\overline{\xi}+\overline{\Phi(p)}\Phi^{\prime}(p)z}{1-|\Phi(p)|^{2}}\right\}

(7.11)

\displaystyle-\tfrac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\textnormal{Re}\left\{\tfrac{\overline{\Phi(p)}\Phi^{\prime\prime}(p)z^{2}+\Phi(p)\overline{\Phi^{\prime\prime}(p)}\overline{\xi}^{2}+2|\Phi^{\prime}(p)|^{2}z\overline{\xi}}{1-|\Phi(p)|^{2}}\right\}

\displaystyle-\tfrac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\textnormal{Re}\left\{\left(\tfrac{\Phi(p)\overline{\Phi^{\prime}(p)}\overline{\xi}+\overline{\Phi(p)}\Phi^{\prime}(p)z}{1-|\Phi(p)|^{2}}\right)^{2}\right\}+O(\varepsilon^{3}).

Notice that
O(\varepsilon^{3})
is a smooth function
On the other hand, one writes

\displaystyle g_{2}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\triangleq\tfrac{1}{b}\tfrac{f(p+\varepsilon z)-f(p+\varepsilon\xi)}{\varepsilon(z-\xi)}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{b}\int_{0}^{1}f^{\prime}\big(p+\varepsilon\xi+s\varepsilon(z-\xi)\big)ds.

Therefore, for any
n\in\mathbb{N},

\partial_{\varepsilon}^{n}g_{2}(\varepsilon)=\frac{1}{b}\int_{0}^{1}f^{(n+1)}\big(p+\varepsilon\xi+s\varepsilon(z-\xi)\big)\big(\xi+s(z-\xi)\big)^{n}ds,

implying in turn that

\displaystyle\partial_{\varepsilon}^{n}g_{2}(0)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{f^{(n+1)}(p)}{b(n+1)}\tfrac{z^{n+1}-\xi^{n+1}}{z-\xi}=\tfrac{f^{(n+1)}(p)}{b(n+1)}\sum_{k=0}^{n}z^{k}\xi^{n-k}.

(7.12)

Putting together (
7.7
), (
7.8
) and (
7.12
), we deduce for small
\varepsilon

\displaystyle\log|1+g_{2}(\varepsilon)|

\displaystyle{=}\log\left|1+\tfrac{f^{\prime}(p)}{b}\right|+\varepsilon\textnormal{Re}\left\{\tfrac{f^{\prime\prime}(p)(z+\xi)}{2(b+f^{\prime}(p))}\right\}

\displaystyle\quad+\tfrac{\varepsilon^{2}}{6}\textnormal{Re}\left\{\tfrac{f^{(3)}(p)}{b+f^{\prime}(p)}\big(z^{2}+\xi^{2}+z\xi\big)\right\}

\displaystyle\quad-\tfrac{\varepsilon^{2}}{8}\textnormal{Re}\left\{\left(\tfrac{f^{\prime\prime}(p)(z+\xi)}{b+f^{\prime}(p)}\right)^{2}\right\}+O(\varepsilon^{3}).

Since

\Phi^{\prime}(z)=b+f^{\prime}(z),

\Phi^{\prime\prime}(z)=f^{\prime\prime}(z)
and
\Phi^{(3)}(z)=f^{(3)}(z)
,
then
we end up with

\displaystyle\log|1+g_{2}(\varepsilon)|

\displaystyle=-\log(b)+\log\left|\Phi^{\prime}(p)\right|+\varepsilon\textnormal{Re}\left\{\tfrac{\Phi^{\prime\prime}(p)(z+\xi)}{2\Phi^{\prime}(p)}\right\}

(7.13)

\displaystyle+\tfrac{\varepsilon^{2}}{6}\textnormal{Re}\left\{\tfrac{\Phi^{(3)}(p)}{\Phi^{\prime}(p)}\big(z^{2}+\xi^{2}+z\xi\big)\right\}

\displaystyle-\tfrac{\varepsilon^{2}}{8}\textnormal{Re}\left\{\left(\tfrac{\Phi^{\prime\prime}(p)(z+\xi)}{\Phi^{\prime}(p)}\right)^{2}\right\}+O(\varepsilon^{3}).

We observe that
O(\varepsilon^{3})=\varepsilon^{3}K_{3}(p,z,\xi)
with
K_{3}
being a smooth function in
(p,z,\xi)\in V\times\mathbb{D}_{2}^{2}
. Inserting (
7.11
) and (
7.13
) into (
7.10
) and using (
2.8
), (
2.11
) and (
2.16
) give the desired result.
∎

The next target is to provide an asymptotic expansion in
\varepsilon
of the linearized operator
\mathbf{G}
, defined in (
3.14
), at a small state
r
. In what follows, and throughout the remainder of this paper, we denote

\mathscr{O}\triangleq[\lambda_{*},\lambda^{*}],

where
\lambda_{*}
and
\lambda^{*}
are as defined in Theorem
3.1
.

Proposition 7.2
.

There exists
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,1)
such that if
r
is smooth with zero average in space and

\varepsilon\leqslant\varepsilon_{0}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\|r\|_{s_{0}+2}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\leqslant 1,

then, the linearized operator of the map
\mathbf{G}
, at a small state
r
in the direction
h\in L^{2}_{0}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R})
is given by

\displaystyle d_{r}\mathbf{G}(r)[h]

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{3}\omega(\lambda)\partial_{\varphi}h+\varepsilon\partial_{\theta}\big[\mathbf{V}^{\varepsilon}(r)h\big]-\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}\mathbf{H}[h]+\varepsilon^{3}\partial_{\theta}\mathbf{Q}_{1}[h]+\varepsilon^{3}\partial_{\theta}\mathcal{R}_{1}^{\varepsilon}[h]+\varepsilon^{4}\partial_{\theta}\mathcal{R}_{2}^{\varepsilon}[h],

with the following properties.

1.

The function
\mathbf{V}^{\varepsilon}(r)
decomposes as follows

\mathbf{V}^{\varepsilon}(r)\triangleq\tfrac{1}{2}-\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}r+\varepsilon^{2}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}\mathtt{g}+V_{1}^{\varepsilon}(r)\big)+\varepsilon^{3}V_{2}^{\varepsilon}(r),

with

\displaystyle\mathtt{g}(\varphi,\theta)

\displaystyle\triangleq\textnormal{Re}\left\{\mathtt{w}_{2}\big(p(\varphi)\big)e^{2{\rm i}\theta}\right\},\qquad\mathtt{w}_{2}(p)\triangleq\big(\partial_{z}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{D}}(p)\big)^{2}+\tfrac{1}{3}S(\Phi)(p)

(7.14)

and
V_{1}^{\varepsilon}(r),

V_{2}^{\varepsilon}(r)
satisfy the estimates

\displaystyle\|V_{1}^{\varepsilon}(r)\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|r\|_{s+1}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\|r\|_{s_{0}+1}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)},

\displaystyle\|V_{2}^{\varepsilon}(r)\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1+\|r\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)},

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{12}V_{1}^{\varepsilon}(r)\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\Delta_{12}r\|_{s+1}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}+\|\Delta_{12}r\|_{s_{0}+1}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\max_{k\in\{1,2\}}\|r_{k}^{\varepsilon}(r)\|_{s+1}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)},

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{12}V_{2}^{\varepsilon}(r)\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\Delta_{12}r\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}+\|\Delta_{12}r\|_{s_{0}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\max_{k\in\{1,2\}}\|r_{k}^{\varepsilon}(r)\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}.

2.

The operator
\mathbf{H}
is the Hilbert transform on the torus and the other non-local terms are integral operators in the form

\displaystyle\mathbf{Q}_{1}[h](\varphi,\theta)

\displaystyle\triangleq\int_{\mathbb{T}}h(\eta)\mathtt{Q}\big(p(\varphi),\theta,\eta\big)d\eta,

(7.15)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{j}^{\varepsilon}[h](\varphi,\theta)

\displaystyle\triangleq\int_{\mathbb{T}}h(\eta)\mathtt{K}_{j}^{\varepsilon}(r)(\varphi,\theta,\eta)d\eta,\quad j\in\{1,2\},

(7.16)

with, see (
2.8
) and (
2.16
) for the definitions of the functions below,

\displaystyle\mathtt{Q}(p,\theta,\eta)

\displaystyle\triangleq\frac{\cos(\theta-\eta)}{r_{\mathbf{D}}^{2}(p)}\cos(\theta-\eta)+\frac{1}{6}\textnormal{Re}\left\{e^{{\rm i}(\theta+\eta)}S(\Phi)(p)\right\}

(7.17)

and
\mathtt{K}_{1}^{\varepsilon}(r)
,
\mathtt{K}_{2}^{\varepsilon}(r)
satisfy the following estimates

\displaystyle\|\mathtt{K}_{1}^{\varepsilon}(r)\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|r\|_{s+1}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\|r\|_{s_{0}+1}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)},

(7.18)

\displaystyle\|\mathtt{K}_{2}^{\varepsilon}(r)\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1+\|r\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}.

(7.19)

Proof.

Throughout the proof, we may disregard the dependence on the variable
\varphi
, specifying it only when it is relevant.
Differentiating (
3.14
) with respect to
r
in the direction
h
gives

\displaystyle d_{r}\mathbf{G}(r)[h](\theta)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{3}\omega(\lambda)\,\partial_{\varphi}h(\theta)-\tfrac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\partial_{\theta}\textnormal{Re}\Big\{\partial_{{z}}\mathcal{R}_{\bf{D}}(p)e^{{\rm i}\theta}\Big\}\tfrac{h(\theta)}{R(\theta)}

(7.20)

\displaystyle+\partial_{\theta}\bigg[d_{r}\Big(\Psi_{1}\big(r,z(\theta)\big)\Big)[h](\theta)+d_{r}\Big(\Psi_{2}\big(r,z(\theta)\big)\Big)[h](\theta)\bigg].

The linearized of
\Psi_{1}
has been computed in
[
59
, Prop. 3.1]
and is given by

\displaystyle\partial_{\theta}d_{r}\Big(\Psi_{1}\big(r,z(\theta)\big)\Big)[h](\theta)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\partial_{\theta}\Big[\big(\tfrac{1}{2}-\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}r(\theta)+\varepsilon^{2}V_{1}^{\varepsilon}(r)(\theta)\big)h(\theta)\Big]-\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}\mathbf{H}[h](\theta)+\varepsilon^{3}\partial_{\theta}\mathcal{R}_{1}^{\varepsilon}[h](\theta),

where
V_{1}^{\varepsilon}
satisfies the desired estimates and
\mathcal{R}_{1}^{\varepsilon}
is an integral operator with the description (
7.16
) and (
7.18
).
Linearizing (
3.13
), one readily finds

\displaystyle d_{r}\Big(\Psi_{2}\big(r,z(\theta)\big)\Big)[h](\theta)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\int_{\mathbb{T}}h(\eta)K\big(\varepsilon z(\theta)+p,\varepsilon z(\eta)+p\big)d\eta

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathbb{T}}\int_{0}^{R(\eta)}d_{r}\Big(K\big(\varepsilon z(\theta)+p,\varepsilon le^{{\rm i}\eta}+p\big)\Big)[h](\theta)ldld\eta

\displaystyle\triangleq J_{1}[h](\theta)+J_{2}[h](\theta).

(7.21)

We shall start with expanding the nonlocal term
J_{1}[h]
using the structure of the kernel (
7.6
),

\displaystyle\partial_{\theta}J_{1}[h](\theta)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\partial_{\theta}\sum_{j=1}^{3}\varepsilon^{j}\int_{\mathbb{T}}h(\eta)K_{j}\big(p,z(\theta),z(\eta)\big)d\eta.

We have used that the test function
h
is of zero spatial average.
From
K_{1}
, detailed in Lemma
7.1
, we obtain

\displaystyle\partial_{\theta}\int_{\mathbb{T}}h(\eta)K_{1}\big(p,z(\theta),z(\eta)\big)d\eta

\displaystyle=\partial_{\theta}\textnormal{Re}\left\{\partial_{z}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{D}}(p)\int_{\mathbb{T}}h(\eta)\big(z(\theta)+z(\eta)\big)d\eta\right\}.

Using one time more that
h
has a zero spatial average, we deduce that

\displaystyle\partial_{\theta}\int_{\mathbb{T}}h(\eta)K_{1}\big(p,z(\theta),z(\eta)\big)d\eta=0.

Regarding the second term, in view of Lemma
7.1
and using once again that
h
has a zero average, we obtain

\displaystyle\partial_{\theta}\int_{\mathbb{T}}h(\eta)K_{2}\big(p,z(\theta),z(\eta)\big)d\eta

\displaystyle=\partial_{\theta}\textnormal{Re}\left\{\tfrac{1}{6}S(\Phi)(p)z(\theta)\int_{\mathbb{T}}h(\eta)z(\eta)d\eta\right\}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{r_{\mathbf{D}}^{2}(p)}\partial_{\theta}\textnormal{Re}\left\{z(\theta)\int_{\mathbb{T}}h(\eta)\overline{z(\eta)}d\eta\right\}.

Applying the parametrization (
3.12
) we infer

\displaystyle\partial_{\theta}\int_{\mathbb{T}}h(\eta)K_{2}\big(p,z(\theta),z(\eta)\big)d\eta

\displaystyle=\partial_{\theta}\textnormal{Re}\left\{\tfrac{1}{6}S(\Phi)(p)\int_{\mathbb{T}}h(\eta)e^{{\rm i}(\theta+\eta)}d\eta\right\}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{r_{\mathbf{D}}^{2}(p)}\partial_{\theta}\textnormal{Re}\left\{\int_{\mathbb{T}}h(\eta)e^{{\rm i}(\theta-\eta)}d\eta\right\}+\varepsilon\partial_{\theta}\mathcal{R}_{1,2}^{\varepsilon}[h],

with
\mathcal{R}_{1,2}^{\varepsilon}
a kernel operator, whose kernel satisfies the estimate (
7.19
). Gathering the preceding identities gives

\displaystyle\partial_{\theta}J_{1}[h]=\varepsilon^{3}\partial_{\theta}\mathbf{Q}_{1}[h]+\varepsilon^{4}\partial_{\theta}\mathcal{R}_{2}^{\varepsilon}[h],

where
\mathbf{Q}_{1}
is a nonlocal operator localizing on the modes
\pm 1
and defined by

\mathbf{Q}_{1}[h](\theta)\triangleq\int_{\mathbb{T}}h(\eta)\mathtt{Q}(p,\theta,\eta)d\eta,

(7.22)

where the kernel takes the form,

\displaystyle\mathtt{Q}(p,\theta,\eta)

\displaystyle\triangleq\frac{\cos(\theta-\eta)}{r_{\mathbf{D}}^{2}(p)}+\frac{1}{6}\textnormal{Re}\left\{e^{{\rm i}(\theta+\eta)}S(\Phi)(p)\right\}.

The remainder operator
\mathcal{R}_{2}^{\varepsilon}
has the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{2}^{\varepsilon}[h]\triangleq\mathcal{R}_{1,2}^{\varepsilon}[h]+\int_{\mathbb{T}}h(\eta)K_{3}\big(p,z(\theta),z(\eta)\big)d\eta.

From Lemma
7.1
, we infer that
K_{3}\in C^{\infty}_{b}(V\times\mathbb{D}_{2}^{2})
