. Therefore
\mathcal{R}_{2}^{\varepsilon}
is an integral operator whose kernel obeys to the estimate of (
7.19
).

Next, let us move to the estimate of the local term in (
7.21
). Performing Taylor formula with the integral contribution
\int_{0}^{R}..
allows to get

\displaystyle J_{2}[h](\theta)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\int_{0}^{1}d_{r}\Big(K\big(\varepsilon z(\theta)+p,\varepsilon le^{{\rm i}\eta}+p\big)\Big)[h](\theta)ldld\eta

(7.23)

\displaystyle+\varepsilon\int_{\mathbb{T}}\int_{0}^{1}\tfrac{r(\eta)}{{\sqrt{1+2\varepsilon\tau r(\eta)}}}d_{r}\Big(K\big(\tau\varepsilon z(\theta)+p,\tau\varepsilon e^{{\rm i}\eta}+p\big)\Big)[h](\theta)d\eta d\tau,

where
\mathbf{L}_{\varepsilon}[r]
is a non-local term with smooth kernel (depending on
K
) at least quadratic in
r.

By using the decomposition (
7.6
), we obtain

\displaystyle d_{r}\Big(K\big(\varepsilon z(\theta)+p,\varepsilon le^{{\rm i}\eta}+p\big)\Big)[h](\theta)=\varepsilon^{2}\tfrac{h(\theta)}{R(\theta)}\textnormal{Re}\left\{\partial_{z}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{D}}(p)e^{{\rm i}\theta}\right\}

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon^{3}h(\theta)\textnormal{Re}\left\{\mathtt{w}_{2}(p)e^{2{\rm i}\theta}\right\}+\varepsilon^{3}\tfrac{h(\theta)l}{6R(\theta)}\textnormal{Re}\left\{S(\Phi)(p)e^{{\rm i}(\theta+\eta)}\right\}+\varepsilon^{3}\tfrac{h(\theta)l}{r_{\mathbf{D}}^{2}(p)R(\theta)}\textnormal{Re}\left\{e^{{\rm i}(\theta-\eta)}\right\}

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon^{4}\tfrac{h(\theta)}{R(\theta)}\textnormal{Re}\Big\{\partial_{z}K_{3}(p,z(\theta),le^{{\rm i}\eta})\,e^{{\rm i}\theta}\Big\},

(7.24)

with
\mathtt{w}_{2}
as in (
7.14
). Hence, the zero space average condition for
r
implies

\displaystyle\varepsilon\int_{\mathbb{T}}\int_{0}^{1}\tfrac{r(\eta)}{{\sqrt{1+2\varepsilon\tau r(\eta)}}}d_{r}\Big(K\big(\tau\varepsilon z(\theta)+p,\tau\varepsilon e^{{\rm i}\eta}+p\big)\Big)[h](\theta)d\eta d\tau=\varepsilon^{4}V_{2,1}(r)h(\theta)

and we can check in a straightforward manner that
V_{2,1}^{\varepsilon}
satisfies the estimates of
V_{2}^{\varepsilon}
in Proposition
7.2
. Coming back to (
7.23
) and using (
7.24
) we find

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}}\int_{0}^{1}d_{r}\Big(K\big(\varepsilon z(\theta)+p(\varphi),\varepsilon le^{{\rm i}\eta}+p(\varphi)\big)\Big)[h](\theta)ldld\eta

\displaystyle{=}\tfrac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\tfrac{h(\theta)}{R(\theta)}\textnormal{Re}\left\{\partial_{z}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{D}}(p)e^{{\rm i}\theta}\right\}+\tfrac{\varepsilon^{3}}{2}\textnormal{Re}\left\{\mathtt{w}_{2}\big(p(\varphi)\big)e^{2{\rm i}\theta}\right\}h(\theta)+\varepsilon^{4}V_{2,2}(r)h(\theta).

Notice that
V_{2,2}(r)
satisfies the same estimates of
V_{2}^{\varepsilon}
in Proposition
7.2
.
Putting the preceding identities in (
7.20
) yields to the asymptotic expansion of the linearized operator as described in Proposition
7.2
.
∎

7.3
Construction of an approximate solution

In the following lemma, we provide the leading term in the asymptotic expansion of
\mathbf{G}(0)
as described by (
3.14
). This result will be pertinent later when developing an appropriate scheme to obtain a precise approximation, as discussed in Lemma
7.5
.

Lemma 7.3
.

There exists
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,1)
such that for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
,
one has

\displaystyle\forall(\varphi,\theta)\in\mathbb{T}^{2},\quad\mathbf{G}(0)(\varphi,\theta)

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\textnormal{Im}\left\{\mathtt{w}_{2}\big(p(\varphi)\big)e^{2{\rm i}\theta}\right\}+O(\varepsilon^{3}),

where
\mathtt{w}_{2}
is defined in (
7.14
).
In addition, the function
\mathbf{G}(0)
does not contain the spatial modes
\pm 1
, that is,

\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathbf{G}(0)(\varphi,\theta)e^{\pm{\rm i}\theta}d\theta=0.

Proof.

To simplify the notation, we shall remove the dependence in
\varphi
. Substituting
r=0
into (
3.14
) gives

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}(0)(\theta)

\displaystyle=\partial_{\theta}\Big[-\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}\textnormal{Re}\Big\{\partial_{{z}}\mathcal{R}_{\bf{D}}(p)e^{{\rm i}\theta}\Big\}+\Psi_{1}\big(0,e^{{\rm i}\theta}\big)+\Psi_{2}\big(0,e^{{\rm i}\theta}\big)\Big].

In view of (
3.13
), we have

\partial_{\theta}\big[\Psi_{1}\big(0,e^{{\rm i}\theta}\big)\big]=\partial_{\theta}\bigg[\int_{\mathbb{T}}\int_{0}^{1}\log\big(\big|1-le^{{\rm i}(\eta-\theta)}\big|\big)ldld\eta\bigg]=0.

As for the
\Psi_{2}
term, one gets by virtue of (
3.13
)

\Psi_{2}\big(0,e^{{\rm i}\theta}\big)=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\int_{0}^{1}K\big(p+\varepsilon e^{{\rm i}\theta},p+\varepsilon le^{{\rm i}\eta}\big)ldld\eta.

We apply Lemma
7.1
with
z=e^{{\rm i}\theta}
and
\xi=le^{{\rm i}\eta}
leading in view of (
7.14
) to

\displaystyle\partial_{\theta}\Big(\Psi_{2}\big(0,e^{{\rm i}\theta}\big)\Big)

\displaystyle{=}\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}\partial_{\theta}\textnormal{Re}\left\{\partial_{{z}}\mathcal{R}_{\bf{D}}(p)e^{{\rm i}\theta}\right\}+\tfrac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\textnormal{Re}\left\{{\rm i}e^{2{\rm i}\theta}\mathtt{w}_{2}(p)\right\}+O(\varepsilon^{3})

\displaystyle{=}\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}\partial_{\theta}\textnormal{Re}\left\{\partial_{{z}}\mathcal{R}_{\bf{D}}(p)e^{{\rm i}\theta}\right\}-\tfrac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\textnormal{Im}\left\{e^{2{\rm i}\theta}\mathtt{w}_{2}(p)\right\}+O(\varepsilon^{3}).

Combining the foregoing identities gives the suitable expansion for
\mathbf{G}(0)
. The final goal is to prove the absence of modes
\pm 1
in the whole quantity
\mathbf{G}(0).
This pertains to show that the Fourier expansion of
\Psi_{2}\big(0,e^{{\rm i}\theta}\big)-\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}\textnormal{Re}\left\{\partial_{{z}}\mathcal{R}_{\bf{D}}(p)e^{{\rm i}\theta}\right\}
does not contain the modes
\pm 1.

Applying (
7.9
) we infer

\displaystyle K(p+\varepsilon z,p+\varepsilon\xi)=\textnormal{Re}\left\{H(p+\varepsilon z,{p+\varepsilon\xi})\right\},

where

\displaystyle H(z_{1},z_{2})\triangleq\log(b)+\textnormal{Log}\left(1+\frac{1}{b}\frac{f(z_{1})-f(z_{2})}{z_{1}-z_{2}}\right)-\textnormal{Log}\big(1-\Phi(z_{2})\overline{\Phi(z_{1})}\big)

and
Log
is the principal value of the complex logarithm function.
Applying this with
z=e^{{\rm i}\theta}
and
\xi=le^{{\rm i}\eta}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\Psi_{2}\big(0,e^{{\rm i}\theta}\big)=\textnormal{Re}\left\{\int_{\mathbb{T}}\int_{0}^{1}H\big(p+\varepsilon e^{{\rm i}\theta},p+\varepsilon le^{{\rm i}\eta}\big)ldld\eta\right\}.

Noting that the function
\xi\in\mathbb{D}_{2}\mapsto H(p+\varepsilon e^{{\rm i}\theta},p+\varepsilon\xi)
is holomorphic for small
\varepsilon
allows to get the power series expansion

\displaystyle H\big(p+\varepsilon e^{{\rm i}\theta},p+\varepsilon\xi\big)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}c_{n}(\varepsilon,p,\theta)\xi^{n}.

Therefore

\displaystyle\Psi_{2}\big(0,e^{{\rm i}\theta}\big)

\displaystyle=\textnormal{Re}\left\{\int_{\mathbb{T}}\int_{0}^{1}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}c_{n}(\varepsilon,p,\theta)e^{{\rm i}n\eta}l^{n+1}dld\eta\right\}

\displaystyle=\textnormal{Re}\left\{\tfrac{1}{2}c_{0}(\varepsilon,p,\theta)\right\}.

It follows that

\displaystyle\Psi_{2}\big(0,e^{{\rm i}\theta}\big)

\displaystyle=\textnormal{Re}\left\{\tfrac{1}{2}H\big(p+\varepsilon e^{{\rm i}\theta},p\big)\right\}

\displaystyle=\textnormal{Re}\left\{\tfrac{1}{2}H_{1}\big(p+\varepsilon e^{{\rm i}\theta},p\big)\right\},

with

\displaystyle H_{1}(z_{1},z_{2})\triangleq\log(b)+\textnormal{Log}\left(1+\frac{1}{b}\frac{f(z_{1})-f(z_{2})}{z_{1}-z_{2}}\right)-\textnormal{Log}\big(1-\Phi(z_{1})\overline{\Phi(z_{2})}\big).

As the function
\xi\in\mathbb{D}_{2}\mapsto H_{1}(p+\varepsilon\xi,p)
is holomorphic for small
\varepsilon,
then

\displaystyle H_{1}(p+\varepsilon e^{{\rm i}\theta},p)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\tfrac{\partial_{z_{1}}^{n}H_{1}(p,p)}{n!}\varepsilon^{n}e^{{\rm i}n\theta},

which implies that

\displaystyle\Psi_{2}\big(0,e^{{\rm i}\theta}\big)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}H_{1}(p,p)+\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}\textnormal{Re}\left\{{\partial_{z_{1}}H_{1}(p,p)}e^{{\rm i}\theta}\right\}+\tfrac{1}{2}\textnormal{Re}\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\tfrac{\partial_{z_{1}}^{n}H_{1}(p,p)}{n!}\varepsilon^{n}e^{{\rm i}n\theta}.

One can check that

\displaystyle H_{1}(p,p)=\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{D}}(p)\quad\hbox{and}\quad\partial_{z_{1}}H_{1}(p,p)=\partial_{z}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{D}}(p).

Consequently,

\displaystyle\Psi_{2}\big(0,e^{{\rm i}\theta}\big)-\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}\textnormal{Re}\left\{\partial_{{z}}\mathcal{R}_{\bf{D}}(p)e^{{\rm i}\theta}\right\}=\tfrac{1}{2}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{D}}(p)+\tfrac{1}{2}\textnormal{Re}\sum_{n\geqslant 2}^{\infty}\tfrac{\partial_{z_{1}}^{n}H_{1}(p,p)}{n!}\varepsilon^{n}e^{{\rm i}n\theta}.

This shows that the Fourier expansion of
\Psi_{2}\big(0,e^{{\rm i}\theta}\big)-\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}\textnormal{Re}\left\{\partial_{{z}}\mathcal{R}_{\bf{D}}(p)e^{{\rm i}\theta}\right\}
does not contain the modes
\pm 1.
This ends the proof of the lemma.

∎

We have already seen in Lemma
7.3
that the function
\mathbf{G}(0)
does not contain the modes
\pm 1.
Furthermore, Proposition
7.2
ensures that
\mathtt{g}
is localized on the mode
2
. Leveraging these insights and adopting a similar approach as developed in
[
59
, Lemma 3.3, Proposition 3.4]
we derive a suitable approximate solution
r_{\varepsilon}
. Upon rescaling the functional, the behavior of the linearized operator is quite similar to the original one. Our result reads as follows.

Lemma 7.4
.

There exists
r_{\varepsilon}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
, satisfying

\displaystyle\|r_{\varepsilon}+\mathtt{g}\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\lesssim\varepsilon,

such that the nonlinear functional

\mathbf{F}(\rho)\triangleq\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon^{2+\mu}}\mathbf{G}(\varepsilon r_{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon^{1+\mu}\rho),\qquad\mu\in(0,1),

(7.25)

satisfies the following estimates

\|\mathbf{F}(0)\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\lesssim\varepsilon^{3-\mu}.

Proof.

The starting point is to write a Taylor expansion of the functional
\mathbf{G}
around
0
,

\mathbf{G}(r)=\mathbf{G}(0)+\partial_{r}\mathbf{G}(0)[r]+\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{r}^{2}\mathbf{G}(0)[r,r]+\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}(1-\tau)^{2}\partial_{r}^{3}\mathbf{G}(\tau r)[r,r,r]d\tau.

Then, we look for an approximate solution in the form
r=\varepsilon r_{0}+\varepsilon^{2}r_{1}.
Hence,

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}(\varepsilon r_{0}+\varepsilon^{2}r_{1})=\mathbf{G}(0)+\varepsilon\partial_{r}\mathbf{G}(0)[r_{0}]+\tfrac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\partial_{r}^{2}\mathbf{G}(0)[r_{0},r_{0}]+\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{r}\mathbf{G}(0)[r_{1}]

(7.26)

\displaystyle+\varepsilon^{3}\partial_{r}^{2}\mathbf{G}(0)[r_{0},r_{1}]+\tfrac{\varepsilon^{4}}{2}\partial_{r}^{2}\mathbf{G}(0)[r_{1},r_{1}]+\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}(1-\tau)^{2}\partial_{r}^{3}\mathbf{G}(\tau r)[r,r,r]d\tau.

In view of Proposition
7.2
one has

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}(0)+\varepsilon\partial_{r}\mathbf{G}(0)[r_{0}]+\tfrac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\partial_{r}^{2}\mathbf{G}(0)[r_{0},r_{0}]=\mathbf{G}(0)+\varepsilon^{4}\omega(\lambda)\partial_{\varphi}r_{0}

(7.27)

\displaystyle+\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{\theta}\Big[\big(\tfrac{1}{2}+\tfrac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\mathtt{g}+\varepsilon^{3}{V}_{2}^{\varepsilon}(0)\big)r_{0}\Big]-\tfrac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\mathbf{H}[h]+\varepsilon^{4}\partial_{\theta}\mathbf{Q}_{1}[r_{0}]-\tfrac{\varepsilon^{4}}{4}\partial_{\theta}\big(r_{0}^{2}\big)+\varepsilon^{5}\partial_{\theta}\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon}(r_{0}),

with

\|\partial_{\theta}\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon}(r_{0})\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\lesssim\|r_{0}\|_{s+2}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}.

At the main order we shall solve the equation

\tfrac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\big[\partial_{\theta}-\mathbf{H}\big]r_{0}+\mathbf{G}(0)=0.

According to Lemma
7.3
, the source term is of size
O(\varepsilon^{2})
and does not contain the modes
0
and
\pm 1
. Therefore, we can solve the previous equation

\displaystyle r_{0}(\varphi,\theta)

\displaystyle=-2\varepsilon^{-2}\big[\partial_{\theta}-\mathbf{H}\big]^{-1}\mathbf{G}(0)

\displaystyle={-}\textnormal{Re}\left\{\mathtt{w}_{2}\big(p(\varphi)\big)e^{2{\rm i}\theta}\right\}+\varepsilon\underline{r}_{0}(\varphi,\theta)

\displaystyle={-}\mathtt{g}(\varphi,\theta)+\varepsilon\underline{r}_{0}(\varphi,\theta),

(7.28)

where
\mathtt{w}_{2}
and
\mathtt{g}
are defined in (
7.14
) and
\underline{r}_{0}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
. Moreover,
\underline{r}_{0}
does not contain the modes
0
and
\pm 1
which implies according to (
7.15
) and (
7.17
) that

\partial_{\theta}\mathbf{Q}_{1}[r_{0}]=0.

Gathering the identities above, we conclude that

\mathbf{G}(0)+\varepsilon\partial_{r}\mathbf{G}(0)[r_{0}]+\tfrac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\partial_{r}^{2}\mathbf{G}(0)[r_{0},r_{0}]=\varepsilon^{4}\mathbf{B}_{1}+\varepsilon^{5}\mathbf{B}_{2},

(7.29)

where
\mathbf{B}_{1}
is given by

\mathbf{B}_{1}\triangleq{-}\omega(\lambda)\partial_{\varphi}\mathtt{g}-\tfrac{3}{4}\partial_{\theta}\big(\mathtt{g}^{2}\big)

and
\mathbf{B}_{2}
satisfies

\|\mathbf{B}_{2}\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\lesssim 1.

Thus,
\mathbf{B}_{1}
contains only even modes and the equation

\tfrac{1}{2}\big[\partial_{\theta}-\mathbf{H}\big]r_{1}+\varepsilon\mathbf{B}_{1}=0

(7.30)

admits a solution that satisfies the estimates

\|r_{1}\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\lesssim\varepsilon.

Since
r_{1}
does not contain the modes
\pm 1
then

\partial_{\theta}\mathbf{Q}_{1}[r_{1}]=0.

Therefore, by (
7.28
)

\displaystyle r_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\triangleq r_{0}+\varepsilon r_{1}

\displaystyle=-\mathtt{g}+\varepsilon\big(\underline{r}_{0}+r_{1}\big)

(7.31)

and inserting (
7.27
), (
7.30
) into (
7.26
) we obtain

\|\mathbf{G}(\varepsilon r_{\varepsilon})\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\lesssim\varepsilon^{5}.

This concludes the proof of Lemma
7.4
. ∎

The next objective is to describe the asymptotic structure of the linearized operator associated with the rescaled function
F
introduced in (
7.25
).

Lemma 7.5
.

There exists
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,1)
such that for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
and any smooth function
\rho
with zero space average and satisfying

\|\rho\|_{s_{0}+2}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\leqslant 1,

the following holds true.
The linearized operator
\mathcal{L}_{0}\triangleq d_{\rho}\mathbf{F}(\rho)
, see (
7.25
), has the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0}h

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{2}\omega(\lambda)\partial_{\varphi}h+\partial_{\theta}\big[\mathbf{V}_{1}^{\varepsilon}(\rho)h\big]-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{H}[h]+{\varepsilon^{2}}\partial_{\theta}\mathbf{Q}_{1}[h]+\varepsilon^{3}\partial_{\theta}\mathcal{R}_{0}^{\varepsilon}(\rho)[h],

(7.32)

where the function
\mathbf{V}_{1}^{\varepsilon}(\rho)
is given by

\displaystyle\mathbf{V}_{1}^{\varepsilon}(\rho)

\displaystyle\triangleq\tfrac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon^{2}}\mathtt{g}-\tfrac{\varepsilon^{2+\mu}}{2}\rho+\varepsilon^{3}{V}^{\varepsilon}(\rho).

(7.33)

The function
\mathtt{g}
is defined in Proposition
7.2
and
{V}^{\varepsilon}(\rho)
enjoys the estimates: for any
s\geqslant s_{0}

\displaystyle\|{V}^{\varepsilon}(\rho)\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1+\varepsilon^{\mu}\|\rho\|_{s+1}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)},

(7.34)

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{12}{V}^{\varepsilon}(\rho)\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon^{\mu}\|\Delta_{12}\rho\|_{s+1}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}+\varepsilon^{\mu}\|\Delta_{12}\rho\|_{s_{0}+1}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\max_{\ell\in\{1,2\}}\|\rho_{\ell}\|_{s+1}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}.

The operator
\mathbf{Q}_{1}
is given in Proposition
7.2
the operator
\mathcal{R}_{0}^{\varepsilon}(\rho)
is an integral operator of the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{0}^{\varepsilon}(\rho)[h](\varphi,\theta)

\displaystyle\triangleq\int_{\mathbb{T}}h(\varphi,\eta)\mathcal{K}_{0}^{\varepsilon}(\rho)(\varphi,\theta,\eta)d\eta,

where the kernel
\mathcal{K}_{0}^{\varepsilon}(\rho)
satisfies: for any
s\geqslant s_{0}

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{K}_{0}^{\varepsilon}(\rho)\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1+\varepsilon^{\mu}\|\rho\|_{s+1}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}.

(7.35)

Moreover, for any
s\geqslant s_{0}
, one has

\displaystyle\big\|d_{\rho}\mathbf{F}(\rho)[h]\big\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|h\|_{s+2}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}+\|\rho\|_{s+2}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\|h\|_{s_{0}+2}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)},

(7.36)

\displaystyle\big\|d_{\rho}^{2}\mathbf{F}(\rho)[h,h]\big\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}\|h\|_{s+2}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\|h\|_{s_{0}+2}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}+\varepsilon^{2}\|\rho\|_{s+2}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\big(\|h\|_{s_{0}+2}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\big)^{2}.

Proof.

Linearizing the functional
\mathbf{F}
defined by (
7.25
) an using Proposition
7.2
we obtain

\displaystyle\partial_{\rho}\mathbf{F}(\rho)[h]

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}(\partial_{r}\mathbf{G})(\varepsilon r_{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon^{1+\mu}\rho)[h]

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{2}\omega_{0}\partial_{\varphi}h+\partial_{\theta}\Big[\Big(\tfrac{1}{2}-\tfrac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}r_{\varepsilon}-\tfrac{\varepsilon^{2+\mu}}{2}\rho(\varphi,\theta){+}\tfrac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\mathtt{g}

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon^{2}{V}_{1}^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon r_{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon^{1+\mu}\rho)+\varepsilon^{3}{V}_{2}^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon r_{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon^{1+\mu}\rho)\Big)h\Big]-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{H}[h]

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon^{2}\mathbf{Q}_{1}[h]+\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{\theta}\mathcal{R}_{1}^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon r_{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon^{1+\mu}\rho)[h]+\varepsilon^{3}\partial_{\theta}\mathcal{R}_{2}^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon r_{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon^{1+\mu}\rho)[h].

By (
7.31
) we infer

{-}r_{\varepsilon}(\varphi,\theta)+\mathtt{g}(\varphi,\theta)\triangleq 2\mathtt{g}(\varphi,\theta)+2\varepsilon r_{2}(\varphi,\theta),\qquad\textnormal{with}\qquad\|r_{2}\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\lesssim 1.

(7.37)

Setting

\displaystyle{V}^{\varepsilon}(\rho)

\displaystyle\triangleq r_{2}+\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}{V}_{1}^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon r_{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon^{1+\mu}\rho)+{V}_{2}^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon r_{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon^{1+\mu}\rho),

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{0}^{\varepsilon}(\rho)[h]

\displaystyle\triangleq\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathcal{R}_{1}^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon r_{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon^{1+\mu}\rho)[h]+\mathcal{R}_{2}^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon r_{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon^{1+\mu}\rho)[h],

gives (
7.32
) and (
7.33
). The estimates (
7.34
), (
7.35
), (
7.36
) follow easily from Proposition
7.2
.
This ends the proof of Lemma
7.5
.
∎

7.4
Straightening the transport part

The purpose of this section is to conjugate the linearized operator
\mathcal{L}_{0}
in (
7.32
) through appropriate symplectic changes of variables so that in the new coordinates, the transport part has constant coefficients. Following the approach outlined in
[
59
, Sec. 4]
, we begin by performing a suitable change of variable acting only on space to transform the vector field in the advection part into a constant one, up to an error term of small size
in
\varepsilon
.
Notably, the time degeneracy is advantageous in this step. With this new structure, one may apply the standard KAM reduction method in the spirit of
[
8
,
58
]
.

Proposition 7.6
.

Let
(\gamma,s_{0},S)
as in (
7.3
). There exists
{\varepsilon}_{0}>0

such that if

\varepsilon\leqslant\varepsilon_{0},\qquad\|\rho\|_{s_{0}+2}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\leqslant 1,

(7.38)

there exist
\beta_{1}
taking the form

\displaystyle\beta_{1}(\lambda,\varphi,\theta)=\varepsilon b_{1}(\lambda,\varphi)+\varepsilon^{2}b_{2}(\lambda,\varphi,\theta)+\varepsilon^{2+\mu}\mathfrak{r}(\lambda,\varphi,\theta),

with

b_{2}(\lambda,\varphi,\theta)\triangleq\textnormal{Im}\Big\{\mathtt{w}_{2}\big(p_{\lambda}(\varphi)\big)e^{{\rm i}2\theta}\Big\},

(7.39)

where
\mathtt{w}_{2}(p)
is defined in (
7.14
) and an invertible symplectic change of variables

\mathscr{B}_{1}h(\lambda,\varphi,\theta)\triangleq\big(1+\partial_{\theta}\beta_{1}(\lambda,\varphi,\theta)\big)h\big(\lambda,\varphi,\theta+\beta_{1}(\lambda,\varphi,\theta)\big)

(7.40)

such that

\displaystyle\mathscr{B}_{1}^{-1}\Big(\varepsilon^{2}\omega(\lambda)\partial_{\varphi}+\partial_{\theta}\big[\mathbf{V}_{1}^{\varepsilon}(\rho)\cdot\big]\Big)\mathscr{B}_{1}=\varepsilon^{2}\omega(\lambda)\partial_{\varphi}+\partial_{\theta}\big[\big(\mathtt{c}_{0}+\varepsilon^{6}{\mathbf{V}}_{2}^{\varepsilon}({\rho})\big)\cdot\big].

(7.41)

Furthermore, we have

1.

The space-time independent function
\lambda\mapsto\mathtt{c}_{0}(\lambda)
writes

\mathtt{c}_{0}\triangleq\tfrac{1}{2}+\varepsilon^{3}\mathtt{c}_{1},\qquad\|\mathtt{c}_{1}\|^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\lesssim 1.

(7.42)

2.

The functions
\beta_{1}
,
\mathfrak{r}
,
b_{1}
and
\mathbf{V}_{2}^{\varepsilon}({\rho})
satisfy the following estimates for all
s\in[s_{0},S],

\displaystyle\|\beta_{1}\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon\left(1+\|\rho\|_{s+{2}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\right),

(7.43)

\displaystyle\|b_{1}\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}+\|\mathfrak{r}\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1+\|\rho\|_{s+{2}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)},

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{V}_{2}^{\varepsilon}({\rho})\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1+\|\rho\|_{s+{3}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}.

3.

The changes of variables
\mathscr{B}_{1}^{\pm 1}
satisfy the following estimates for all
s\in[s_{0},S],

\|\mathscr{B}_{1}^{\pm 1}h\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\lesssim\|h\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}+\varepsilon\|\rho\|_{s+3}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\|h\|_{s_{0}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}.

(7.44)

4.

Given two states
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
with the smallness property (
7.38
), then

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{12}\mathtt{c}_{0}\|^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon^{3}\|\Delta_{12}\rho\|_{s_{0}+2}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)},

(7.45)

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{12}\mathbf{V}_{2}^{\varepsilon}\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\Delta_{12}\rho\|_{s+3}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}+\|\Delta_{12}\rho\|_{s_{0}+3}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\max_{k\in\{1,2\}}\|\rho_{k}\|_{s+3}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}.

(7.46)

Proof.

Let us consider a symplectic diffeomorphism of the torus in the form

\mathscr{B}_{1}h(\varphi,\theta)\triangleq\big(1+\partial_{\theta}\beta_{1}(\varphi,\theta)\big)\mathrm{B}_{1}h(\varphi,\theta),\qquad\mathrm{B}_{1}h(\varphi,\theta)\triangleq h\big(\varphi,\theta+\beta_{1}(\varphi,\theta)\big),

(7.47)

with
\beta_{1}
to be chosen later in order to reduce the size of the coefficients. Due to the symplectic nature of
\mathscr{B}_{1},
we have

\displaystyle\langle h\rangle_{\theta}=0\quad\Rightarrow\quad\langle\mathscr{B}_{1}h\rangle_{\theta}=0.

Applying Lemma
A.1
-2, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathscr{B}_{1}^{-1}\Big(\varepsilon^{2}\omega(\lambda)\partial_{\varphi}+\partial_{\theta}\big[\mathbf{V}_{1}^{\varepsilon}(\rho)\cdot\big]\Big)\mathscr{B}_{1}=\varepsilon^{2}\omega(\lambda)\partial_{\varphi}+\partial_{y}\Big[\mathrm{B}_{1}^{-1}\big(\widehat{\mathbf{V}}_{1}^{\varepsilon}(\rho)\big)\cdot\Big],

with, using (
7.33
),

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{V}}_{1}^{\varepsilon}(\rho)

\displaystyle\triangleq\varepsilon^{2}\omega(\lambda)\partial_{\varphi}\beta_{1}+\mathbf{V}_{1}^{\varepsilon}(\rho)\big(1+\partial_{\theta}\beta_{1}\big)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{2}\omega(\lambda)\partial_{\varphi}\beta_{1}+\Big(\tfrac{1}{2}+\varepsilon^{2}\mathtt{g}-\tfrac{\varepsilon^{2+\mu}}{2}\rho+\varepsilon^{3}{V}^{\varepsilon}(\rho)\Big)\big(1+\partial_{\theta}\beta_{1}\big).

Select
\beta_{1}
in the following form

\beta_{1}(\lambda,\varphi,\theta)=\beta_{1,1}(\lambda,\varphi)+\varepsilon^{2}\beta_{1,2}(\lambda,\varphi,\theta)+\varepsilon^{4}\beta_{1,3}(\lambda,\varphi,\theta),

(7.48)

such that for any
n\in\{1,2,3\},
the function
\beta_{1,n}
satisfies the following constraints

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\tfrac{1}{2}\,\partial_{\theta}\beta_{1,2}&=\mathtt{F}_{1}-\langle\mathtt{F}_{1}\rangle_{\theta},\qquad\mathtt{F}_{1}\triangleq-\mathtt{g}+\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon^{\mu}\rho-\varepsilon V^{\varepsilon}(\rho),\\
\tfrac{1}{2}\,\partial_{\theta}\beta_{1,3}&=\mathtt{F}_{2}-\langle\mathtt{F}_{2}\rangle_{\theta},\qquad\mathtt{F}_{2}\triangleq-\omega(\lambda)\partial_{\varphi}\beta_{1,2}-\big(\mathtt{g}-\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon^{\mu}\rho+\varepsilon V^{\varepsilon}(\rho)\big)\partial_{\theta}\beta_{1,2},\end{cases}

(7.49)

and

\omega(\lambda)\partial_{\varphi}\beta_{1,1}=-\langle\mathtt{F}_{1}\rangle_{\theta}-\varepsilon^{2}\langle\mathtt{F}_{2}\rangle_{\theta}+\langle\mathtt{F}_{1}\rangle_{\theta,\varphi}+\varepsilon^{2}\langle\mathtt{F}_{2}\rangle_{\theta,\varphi}.

(7.50)

With these choices, one can check that

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{V}}_{1}^{\varepsilon}(\rho)=\tfrac{1}{2}-\varepsilon^{2}\langle\mathtt{F}_{1}\rangle_{\theta,\varphi}-\varepsilon^{4}\langle\mathtt{F}_{2}\rangle_{\theta,\varphi}+\varepsilon^{6}\widetilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1}^{\varepsilon}(\rho),

with

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1}^{\varepsilon}(\rho)\triangleq\omega(\lambda)\partial_{\varphi}\beta_{1,3}+\big(\mathtt{g}-\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon^{\mu}\rho+\varepsilon V^{\varepsilon}(\rho)\big)\partial_{\theta}\beta_{1,3}.

(7.51)

Using in particular (
7.14
), we have

\langle\rho\rangle_{\theta}=0,\qquad\langle\mathtt{g}\rangle_{\theta}=0.

(7.52)

Therefore,

\langle\mathtt{F}_{1}\rangle_{\theta}=-\varepsilon\langle V^{\varepsilon}(\rho)\rangle_{\theta}

(7.53)

and

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{V}}_{1}^{\varepsilon}(\rho)=\tfrac{1}{2}+\varepsilon^{3}\langle{V}^{\varepsilon}(\rho)\rangle_{\theta,\varphi}-\varepsilon^{4}\langle F_{2}\rangle_{\theta,\varphi}+\varepsilon^{6}\widetilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1}^{\varepsilon}(\rho).

By defining

\mathbf{V}_{2}^{\varepsilon}({\rho})\triangleq\mathrm{B}_{1}^{-1}\widetilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1}^{\varepsilon}(\rho),\qquad\mathtt{c}_{0}\triangleq\tfrac{1}{2}+\varepsilon^{3}\mathtt{c}_{1},\qquad\mathtt{c}_{1}\triangleq\big(\langle{V}^{\varepsilon}(\rho)\rangle_{\theta,\varphi}-\varepsilon\langle F_{2}\rangle_{\theta,\varphi}\big),

(7.54)

we end up with the identity (
7.41
).
The first equation in (
7.49
) writes

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\,\partial_{\theta}\beta_{1,2}(\varphi,\theta)=-\mathtt{g}(\varphi,\theta)+\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon^{\mu}\rho-\varepsilon V^{\varepsilon}(\rho)+\varepsilon\langle V^{\varepsilon}(\rho)\rangle_{\theta}.

The right hand-side has zero space average according to (
7.52
). Consequently, we can solve this equation via Fourier expansion to get from (
7.14
) that

\beta_{1,2}=b_{2}+\varepsilon^{\mu}\mathfrak{r}_{2},

(7.55)

with
b_{2}
as in (
7.39
) and
\mathfrak{r}_{2}
satisfying, in view of (
7.34
), the following esitmate

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{r}_{2}\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\lesssim 1+\varepsilon^{\mu}\|\rho\|_{s+1}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}.

As consequence

\displaystyle\|\beta_{1,2}\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\lesssim 1+\|\rho\|_{{s+1}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}.

(7.56)

One can proceed in a similar way with the second equation in (
7.49
) and get

\displaystyle\|\beta_{1,3}\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\lesssim 1+\|\rho\|_{{s+2}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}.

(7.57)

Now, from (
7.53
), the equation (
7.50
) becomes

\displaystyle\omega(\lambda)\partial_{\varphi}\beta_{1,1}=\varepsilon\big[\langle{V}^{\varepsilon}(\rho)\rangle_{\theta}-\langle{V}^{\varepsilon}(\rho)\rangle_{\theta,\varphi}+\varepsilon\langle\mathtt{F}_{2}\rangle_{\theta,\varphi}-\varepsilon\langle\mathtt{F}_{2}\rangle_{\theta}\big].

The
\varphi
-average in the right hand-side vanishes so we can invert and find from (
7.34
),

\beta_{1,1}=\varepsilon b_{1},\qquad\|b_{1}\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\lesssim 1+\varepsilon^{\mu}\|\rho\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}.

(7.58)

Putting together the identities (
7.48
),(
7.55
) and (
7.58
) leads to

\displaystyle\beta_{1}(\varphi,\theta)=\varepsilon b_{1}(\varphi)+\varepsilon^{2}b_{2}(\varphi,\theta)+\varepsilon^{2+\mu}\mathfrak{r}(\varphi,\theta),\qquad\textnormal{with}\qquad\mathfrak{r}(\varphi,\theta)\triangleq\mathfrak{r}_{2}(\varphi,\theta)+\varepsilon^{2-\mu}\beta_{1,3}(\varphi,\theta).

Moreover, from the last identity and the foregoing estimates, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\beta_{1}\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}+\|\mathfrak{r}\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\lesssim\varepsilon\big(1+\|\rho\|_{{s+2}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\big).

This estimate together with Lemma
A.2
concludes (
7.44
).
Now, from (
7.49
), (
7.54
), (
7.56
) and (
7.34
), we infer

\displaystyle\|\mathtt{c}_{1}\|^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\lesssim 1.

In addition, by (
7.51
), (
7.34
), (
7.57
), and the product law (
7.4
), we find

\|\widetilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1}^{\varepsilon}(\rho)\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\lesssim 1+\|\rho\|_{s+{3}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}.

(7.59)

Applying (
A.4
) together with (
7.59
) and (
7.38
), we get

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{V}_{2}^{\varepsilon}(\rho)\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}

\displaystyle=\|\mathrm{B}_{1}^{-1}\widetilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1}^{\varepsilon}(\rho)\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|\widetilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1}^{\varepsilon}(\rho)\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\big(1+C\|\beta_{1}\|_{s_{0}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\big)+C\|\beta_{1}\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\|\widetilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1}^{\varepsilon}(\rho)\|_{s_{0}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\Big)

\displaystyle\lesssim 1+\|\rho\|_{s+{3}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}.

Similarly, from Lemma
A.2
, the second estimate in (
7.34
), (
7.38
) we conclude (
7.45
). Finally, by (
7.42
) and (
7.34
), we find (
7.46
).
This ends the proof of Proposition
7.6
.
∎

Now, we shall state the final reduction step based on KAM tools. The proof is quite similar to that of
[
59
, Prop. 4.2]
and we recommend the reader this references for more details. To state the result, we need to use the following sequence
(N_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{-1\}}
given by

N_{-1}\triangleq 1,\qquad\forall n\in\mathbb{N},\quad N_{n}\triangleq N_{0}^{\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^{n}},

(7.60)

for some
N_{0}\geqslant 2.

Proposition 7.7
.

Let
(\gamma,s_{0},S)
as in (
7.3
) and

\mu_{2}\geqslant 4\tau+3.

(7.61)

There exists
{\varepsilon}_{0}>0
such that if

\displaystyle\gamma\varepsilon^{-2}\leqslant 1,\qquad N_{0}^{\mu_{2}}\varepsilon^{6}\gamma^{-1}\leqslant{\varepsilon}_{0},\qquad\|\rho\|_{\frac{3}{2}\mu_{2}+2s_{0}+2\tau+4}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\leqslant 1,

(7.62)

then, we can find
\mathtt{c}\triangleq\mathtt{c}(\lambda,\rho)\in\textnormal{Lip}_{\gamma}(\mathscr{O},\mathbb{R})
and
\beta_{2}\in\textnormal{Lip}_{\gamma}(\mathscr{O},H^{S})

with the following properties:

1.

The function
\lambda\in\mathscr{O}\mapsto\mathtt{c}(\lambda,\rho)
satisfies the following estimate,

\|\mathtt{c}-\mathtt{c}_{0}\|^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\lesssim{\varepsilon}^{{6}},

where
\mathtt{c}_{0}
is defined in (
7.42
).

2.

Consider the transformation
\mathscr{B}_{2}
given by

\mathscr{B}_{2}h(\lambda,\varphi,\theta)\triangleq\big(1+\partial_{\theta}\beta_{2}(\lambda,\varphi,\theta)\big)h\big(\lambda,\varphi,\theta+\beta_{2}(\lambda,\varphi,\theta)\big).

(7.63)

It is invertible with inverse taking the form

\displaystyle\mathscr{B}_{2}^{-1}h(\lambda,\varphi,\theta)\triangleq\big(1+\partial_{\theta}\widehat{\beta}_{2}(\lambda,\varphi,\theta)\big)h\big(\lambda,\varphi,\theta+\widehat{\beta}_{2}(\lambda,\varphi,\theta)\big).

Moreover, for any
s\in[s_{0},S],

\|\mathscr{B}_{2}^{\pm 1}h\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\lesssim\|h\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}+{\varepsilon^{{6}}\gamma^{-1}}\|\rho\|_{s+2\tau+{5}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\|h\|_{s_{0}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}

and

\|\widehat{\beta}_{2}\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\lesssim\|\beta_{2}\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\lesssim\varepsilon^{{6}}\gamma^{-1}\left(1+\|\rho\|_{s+2\tau+{4}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\right).

(7.64)

3.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, if
\lambda
belongs to the Cantor set

{\mathscr{O}_{n}^{1}(\rho)}\triangleq\bigcap_{(l,j)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\atop 1\leqslant|j|\leqslant N_{n}}\Big\{\lambda\in\mathscr{O}\quad\textnormal{s.t.}\quad\big|\varepsilon^{2}\omega(\lambda)l+j\mathtt{c}(\lambda,\rho)\big|\geqslant{\gamma}{|j|^{-\tau}}\Big\},

(7.65)

then the following identity holds

\displaystyle\mathscr{B}_{2}^{-1}\Big(

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{2}\omega(\lambda)\partial_{\varphi}+\partial_{\theta}\big[\big(\mathtt{c}_{0}+\varepsilon^{6}\mathbf{V}_{2}^{\varepsilon}(\rho)\big)\cdot\big]\Big)\mathscr{B}_{2}=\varepsilon^{2}\omega(\lambda)\partial_{\varphi}+\mathtt{c}(\lambda,\rho)\partial_{\theta}+\mathtt{E}_{n},

(7.66)

with
\mathtt{E}_{n}
a linear operator satisfying

\|\mathtt{E}_{n}h\|_{s_{0}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\lesssim\varepsilon^{{6}}N_{0}^{\mu_{2}}N_{n+1}^{-\mu_{2}}\|h\|_{s_{0}+2}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}.

(7.67)

4.

Given two small states
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
both satisfying the smallness properties (
7.62
), we have

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{12}\mathtt{c}\|^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon^{3}\|\Delta_{12}\rho\|_{2{s}_{0}+2\tau+3}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}.

(7.68)

Now we can state the main result of this subsection which addresses the conjugation of the linearized
operator
\mathcal{L}_{0}
,
as described by (
7.25
), using the changes of coordinates discussed before.

Proposition 7.8
.

Given the conditions (
7.3
), (
7.61
) and (
7.62
). Let
\mathscr{B}_{1},\mathscr{B}_{2}
as in (
7.40
) and (
7.63
). Then, the following properties hold true.

1.

The operator

\mathscr{B}\triangleq\mathscr{B}_{1}\mathscr{B}_{2}

writes

\mathscr{B}h(\lambda,\varphi,\theta)=\big(1+\partial_{\theta}\beta(\lambda,\varphi,\theta)\big)h\big(\lambda,\varphi,\theta+\beta(\lambda,\varphi,\theta)\big),

(7.69)

with

\displaystyle\beta(\lambda,\varphi,\theta)\triangleq\beta_{1}(\lambda,\varphi,\theta)+\beta_{2}\big(\lambda,\varphi,\theta+\beta_{1}(\lambda,\varphi,\theta)\big)

satisfying the estimate

\|\beta\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\lesssim\varepsilon\big(1+\|\rho\|_{s+2\tau+{4}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\big).

Moreover,
\mathscr{B}
is invertible and satisfies the estimate, for any
s\in[s_{0},S]

\displaystyle\|\mathscr{B}^{\pm 1}h\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|h\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}+\|\rho\|_{s+2\tau+{5}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\|h\|_{s_{0}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}.

2.

For any
\lambda
in the Cantor set
{\mathscr{O}_{n}^{1}(\rho)}
, defined in (
7.65
),
one has

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1}\triangleq\mathscr{B}^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{0}\mathscr{B}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{2}\omega(\lambda)\partial_{\varphi}+\mathtt{c}(\lambda,\rho)\partial_{\theta}-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{\theta}\mathfrak{Q}_{1}+\partial_{\theta}\mathfrak{R}_{1}^{\varepsilon}+\mathtt{E}_{n},

where the linear operator
\mathtt{E}_{n}
is the same as in Proposition
7.7
and

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Q}_{1}[h](\varphi,\theta)

\displaystyle\triangleq\int_{\mathbb{T}}h(\varphi,\eta)\mathtt{Q}_{1}\big(p(\varphi),\theta,\eta\big)d\eta,

\displaystyle\mathtt{Q}_{1}(p,\theta,\eta)

\displaystyle\triangleq\tfrac{\cos(\theta-\eta)}{r_{\mathbf{D}}^{2}(p)}-\tfrac{1}{2}\textnormal{Re}\left\{\big(\partial_{z}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{D}}(p)\big)^{2}e^{{\rm i}(\theta+\eta)}\right\}.

In addition, the operator
\partial_{\theta}\mathfrak{R}_{1}^{\varepsilon}
satisfies the estimates: for any
s\in[s_{0},S],\,N\geqslant 0
,

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\theta}\mathfrak{R}_{1}^{\varepsilon}h\|_{s,N}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\big(\varepsilon^{2+{\mu}}+\varepsilon^{6}\gamma^{-1}\big)\Big(\|h\|_{{s}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\big(1+\|\rho\|_{s_{0}+2\tau+{5}+N}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\big)+\|h\|_{{s_{0}}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\|\rho\|_{s+2\tau+{5}+N}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\Big).

Proof.

{\bf 1}.
The structure (
7.69
) follows from Lemma
A.1
-1 together with (
7.40
) and (
7.63
). Then, making use of Lemma
A.2
-
1
, (
7.43
) and (
7.64
), we get the desired estimates.

{\bf 2}.
According to (
7.32
), (
7.41
) and (
7.66
), we get that for any
\lambda\in\mathscr{O}_{n}^{1}(\rho)

\displaystyle\mathscr{B}^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{0}\mathscr{B}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{2}\omega(\lambda)\partial_{\varphi}+\mathtt{c}(\lambda,\rho)\partial_{\theta}+\mathtt{E}_{n}-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathscr{B}^{-1}\mathbf{H}\mathscr{B}+\varepsilon^{2}\mathscr{B}^{-1}\partial_{\theta}\mathbf{Q}_{1}\mathscr{B}+\varepsilon^{3}\mathscr{B}^{-1}\partial_{\theta}\mathcal{R}^{\varepsilon}_{0}(\rho)\mathscr{B}.

(7.70)

In the sequel, for convenience, we will omit the dependence on
\lambda
since it does not play any role here.
Denoting

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}_{0}\triangleq\mathscr{B}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{H}\mathscr{B}_{1}-\mathbf{H},

we infer

\displaystyle\mathscr{B}^{-1}\mathbf{H}\mathscr{B}-\mathbf{H}

\displaystyle=\mathscr{B}_{2}^{-1}\mathscr{B}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{H}\mathscr{B}_{1}\mathscr{B}_{2}-\mathbf{H}

\displaystyle=\mathscr{B}_{2}^{-1}\big[\mathscr{B}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{H}\mathscr{B}_{1}-\mathbf{H}\big]\mathscr{B}_{2}+\mathscr{B}_{2}^{-1}\mathbf{H}\mathscr{B}_{2}-\mathbf{H}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{H}_{0}+(\mathscr{B}_{2}^{-1}\mathbf{H}_{0}\mathscr{B}_{2}-\mathbf{H}_{0})+\mathscr{B}_{2}^{-1}\mathbf{H}\mathscr{B}_{2}-\mathbf{H}.

(7.71)

Note that the inverse diffeomorphism
\mathscr{B}_{1}^{-1}
admits the form

\mathscr{B}_{1}^{-1}h(\varphi,y)=\big(1+\partial_{y}\widehat{\beta_{1}}(\varphi,y)\big)h\big(\varphi,y+\widehat{\beta_{1}}(\varphi,y)\big),

where, by means of
[
59
, Lem. A.6]
and Proposition
7.6

\displaystyle\widehat{\beta_{1}}(\varphi,\theta)=-\varepsilon\,b_{1}(\varphi)-\varepsilon^{2}b_{2}\big(\varphi,\theta\big)+\varepsilon^{2+\mu}\widehat{\mathfrak{r}}\big(\varphi,\theta\big)\qquad\textnormal{with}\qquad\|\widehat{\mathfrak{r}}\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\lesssim 1+\|\rho\|_{{s+3}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}.

(7.72)

Thus, in view of (
7.5
), we get

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}_{0}[h](\lambda,\varphi,\theta)=\partial_{\theta}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathbb{K}_{0}(\lambda,\varphi,\theta,\eta)h(\lambda,\varphi,\eta)\,d\eta,

where

\displaystyle\mathbb{K}_{0}(\lambda,\varphi,\theta,\eta)

\displaystyle\triangleq\log\left|\frac{e^{{\rm i}\,(\theta+\widehat{\beta_{1}}(\lambda,\varphi,\theta))}-e^{{\rm i}\,(\eta+\widehat{\beta_{1}}(\lambda,\varphi,\eta))}}{e^{{\rm i}\,\theta}-e^{{\rm i}\,\eta}}\right|

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{2}\hbox{Im}\bigg\{\frac{e^{{\rm i}\,\theta}b_{2}(\varphi,\theta)-e^{{\rm i}\,\eta}b_{2}(\varphi,\eta)}{e^{{\rm i}\,\theta}-e^{{\rm i}\,\eta}}\bigg\}+\varepsilon^{2+\mu}\mathbb{K}_{1}(\varphi,\theta,\eta),

with the kernel
\mathbb{K}_{1}
satisfying the estimate

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{K}_{1}\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\lesssim 1+\|\rho\|_{s+{3}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)},

(7.73)

where we have used Taylor expansion combined with (
7.72
).
Making appeal to the expression (
7.39
) and using the identity

\displaystyle\big(e^{{\rm i}2\theta}-e^{{\rm i}2\eta}\big)\hbox{Im}\Big\{\tfrac{e^{{\rm i}\eta}}{e^{{\rm i}\theta}-e^{{\rm i}\eta}}\Big\}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2{\rm i}}\big(e^{{\rm i}\theta}+e^{{\rm i}\eta}\big)^{2},

we infer

\displaystyle\mathbb{K}_{0}(\varphi,\theta,\eta)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{2}\Big(b_{2}(\varphi,\theta)-b_{2}(\varphi,\eta)\Big)\hbox{Im}\Big\{\tfrac{e^{{\rm i}\eta}}{e^{{\rm i}\theta}-e^{{\rm i}\eta}}\Big\}+\varepsilon^{2+\mu}\mathbb{K}_{1}(\varphi,\theta,\eta)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{2}\textnormal{Im}\Big\{\mathtt{w}_{2}\big(p(\varphi)\big)\big(e^{{\rm i}2\theta}-e^{{\rm i}2\eta}\big)\Big\}\hbox{Im}\Big\{\tfrac{e^{{\rm i}\eta}}{e^{{\rm i}\theta}-e^{{\rm i}\eta}}\Big\}+\varepsilon^{2+\mu}\mathbb{K}_{1}(\varphi,\theta,\eta)

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\textnormal{Re}\Big\{\mathtt{w}_{2}\big(p(\varphi)\big)\big(e^{{\rm i}\theta}+e^{{\rm i}\eta}\big)^{2}\Big\}+\varepsilon^{2+\mu}\mathbb{K}_{1}(\varphi,\theta,\eta).

As a consequence, using in particular the fact that
h
has zero space average, we can write

\mathbf{H}_{0}[h]=-\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{\theta}\widehat{\mathbf{Q}}_{1}[h]+\varepsilon^{2+\mu}\mathbf{H}_{1}[h],

(7.74)

where

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{Q}}_{1}[h](\varphi,\theta)

\displaystyle\triangleq\int_{\mathbb{T}}\textnormal{Re}\Big\{\mathtt{w}_{2}\big(p(\varphi)\big)e^{{\rm i}(\theta+\eta)}\Big\}h(\varphi,\eta)\,d\eta,

(7.75)

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}_{1}[h](\varphi,\theta)

\displaystyle\triangleq\partial_{\theta}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathbb{K}_{1}(\varphi,\theta,\eta)h(\varphi,\eta)\,d\eta.

Besides, one has

\displaystyle\mathscr{B}^{-1}\partial_{\theta}\mathbf{Q}_{1}\mathscr{B}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\theta}\mathbf{Q}_{1}+\mathscr{B}^{-1}\partial_{\theta}\mathbf{Q}_{1}\mathscr{B}-\partial_{\theta}\mathbf{Q}_{1}.

(7.76)

Plugging (
7.71
), (
7.76
) and (
7.74
) into (
7.70
) implies

\displaystyle\mathscr{B}^{-1}\mathscr{L}_{1}\mathscr{B}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{2}\omega(\lambda)\partial_{\varphi}+\mathtt{c}\partial_{\theta}+\mathtt{E}_{n}^{0}-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{\theta}\mathfrak{Q}_{1}+\partial_{\theta}\mathfrak{R}_{1}^{\varepsilon},

where

\mathfrak{Q}_{1}\triangleq\mathbf{Q}_{1}+\tfrac{1}{2}\widehat{\mathbf{Q}}_{1}

(7.77)

and

\displaystyle\partial_{\theta}\mathfrak{R}_{1}^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\triangleq\varepsilon^{3}\mathscr{B}^{-1}\partial_{\theta}\mathcal{R}^{\varepsilon}_{0}(\rho)\mathscr{B}-\tfrac{1}{2}[\mathscr{B}_{2}^{-1}\mathbf{H}_{0}\mathscr{B}_{2}-\mathbf{H}_{0}]-\tfrac{1}{2}\big[\mathscr{B}_{2}^{-1}\mathbf{H}\mathscr{B}_{2}-\mathbf{H}\big]

(7.78)

\displaystyle-\varepsilon^{2}\big[\mathscr{B}^{-1}\partial_{\theta}\mathbf{Q}_{1}\mathscr{B}-\partial_{\theta}\mathbf{Q}_{1}\big]-{\tfrac{1}{2}}\varepsilon^{2+\mu}\mathbf{H}_{1}.

Combining (
7.77
), (
7.75
), (
7.14
), (
7.15
) and (
7.17
) yields the following integral representation

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Q}_{1}[h](\varphi,\theta)

\displaystyle\triangleq\int_{\mathbb{T}}h(\varphi,\eta)\mathtt{Q}_{1}\big(p(\varphi),\theta,\eta\big)d\eta,\qquad\mathtt{Q}_{1}(p,\theta,\eta)\triangleq\tfrac{\cos(\theta-\eta)}{r_{\mathbf{D}}^{2}(p)}-\tfrac{1}{2}\textnormal{Re}\left\{\big(\partial_{z}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{D}}(p)\big)^{2}e^{{\rm i}(\theta+\eta)}\right\}.

Now, by virtue of
[
12
, Lem. 2.36]
, (
7.64
) and (
7.62
) we can write

\displaystyle(\mathscr{B}_{2}^{-1}\mathbf{H}\mathscr{B}_{2}-\mathbf{H})h(\varphi,\theta)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}}{\mathscr{K}}_{1}(\varphi,\theta,\eta)h(\varphi,\eta)\,d\eta,

with
\mathscr{K}_{1}
satisfying the following estimates, for any
s\in[s_{0},S],

\displaystyle\|\mathscr{K}_{1}\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\lesssim\varepsilon^{{6}}\gamma^{-1}\left(1+\|\rho\|_{s+2\tau+{5}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\right).

(7.79)

Moreover, according to Lemma
A.3
, (
7.43
), (
7.64
) and (
7.62
), we have

\displaystyle\big(\mathscr{B}^{-1}\partial_{\theta}\mathbf{Q}_{1}\mathscr{B}-\partial_{\theta}\mathbf{Q}_{1}\big)[h](\varphi,\theta)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}}h(\varphi,{\eta})\mathscr{K}_{2}(\varphi,\theta,\eta)d{\eta},

\displaystyle\big(\mathscr{B}_{2}^{-1}\mathbf{H}_{0}\mathscr{B}_{2}-\mathbf{H}_{0}\big)[h](\varphi,\theta)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}}h(\varphi,\eta){\mathscr{K}}_{3}(\varphi,\theta,\eta)d\eta,

\displaystyle\mathscr{B}^{-1}\partial_{\theta}\mathcal{R}^{\varepsilon}_{0}(\rho)\mathscr{B}[h](\varphi,\theta)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}}h(\varphi,\eta)\mathscr{K}_{4}(\varphi,\theta,\eta)d\eta,

with

\displaystyle\|\mathscr{K}_{2}\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon\left(1+\|\rho\|_{s+2\tau+{5}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\right),

\displaystyle\|\mathscr{K}_{3}\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon^{6}\gamma^{-1}\left(1+\|\rho\|_{s+2\tau+{5}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\right),

\displaystyle\|\mathscr{K}_{4}\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1+\|\rho\|_{s+2\tau+{5}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}.

Thus, the operator
\partial_{\theta}\mathfrak{R}_{1}^{\varepsilon}
, introduced in (
7.78
), is an integral operator with the kernel

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{1}^{\varepsilon}\triangleq\varepsilon^{3}{\mathscr{K}}_{4}-\tfrac{1}{2}{\mathscr{K}}_{3}-\tfrac{1}{2}{\mathscr{K}}_{1}-\varepsilon^{2}{\mathscr{K}}_{2}+\varepsilon^{3}\mathcal{K}_{0}^{\varepsilon}-\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon^{2+\mu}\partial_{\theta}\mathbb{K}_{1},

which satisfies the estimate

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{K}_{1}^{\varepsilon}\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\lesssim\big(\varepsilon^{2+{\mu}}+\varepsilon^{6}\gamma^{-1}\big)\left(1+\|\rho\|_{s+2\tau+{5}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\right).

We conclude the estimate of
\mathfrak{R}_{1}^{\varepsilon}
by applying Lemma
A.3
. This ends the proof of Proposition
7.8
.
∎

7.5
Invertibility of the linearized operator

The main goal this section is to transform the linearized operator
\mathcal{L}_{0}
, as defined in (
7.32
), into a Fourier multiplier up to a small error. This step is required along Nash-Moser scheme and will be done in the spirit of
[
59
, Sec.
5
]
. As the leading term of this operator degenerates on the modes
\pm 1,
we shall first split the phase space into two parts: a subspace localized on the modes
\pm 1
and its complement. This leads to explore the invertibility of a matrix-valued operator that will be done in different steps as detailed below.

Let
\Pi_{1}
stand for the orthogonal projection on the modes
\pm 1
acting on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2},\mathbb{R})
as follows

\displaystyle h(\varphi,\theta)=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}h_{j}(\varphi)e^{{\rm i}j\theta}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\Pi_{1}h\triangleq\sum_{j=\pm 1}h_{j}(\varphi)e^{{\rm i}j\theta}.

(7.80)

We also define

\Pi_{1}^{\perp}\triangleq\textnormal{Id}-\Pi_{1}.

As the function
h
is real-valued then

\forall j\in\mathbb{Z},\quad h_{-j}(\varphi)=\overline{h_{j}(\varphi)}.

Now, we decompose the phase space
\textnormal{Lip}_{\gamma}(\mathscr{O},H_{0}^{s})
as follows,

\displaystyle X^{s}\triangleq\textnormal{Lip}_{\gamma}(\mathscr{O},H_{0}^{s})=X^{s}_{1}\overset{\perp}{\oplus}X^{s}_{\perp},

(7.81)

with

\displaystyle X^{s}_{1}\triangleq\big\{h\in\textnormal{Lip}_{\gamma}(\mathscr{O},H^{s}_{0})\quad\textnormal{s.t.}\quad\Pi_{1}h=h\big\},

\displaystyle X^{s}_{\perp}\triangleq\big\{h\in\textnormal{Lip}_{\gamma}(\mathscr{O},H^{s}_{0})\quad\textnormal{s.t.}\quad\Pi_{1}^{\perp}h=h\big\}.

Recall from Proposition
7.8
and Proposition
7.7
that the operator
\mathcal{L}_{1}
decomposes as below

\mathcal{L}_{1}=\mathbb{L}_{1}+\partial_{\theta}\mathfrak{R}_{1}+\mathtt{E}_{n},\qquad\mathbb{L}_{1}\triangleq\varepsilon^{2}\omega(\lambda)\partial_{\varphi}+\mathtt{c}(\lambda,\rho)\partial_{\theta}-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{\theta}\mathfrak{Q}_{1},

(7.82)

with

\mathfrak{Q}_{1}[h](\varphi,\theta)\triangleq r_{\mathbf{D}}^{-2}\big(p(\varphi)\big)\textnormal{Re}\left\{h_{1}(\varphi)e^{{\rm i}\theta}\right\}-\tfrac{1}{2}\textnormal{Re}\left\{\big[\partial_{z}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{D}}\big(p(\varphi)\big)\big]^{2}h_{-1}(\varphi)e^{{\rm i}\theta}\right\}

and

\mathtt{c}=\tfrac{1}{2}+\varepsilon^{3}\mathtt{c}_{1}+(\mathtt{c}-\mathtt{c}_{0})\triangleq\tfrac{1}{2}+\varepsilon^{3}\mathtt{c}_{2},

(7.83)

such that

\displaystyle\|\mathtt{c}_{2}\|^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\leqslant C.

(7.84)

Upon straightforward analysis, it becomes apparent that the linear operator

\mathbb{L}_{1}+\partial_{\theta}\mathfrak{R}_{1}^{\varepsilon}:\textnormal{Lip}_{\gamma}(\mathscr{O},X^{s})\to\textnormal{Lip}_{\gamma}(\mathscr{O},X^{s-1})

is well-defined and its action is equivalent to the matrix operator
\displaystyle{\mathbb{M}:X^{s}_{1}\times X^{s}_{\perp}\to X^{s-1}_{1}\times X^{s-1}_{\perp}}
with

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}

\displaystyle\triangleq\begin{pmatrix}\Pi_{1}\mathbb{L}_{1}\Pi_{1}&0\\
0&\Pi_{1}^{\perp}\mathbb{L}_{1}\Pi_{1}^{\perp}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}\Pi_{1}\partial_{\theta}\mathfrak{R}_{1}^{\varepsilon}\Pi_{1}&\Pi_{1}\partial_{\theta}\mathfrak{R}_{1}^{\varepsilon}\Pi_{1}^{\perp}\\
\Pi_{1}^{\perp}\partial_{\theta}\mathfrak{R}_{1}^{\varepsilon}\Pi_{1}&\Pi_{1}^{\perp}\partial_{\theta}\mathfrak{R}_{1}^{\varepsilon}\Pi_{1}^{\perp}\end{pmatrix}

(7.85)

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathbb{M}_{1}+\partial_{\theta}\mathbb{R}_{1},

where, according to Proposition
7.8
and (
7.103
), for any
H=(h_{1},h_{2})\in X_{1}^{s}\times X_{\perp}^{s}
,
s\in[s_{0},S]
and
N\geqslant 0
, one has

\|\partial_{\theta}\mathbb{R}_{1}H\|_{s,N}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\leqslant C\varepsilon^{2+{\mu}}\Big(\|H\|_{{s}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\big(1+\|\rho\|_{s_{0}+N+2\tau+5}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\big)+\|\rho\|_{s+N+2\tau+5}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\|H\|_{{s_{0}}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\Big).

(7.86)

Hence, we will examine the invertibility of the scalar operator
\mathbb{L}_{1}+\partial_{\theta}\mathfrak{R}_{1}^{\varepsilon}
by analyzing the invertibility of the matrix operator
\mathbb{M}
. This analysis involves inverting its main part
\mathbb{M}_{1}
alongside employing perturbative arguments. To achieve this, we will break the process down into several steps.

7.5.1
Degeneracy of the modes
\pm 1
and monodromy matrix

The main objective is to demonstrate the invertibility of the operator
\Pi\mathbb{L}_{1}\Pi
, defined through (
7.85
) and (
7.82
). More precisely, by virtue of (
7.82
), (
7.83
) and the identity

(\partial_{\theta}-\mathbf{H})\Pi_{1}=0,

one has

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}_{1,1}\triangleq\Pi_{1}\mathbb{L}_{1}\Pi_{1}=\varepsilon^{2}\Big(\omega(\lambda)\partial_{\varphi}+\varepsilon\mathtt{c}_{2}(\lambda)\Pi_{1}\partial_{\theta}+\Pi_{1}\partial_{\theta}\mathfrak{Q}_{1}\Pi_{1}\Big).

Notably, this operator localizes in the Fourier spatial modes
\pm 1
and displays a degeneracy in
\varepsilon
. To invert it, we consider an arbitrary real-valued function

(\varphi,\theta)\mapsto g(\varphi,\theta)=\displaystyle{\sum_{j=\pm 1}g_{j}(\varphi)e^{{\rm i}j\theta}\in X^{s-1}_{1}}

and we shall solve in the real space
X^{s}_{1}
the equation

\mathbb{L}_{1,1}h=g.

Since
g
and
h
are real then
g_{-n}=\overline{g_{n}}
and
h_{-n}=\overline{h_{n}}
. Thus,
using Fourier expansion, the last equation is equivalent to

\partial_{\varphi}H-\mathbf{A}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\varphi)H=\varepsilon^{-2}G,

(7.87)

where

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\varphi)\triangleq\begin{pmatrix}\mathtt{u}_{\lambda}(\varphi)-\frac{{\rm i}\varepsilon\mathtt{c}_{2}(\lambda)}{2\omega(\lambda)}&\mathtt{v}_{\lambda}(\varphi)\\
\overline{\mathtt{v}_{\lambda}(\varphi)}&\overline{\mathtt{u}_{\lambda}(\varphi)}+\frac{{\rm i}\varepsilon\mathtt{c}_{2}(\lambda)}{2\omega(\lambda)}\end{pmatrix},\,\qquad\begin{aligned} \mathtt{u}_{\lambda}(\varphi)&=-\tfrac{{\rm i}}{2\omega(\lambda)}\tfrac{1}{r_{\mathbf{D}}^{2}(p)},\\
\mathtt{v}_{\lambda}(\varphi)&=\tfrac{{\rm i}}{4\omega(\lambda)}\big(\partial_{z}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{D}}(p)\big)^{2}\end{aligned}

(7.88)

and

G(\varphi)\triangleq\frac{1}{\omega(\lambda)}\begin{pmatrix}g_{1}(\varphi)\\
\overline{g_{1}(\varphi)}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{C}^{2},\,\qquad H(\varphi)\triangleq\begin{pmatrix}h_{1}(\varphi)\\
\overline{h_{1}(\varphi)}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{C}^{2}.

We intend to prove the following result.

Proposition 7.9
.

Assume (
3.18
), there exists
\varepsilon_{1}>0
such that for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{1})
, the operator
\mathbb{L}_{1,1}:X^{s}_{1}\to X^{s-1}_{1}
is invertible with inverse
\mathbb{L}_{1,1}^{-1}
satisfying

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{L}_{1,1}^{-1}g\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\leqslant C\varepsilon^{-2}\|g\|_{s-1}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}.

Proof.

Let
(\varphi,\phi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\mapsto\mathscr{F}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\varphi,\phi)
be the fundamental matrix defined through the
2\times 2
matrix ODE

\begin{cases}\partial_{\varphi}\mathscr{F}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\varphi,\phi)-\mathbf{A}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\varphi)\mathscr{F}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\varphi,\phi)=0,\\
\mathscr{F}(\phi,\phi)=\textnormal{Id}.\end{cases}

Then the solution
H
can be expressed in the form

\displaystyle H(\varphi)=\mathscr{F}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\varphi,0)H(0)+\varepsilon^{-2}\int_{0}^{\varphi}\mathscr{F}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\varphi,\phi)G(\phi)d\phi.

(7.89)

Observe that the matrix
A
, defined by (
7.88
), is
2\pi-
periodic in the variable
\varphi
. Then
H
is
2\pi-
periodic if and only if

H(2\pi)=H(0).

Equivalently,

\displaystyle\big(\hbox{Id}-\mathscr{F}(\varepsilon,\lambda,2\pi,0)\big)H(0)=\varepsilon^{-2}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathscr{F}(\varepsilon,\lambda,2\pi,\phi)G(\phi)d\phi.

(7.90)

To find a unique solution to this equation it is enough to show that the matrix
\hbox{Id}-\mathscr{F}(\varepsilon,\lambda,2\pi,0)
is invertible.
To this end, we use the decomposition

\mathbf{A}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\varphi)=\mathbb{A}_{\lambda}(\varphi)+\mathbf{B}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\varphi),\qquad\mathbb{A}_{\lambda}(\varphi)\triangleq\mathbf{A}(0,\lambda,\varphi),\qquad\mathbf{B}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\varphi)\triangleq\mathbf{A}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\varphi)-\mathbf{A}(0,\lambda,\varphi).

According to (
7.84
), one has

\sup_{\varphi\in\mathbb{R}}\|\mathbf{B}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\varphi)\|\lesssim\varepsilon.

(7.91)

Now, consider the fundamental solution of the unperturbed problem

\begin{cases}\partial_{\varphi}\mathscr{M}_{\lambda}(\varphi)-\mathbb{A}_{\lambda}(\varphi)\mathscr{M}_{\lambda}(\varphi)=0,\\
\mathscr{M}_{\lambda}(0)=\textnormal{Id}.\end{cases}

(7.92)

Then one may write

\mathscr{F}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\varphi,0)=\mathscr{M}_{\lambda}(\varphi)+\mathscr{G}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\varphi),

with

\partial_{\varphi}\mathscr{G}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\varphi)-\mathbb{A}_{\lambda}(\varphi)\,\mathscr{G}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\varphi)=-\mathbf{B}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\varphi)\mathscr{F},\quad\mathscr{G}(\varepsilon,\lambda,0)=0.

and

\displaystyle\sup_{\varphi\in[0,2\pi]}\|\mathscr{G}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\varphi)\|\leqslant C\varepsilon,

where we have used for the last inequality the estimate (
7.91
).
The resolvant matrix must have the following form

\mathscr{M}_{\lambda}(\varphi)=\begin{pmatrix}a_{\lambda}(\varphi)&b_{\lambda}(\varphi)\\
\overline{b_{\lambda}(\varphi)}&\overline{a_{\lambda}(\varphi)}\end{pmatrix},\qquad a_{\lambda}(0)=1,\qquad b_{\lambda}(0)=0.

(7.93)

Remark that the property
\textnormal{Tr}(\mathbb{A}_{\lambda})\equiv 0
gives

|a_{\lambda}(\varphi)|^{2}-|b_{\lambda}(\varphi)|^{2}=\det\big(\mathscr{M}_{\lambda}(\varphi)\big)=\exp\left(\int_{0}^{\varphi}\textnormal{Tr}\big(\mathbb{A}_{\lambda}(u)\big)du\right)=1.

Hence,

\det(\mathscr{M}_{\lambda}-\textnormal{Id})=|a_{\lambda}-1|^{2}-|b_{\lambda}|^{2}=2\big(1-\textnormal{Re}(a_{\lambda})\big)=2-\textnormal{Tr}\big(\mathscr{M}_{\lambda}(2\pi)\big).

In particular,

\det\big(\mathscr{M}_{\lambda}(2\pi)-\textnormal{Id}\big)\neq 0\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad\textnormal{Tr}\big(\mathscr{M}_{\lambda}(2\pi)\big)\neq 2.

(7.94)

Thus, under the assumption (
3.18
), the matrix
\mathscr{M}_{\lambda}(2\pi)-\textnormal{Id}
is invertible and there exist
C>0
such that

\|\big(\mathscr{M}_{\lambda}(2\pi)-\textnormal{Id}\big)^{-1}\|^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\leqslant C.

Therefore, by using perturbation arguments we conclude that the matrix
\mathscr{F}(\varepsilon,\lambda,2\pi)-\textnormal{Id}
is invertible on
[\lambda_{*},\lambda^{*}]
and we have

\|\big(\mathscr{F}(\varepsilon,2\pi)-\textnormal{Id}\big)^{-1}\|^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\leqslant C.

Consequentely, the equation (
7.90
) admits a unique solution satisfying

\displaystyle\|H(0)\|^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}_{s}\leqslant C\varepsilon^{-2}\|G\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})},

(7.95)

where we have used, for
\varepsilon
small enough, the estimate

\sup_{\varphi,\phi\in[0,2\pi]}\|\mathscr{F}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\varphi,\phi)\|\leqslant C.

From the previous analysis we conclude that the equation (
7.87
) admits a unique solution which satisfies, in view of (
7.95
) and (
7.89
) the estimate: for all
s>1

\displaystyle\|H\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\leqslant C\varepsilon^{-2}\|G\|_{s-1}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}.

This implies that the linear operator
\mathbb{L}_{1,1}:X^{s}_{1}\to X^{s-1}_{1}
is invertible on
[\lambda_{*},\lambda^{*}]
and

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{L}_{1,1}^{-1}g\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\leqslant C\varepsilon^{-2}\|g\|_{s-1}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}.

This achieves the proof of the desired result.
∎

We shall end this section with some comments on the monodromy matrix that can be linked to Riccatti equation.
As we have seen before,

\displaystyle\det(\mathscr{M}_{\lambda})=1,

which implies that the map

\displaystyle\varphi\in\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathscr{M}_{\lambda}(\varphi)\in SL(2;\mathbb{C})

is well defined. We will associate to each element
\mathscr{M}_{\lambda}(\varphi)
the Möbius transform

\displaystyle T(\varphi):z\in\mathbb{D}\mapsto\frac{a_{\lambda}(\varphi)\,z+b_{\lambda}(\varphi)}{\overline{b_{\lambda}(\varphi)}\,z+\overline{a_{\lambda}(\varphi)}}\in\mathbb{D}.

Notice that
T(\varphi):\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{D}
is an automorphism with
T(0)=\hbox{Id}.
Straightforward computations based on (
7.92
) lead to

\begin{cases}\partial_{\varphi}T=-{\rm i}\mu\,T-\overline{\mathtt{v}_{\lambda}}\,T^{2}+\mathtt{v}_{\lambda},\\
T(0,z)=z\in\mathbb{D},\end{cases}

with
T^{2}(\varphi,z)\triangleq\big(T(\varphi,z)\big)^{2}
and

\mu\triangleq\tfrac{e^{2\lambda}}{\omega(\lambda)},\qquad\mathtt{v}_{\lambda}(\varphi)\triangleq\tfrac{{\rm i}}{4\omega(\lambda)}\Big(\partial_{z}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{D}}\big(p(\varphi)\big)\Big)^{2}.

By setting

\displaystyle\mathtt{T}(\varphi)\triangleq e^{-i\mu\varphi}T(\varphi),\qquad\varrho(\varphi)\triangleq e^{i\mu\varphi}\mathtt{v}_{\lambda}(\varphi),

we get Riccatti equation

\begin{cases}\partial_{\varphi}\mathtt{T}=-\overline{\varrho}\,\,\mathtt{T}^{2}+\varrho,\\
\mathtt{T}(0,z)=z\in\mathbb{D}.\end{cases}

7.5.2
Invertibility of
\Pi_{1}^{\perp}\mathbb{L}_{1}\Pi_{1}^{\perp}

The purpose here is to find a right inverse for the operator

\mathbb{L}_{1,\perp}\triangleq\Pi_{1}^{\perp}\mathbb{L}_{1}\Pi_{1}^{\perp},

where
\mathbb{L}_{1}
is defined through (
7.82
) and (
7.80
). Recall from Proposition
7.8
-2 that the operator
\mathfrak{Q}_{1}
localizes on the modes
\pm 1
implying that

\Pi_{1}^{\perp}\mathfrak{Q}_{1}\Pi_{1}^{\perp}=0.

Therefore, the linear operator

\mathbb{L}_{1,\perp}:X^{s}_{\perp}\to X^{s-1}_{\perp}

is well-defined and assumes the structure

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}_{1,\perp}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{2}\omega(\lambda)\partial_{\varphi}+\mathcal{D}_{1,\perp},\qquad\mathcal{D}_{1,\perp}\triangleq\mathtt{c}(\lambda)\partial_{\theta}-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{H}.

(7.96)

In view of (
7.83
), one has

\displaystyle\forall l\in\mathbb{Z},\quad\forall|j|\geqslant 2,\quad\mathcal{D}_{1,\perp}\mathbf{e}_{l,j}={\rm i}\mu_{j,2}\,\mathbf{e}_{l,j},

(7.97)

with

\displaystyle\mu_{j,2}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=j\left(\tfrac{1}{2}+\varepsilon^{3}\mathtt{c}_{2}(\lambda)\right)-\tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{j}{|j|}\cdot

(7.98)

The main result of this subsection reads as follows.

Proposition 7.10
.

Let
(\tau,\gamma,s_{0},S)
as in (
7.3
). There exists
\varepsilon_{0}>0
small enough such that for any
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}),

there exists a family of linear operators
\big(\mathtt{T}_{n}\big)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfying for any
N\geqslant 0
and
s\in[s_{0},S],

\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\|\mathtt{T}_{n}h\|_{s,N}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\lesssim\gamma^{-1}\|h\|_{s,2\tau+N}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}

and for
\lambda
in the Cantor set

\mathscr{O}_{n}^{2}(\rho)\triangleq\bigcap_{(l,j)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\atop 2\leqslant|j|\leqslant N_{n}}\Big\{\lambda\in\mathscr{O}\quad\textnormal{s.t.}\quad|\varepsilon^{2}\omega(\lambda)l+\mu_{j,2}(\lambda)|\geqslant\gamma|j|^{-\tau}\Big\},

(7.99)

we get

\mathbb{L}_{1,\perp}\mathtt{T}_{n}=\textnormal{Id}+{\mathtt{E}_{n}^{2}},

where
\mathtt{E}_{n}^{2}
satisfies for any
s\in[s_{0},S],

\displaystyle\|\mathtt{E}_{n}^{2}h\|_{s_{0}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\gamma^{-1}N_{n}^{s_{0}-s}\|h\|_{{s,2\tau+1}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}.

Proof.

In view of (
7.96
), one may write

\mathbb{L}_{1,\perp}=\mathtt{L}_{n}+\Pi_{N_{n}}^{\perp}\mathcal{D}_{1,\perp},\qquad\mathtt{L}_{n}\triangleq\varepsilon^{2}\omega(\lambda)\partial_{\varphi}+\Pi_{N_{n}}\mathcal{D}_{1,\perp},

(7.100)

where the projectors
\Pi_{N_{n}}
and
\Pi^{\perp}_{N_{n}}
are defined by

\Pi_{N_{n}}h\triangleq\sum_{\underset{\langle l,j\rangle\leqslant N_{n}}{(l,j)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}}h_{l,j}\mathbf{e}_{l,j}\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad\Pi^{\perp}_{N_{n}}\triangleq\textnormal{Id}-\Pi_{N_{n}}.

Therefore,

\mathtt{L}_{n}\mathbf{e}_{l,j}=\begin{cases}{\rm i}\big(\varepsilon^{2}\omega(\lambda)\,l+\mu_{j,2}\big)\mathbf{e}_{l,j},&\textnormal{if }2\leqslant|j|\leqslant N_{n}\textnormal{ and }l\in\mathbb{Z},\\
{\rm i}\,\varepsilon^{2}\omega(\lambda)\,l\mathbf{e}_{l,j},&\textnormal{if }|j|>N_{n}\textnormal{ and }l\in\mathbb{Z}.\end{cases}

Define the operator
\mathtt{T}_{n}
by

\mathtt{T}_{n}h(\varphi,\theta)\triangleq-{\rm i}\sum_{(l,j)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\atop 2\leqslant|j|\leqslant N_{n}}\tfrac{\chi\left((\varepsilon^{2}\omega(\lambda)\,l+\mu_{j,2})\gamma^{-1}|j|^{\tau}\right)}{\varepsilon^{2}\omega(\lambda)\,l+\mu_{j,2}}h_{l,j}\,\mathbf{e}_{l,j}(\varphi,\theta),

where
\chi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R},[0,1])
is an even positive cut-off function such that

\chi(\xi)=\begin{cases}0,&\textnormal{if }|\xi|\leqslant\frac{1}{3},\\
1,&\textnormal{if }|\xi|\geqslant\frac{1}{2}.\end{cases}

In the Cantor set
\mathscr{O}_{n}^{2}(\rho)
one has

\displaystyle\mathtt{L}_{n}\mathtt{T}_{n}=\hbox{Id}+\Pi_{N_{n}}^{\perp}.

(7.101)

Arguing in a similar way to
[
59
, Prop. 5.3]
one can show that,
for any
N\geqslant 0
,

\displaystyle\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\|\mathtt{T}_{n}h\|_{s,N}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\gamma^{-1}\|h\|_{s,2\tau+N}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}.

(7.102)

Now, from (
7.100
) and (
7.101
) we conclude that on the Cantor set
\mathscr{O}_{n,2}
we have the identity

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}_{1,\perp}\mathtt{T}_{n}=\hbox{Id}+\mathtt{E}_{n}^{2},\qquad\mathtt{E}_{n}^{2}\triangleq\Pi_{N_{n}}^{\perp}+\Pi_{N_{n}}^{\perp}\mathcal{D}_{1,\perp}\mathtt{T}_{n}.

Finally, the estimate on the remainder
\mathtt{E}_{n}^{2}
follows immediately from (
7.102
) and (
7.97
).
This ends the proof of the desired result.
∎

7.5.3
Invertibility of
\mathbb{M}

The next goal is to revisit the matrix operator introduced in (
7.85
) and explore its invertibility. Here is our key result.

Proposition 7.11
.

Under the assumptions of Proposition
7.10
and the smallness condition

\varepsilon^{-1}\gamma+{\varepsilon^{2+{\mu}}\gamma^{-1}\leqslant\varepsilon_{0}},\qquad\|\rho\|_{s_{0}+4\tau+5}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\leqslant 1,

(7.103)

the following holds true.
There exists a family of linear operators
\mathbb{P}_{n}
satisfying for any
s\in[s_{0},S],

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{P}_{n}H\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\leqslant C\gamma^{-1}\Big(\|H\|_{s+2\tau}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}+\|\rho\|_{s+2\tau}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\|H\|_{s_{0}+2\tau}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\Big),

(7.104)

such that in the Cantor set
\mathscr{O}_{n}^{2}
, defined in Proposition
7.10
, we have

\mathbb{M}\mathbb{P}_{n}=\textnormal{Id}_{X_{1}^{s}\times X_{\perp}^{s}}+\mathbb{E}_{n}^{2},

with

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{E}_{n}^{2}H\|_{s_{0}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\gamma^{-1}N_{n}^{s_{0}-s}\Big(\|H\|_{{s+2\tau+1}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}+\|\rho\|_{s+4\tau+5}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\|H\|_{{s_{0}+2\tau}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\Big).

(7.105)

Proof.

Consider the operator

\displaystyle\mathbb{K}_{n}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{L}_{1,1}^{-1}&0\\
0&\mathtt{T}_{n}\end{pmatrix},

where the operator
\mathtt{T}_{n}
was defined in Proposition
7.10
.
For all
\lambda\in\mathscr{O}_{n}^{2}
one has the identity

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}_{1}\mathbb{K}_{n}

\displaystyle=\hbox{Id}_{X_{1}^{s}\times X_{\perp}^{s}}+\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&\mathtt{E}_{n}^{2}\end{pmatrix}.

(7.106)

Moreover, according to Proposition
7.9
, Proposition
7.10
and (
7.103
), for any
H=(h_{1},h_{2})\in X_{1}^{s}\times X_{\perp}^{s}
we have

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{K}_{n}H\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\gamma^{-1}\|H\|_{s,2\tau}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}.

(7.107)

Inserting (
7.86
) into (
7.107
) gives, according to (
7.103
) and
\mu\in(0,1)
,

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{K}_{n}\partial_{\theta}\mathbb{R}_{1}H\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\varepsilon^{2+\mu}\gamma^{-1}\Big(\|H\|_{{s}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}+\|\rho\|_{s+4\tau+5}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\|H\|_{{s_{0}}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\Big).

(7.108)

Consequently, under the condition (
7.103
), the operator

\hbox{Id}_{X_{1}^{s_{0}}\times X_{\perp}^{s_{0}}}+\mathbb{K}_{n}\partial_{\theta}\mathbb{R}_{1}:{X_{1}^{s_{0}}\times X_{\perp}^{s_{0}}}\to{X_{1}^{s_{0}}\times X_{\perp}^{s_{0}}}

is invertible, with

\|(\hbox{Id}_{X_{1}^{s}\times X_{\perp}^{s}}+\mathbb{K}_{n}\partial_{\theta}\mathbb{R}_{1})^{-1}H\|_{s_{0}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\leqslant 2\|H\|_{s_{0}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}.

As to the invertibility for
s\in[s_{0},S]
, one can check by induction from (
7.108
),
\forall m\geqslant 1,

that under the smallness condition (
7.103
) one has

\displaystyle\sum_{m\geqslant 0}\|(\mathbb{K}_{n}\partial_{\theta}\mathbb{R}_{1})^{m}H\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\|H\|_{{s}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}+C\|\rho\|_{s+4\tau+5}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\|H\|_{{s_{0}}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}.

This implies that

\displaystyle\|(\hbox{Id}_{X_{1}^{s}\times X_{\perp}^{s}}+\mathbb{K}_{n}\partial_{\theta}\mathbb{R}_{1})^{-1}H\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\|H\|_{{s}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}+C\|\rho\|_{s+4\tau+5}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\|H\|_{{s_{0}}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}.

(7.109)

Now define the operator

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{n}=\big(\hbox{Id}_{X_{1}^{s}\times X_{\perp}^{s}}+\mathbb{K}_{n}\partial_{\theta}\mathbb{R}_{1}\big)^{-1}\mathbb{K}_{n}.

(7.110)

From (
7.110
), (
7.109
), (
7.107
) and (
7.103
), we obtain (
7.104
).
Moreover, in view of (
7.106
) and (
7.110
) we deduce that in the Cantor set
\mathscr{O}_{n}^{2}
one has

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}\mathbb{P}_{n}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathbb{M}_{1}+\left(\mathbb{M}_{1}\mathbb{K}_{n}-\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&\mathtt{E}_{n}^{2}\end{pmatrix}\right)\partial_{\theta}\mathbb{R}_{1}\right)\left(\hbox{Id}_{X_{1}^{s}\times X_{\perp}^{s}}+\mathbb{K}_{n}\partial_{\theta}\mathbb{R}_{1}\right)^{-1}\mathbb{K}_{n}

(7.111)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{M}_{1}\mathbb{K}_{n}-\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&\mathtt{E}_{n}^{2}\end{pmatrix}\partial_{\theta}\mathbb{R}_{1}\mathbb{P}_{n}

\displaystyle=\hbox{Id}_{X_{1}^{s}\times X_{\perp}^{s}}+\mathbb{E}_{n}^{2},

where

\,\mathbb{E}_{n}^{2}\triangleq\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&\mathtt{E}_{n}^{2}\end{pmatrix}-\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&\mathtt{E}_{n}^{2}\end{pmatrix}\partial_{\theta}\mathbb{R}_{1}\mathbb{P}_{n}.

By virtue of Proposition
7.10
, (
7.86
), (
7.104
) and (
7.103
) one gets (
7.105
).
Finally, by construction, one has the algebraic properties

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}=\begin{pmatrix}\Pi_{1}&0\\
0&\Pi_{1}^{\perp}\end{pmatrix}\mathbb{M}\begin{pmatrix}\Pi_{1}&0\\
0&\Pi_{1}^{\perp}\end{pmatrix}\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad\mathbb{P}_{n}=\begin{pmatrix}\Pi_{1}&0\\
0&\Pi_{1}^{\perp}\end{pmatrix}\mathbb{P}_{n}\begin{pmatrix}\Pi_{1}&0\\
0&\Pi_{1}^{\perp}\end{pmatrix}.

(7.112)

This completes the proof of the desired result.
∎

7.5.4
Invertibility of
{\mathcal{L}_{1}}
and
{\mathcal{L}_{0}}

In this section we intend to explore the existence of an approximate right inverse to the operators
{\mathcal{L}_{1}}
and
{\mathcal{L}_{0}}
defined in (
7.82
) and Proposition
7.8
, respectively.

Proposition 7.12
.

Given the conditions (
7.3
), (
7.61
).
There exists
\epsilon_{0}>0
such that under the assumptions

\varepsilon^{-1}\gamma+\varepsilon^{4+\mu}\gamma^{-1}N_{0}^{\mu_{2}}+{\varepsilon^{2+{\mu}}\gamma^{-1}\leqslant\epsilon_{0}},\qquad\|\rho\|_{2s_{0}+2\tau+4+\frac{3}{2}\mu_{2}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\leqslant 1,

(7.113)

the following assertions holds true.

1.

There exists a family of linear operators
\big({\mathbf{T}}_{n}\big)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfying

\forall\,s\in\,[s_{0},S],\quad\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\|{\mathbf{T}}_{n}h\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\leqslant C\gamma^{-1}\Big(\|h\|_{s+2\tau}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}+\|\rho\|_{s+2\tau}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\|h\|_{s_{0}+2\tau}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\Big)

and such that in the Cantor set
\mathscr{O}_{n}^{2}
, where
\mathscr{O}_{n}^{2}
is introduced in Proposition
7.10
,
we have

{\mathcal{L}_{1}}\,{\mathbf{T}}_{n}=\textnormal{Id}+\mathbf{E}_{n},

where
\mathbf{E}_{n}
satisfies the following estimate

\displaystyle\forall\,s\in[s_{0},S],\quad\|\mathbf{E}_{n}h\|_{s_{0}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\gamma^{-1}N_{n}^{s_{0}-s}\Big(\|h\|_{s+2\tau+1}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}+\|\rho\|_{s+4\tau+5}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\|h\|_{s_{0}+2\tau}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon^{6}\gamma^{-1}N_{0}^{\mu_{2}}N_{n+1}^{-\mu_{2}}\|h\|_{s_{0}+2\tau+2}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}.

2.

Set

\mathcal{T}_{n}\triangleq\mathscr{B}{\mathbf{T}_{n}}\mathscr{B}^{-1}.

Then,

\forall\,s\in\,[s_{0},S],\quad\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\|{\mathcal{T}}_{n}h\|_{s}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\leqslant C\gamma^{-1}\Big(\|h\|_{s+2\tau}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}+\|\rho\|_{s+{4\tau+5}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\|h\|_{s_{0}+2\tau}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\Big).

Moreover, in the Cantor set
\mathscr{O}_{n}^{1}(\rho)\cap\mathscr{O}_{n}^{2}(\rho)

(
see (
7.65
) for
\mathscr{O}_{n}^{1}(\rho))

one has the identity

{\mathcal{L}_{0}}\,{\cal{T}}_{n}=\textnormal{Id}+{\cal{E}}_{n},

where
\mathcal{E}_{n}:=\mathscr{B}{\mathbf{E}_{n}}\mathscr{B}^{-1}
satisfies the estimate

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{E}_{n}h\|_{s_{0}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\gamma^{-1}N_{n}^{s_{0}-s}\big(\|h\|_{s+2\tau+1}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}+\|\rho\|_{s+4\tau{+6}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\|h\|_{s_{0}+2\tau}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\big)

\displaystyle\quad+C\varepsilon^{6}\gamma^{-1}N_{0}^{\mu_{2}}N_{n+1}^{-\mu_{2}}\|h\|_{s_{0}+2\tau+2}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}.

Proof.

In view of (
7.85
), (
7.110
) and (
7.111
) we may write

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}M_{1}&M_{2}\\
M_{3}&M_{4}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\mathbb{P}_{n}=\begin{pmatrix}P_{1}&P_{2}\\
P_{3}&P_{4}\end{pmatrix}\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad\mathbb{E}_{n}^{2}=\begin{pmatrix}E_{n,1}^{2}&E_{n,2}^{2}\\
E_{n,3}^{2}&E_{n,4}^{2}\end{pmatrix}.

It follows from (
7.82
) that

\mathcal{L}_{1}=\sum_{j=1}^{4}M_{j}+\mathtt{E}_{n}.

By denoting

{\mathbf{T}_{n}}=\sum_{i=1}^{4}P_{i}\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad{\mathbf{E}_{n}}=\sum_{i=1}^{4}E_{n,i}^{2}+\mathtt{E}_{n}{\mathbf{T}_{n}}

and using the algebraic structure (
7.112
) and the identity (
7.111
) we conclude that

\mathcal{L}_{1}{\mathbf{T}_{n}}=\hbox{Id}+{\mathbf{E}_{n}}.

The estimates of
\mathbf{T}_{n}
and
\mathbf{E}_{n}
can be easily achieved from Proposition
7.11
and Proposition
7.7
.
The second point follows immediately from the fact that
\mathcal{L}_{1}=\mathscr{B}^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{0}\mathscr{B},
Proposition
7.12
-1 and Proposition
7.8
-1 together with (
7.113
).
This achieves the proof of Proposition
7.12
.∎

7.6
Construction of solutions and Cantor set measure

To construct solutions to the nonlinear equation

\mathbf{F}(\rho)\triangleq\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon^{2+\mu}}\mathbf{G}(\varepsilon r_{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon^{\mu+1}\rho)=0

(7.114)

introduced in (
7.25
), we apply a modified Nash-Moser scheme, in the spirit of
[
59
]
. The tame estimates on the approximate right inverse of
\mathcal{L}_{0}=d_{\rho}\mathbf{F}(\rho)
, obtained in Proposition
7.12
are identical to those found in
[
59
, Prop. 5.5]
. Consequently, the proof of Proposition
7.13
follows the same lines of
[
59
, Prop. 6.1]
. Therefore, we shall omit the proof and only provide a complete statement of the main results. For this end,
we define the finite-dimensional space

E_{n}\triangleq\Big\{h:[\lambda_{*},\lambda^{*}]\times\mathbb{T}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}\quad\textnormal{s.t.}\quad\Pi_{N_{n}}h=h\Big\},

where
\Pi_{N_{n}}
is the projector defined by

h(\varphi,\theta)=\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}\atop j\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}h_{l,j}e^{{\rm i}(l\varphi+j\theta)},\qquad\Pi_{N_{n}}h(\varphi,\theta)\triangleq\sum_{|l|+|j|\leqslant N_{n}}h_{l,j}e^{{\rm i}(l\varphi+j\theta)},

and the sequence
(N_{n})_{n}
was defined in (
7.60
). Here, we will utilize the parameters introduced in (
7.3
), along with the following additional value.

\displaystyle\quad\mathtt{b}_{0}=3-\mu.

(7.115)

We shall also fix the values of
N_{0}
and
\gamma
as below

N_{0}\triangleq\varepsilon^{-{\delta}},\qquad\gamma=\varepsilon^{2+{\delta}}.

(7.116)

Moreover, we shall impose the following constraints required along the Nash-Moser scheme,

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}1+\tau&<&a_{2},\\
3s_{0}+12\tau+15+\tfrac{3}{2}a_{2}&<&a_{1},\\
\frac{2}{3}a_{1}&<&\mu_{2},\\
0<\delta&<&\min\big(\mu,\tfrac{1-\mu}{a_{1}+2},{\tfrac{2+\mu}{1+\mu_{2}}\big)},\\
12\tau+3+\tfrac{6}{\delta}&<&\mu_{1},\\
\max\left(s_{0}+4\tau+3+\frac{2}{3}\mu_{1}+a_{1}+{\frac{4}{\delta}},3s_{0}+4\tau+6+3\mu_{2}\right)&<&b_{1}.\end{array}\right.

(7.117)

We have the following result.

Proposition 7.13

(Nash-Moser scheme)
.

Given the conditions (
7.115
), (
7.116
) and (
7.117
). There exist
C_{\ast}>0
and
{\varepsilon}_{0}>0
such that for any
\varepsilon\in[0,\varepsilon_{0}]
we get for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
the following properties,

•

(\mathcal{P}1)_{n}
There exists a Lipschitz function

\rho_{n}:\begin{array}[t]{rcl}[\lambda_{*},\lambda^{*}]&\rightarrow&E_{n-1}\\
\lambda&\mapsto&\rho_{n}\end{array}

satisfying

\rho_{0}=0\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\,\|\rho_{n}\|_{{2s_{0}+2\tau+3}}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\leqslant C_{\ast}\varepsilon^{\mathtt{b}_{0}}\gamma^{-1}\quad\hbox{for}\quad n\geqslant 1.

By setting

\quad{u}_{n}=\rho_{n}-\rho_{n-1}\quad\hbox{for}\quad n\geqslant 1,

we have

\forall s\in[s_{0},S],\,\|{u}_{1}\|_{s}^{{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}}\leqslant\tfrac{1}{2}C_{\ast}\varepsilon^{\mathtt{b}_{0}}\gamma^{-1}\quad\hbox{and}\quad\|{u}_{k}\|_{{{2s_{0}+2\tau+3}}}^{{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}}\leqslant C_{\ast}\varepsilon^{\mathtt{b}_{0}}\gamma^{-1}N_{k-1}^{-a_{2}}\quad\forall\,\,2\leqslant k\leqslant n.

•

(\mathcal{P}2)_{n}
Set

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{0}=[\lambda_{*},\lambda^{*}]\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\mathcal{A}_{n+1}=\mathcal{A}_{n}\cap\mathcal{O}_{n}^{1}(\rho_{n})\cap\mathcal{O}_{n}^{2}(\rho_{n}),\quad\forall n\in\mathbb{N}.

(7.118)

Then we have the following estimate

\|\mathbf{F}(\rho_{n})\|_{s_{0},\mathcal{A}_{n}}^{{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}}\leqslant C_{\ast}\varepsilon^{\mathtt{b}_{0}}N_{n-1}^{-a_{1}}.

•

(\mathcal{P}3)_{n}
High regularity estimate:
\|\rho_{n}\|_{b_{1}}^{{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}}\leqslant C_{\ast}\varepsilon^{\mathtt{b}_{0}}\gamma^{-1}N_{n-1}^{\mu_{1}}.
Here, we have used the notation (
7.2
).

Next, we shall study the convergence of Nash-Moser scheme, stated in Proposition
7.13
, show that the limit is a solution to the problem (
7.114
) and establish a lower bound for the Lebesgue measure of the final Cantor.

Corollary 7.14
.

There exists
\lambda\in[\lambda_{*},\lambda^{*}]\mapsto\rho_{\infty}
satisfying

\|\rho_{\infty}\|_{2s_{0}+2\tau+3}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\leqslant C_{\ast}\varepsilon^{\mathtt{b}_{0}}\gamma^{-1}\quad\hbox{and}\quad\|\rho_{\infty}-\rho_{m}\|_{2s_{0}+2\tau+3}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}\leqslant C_{\ast}\varepsilon^{\mathtt{b}_{0}}\gamma^{-1}N_{m}^{-a_{2}}

such that

\displaystyle\forall\lambda\in\mathtt{C}_{\infty}\triangleq\bigcap_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{A}_{m},\qquad\mathbf{F}\big(\rho_{\infty}(\lambda)\big)=0.

(7.119)

Proof.

In view of Proposition
7.13
one has for all
m\geqslant 1,

\rho_{m}=\sum_{n=1}^{m}u_{n}.

Define the formal infinite sum

\rho_{\infty}\triangleq\,\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}u_{n}.

Then the claimed estimates follow immediately from the estimates of
(\mathcal{P}1)_{m}
, (
7.60
) and (
7.116
). Thus, the sequence
(\rho_{m})_{m\geqslant 1}
converges pointwise to
\rho_{\infty}
. In view of
(\mathcal{P}2)_{m}
we obtain

\|\mathbf{F}(\rho_{m})\|_{s_{0},\mathtt{C}_{\infty}}^{{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}}\leqslant C_{\ast}\varepsilon^{\mathtt{b}_{0}}N_{m-1}^{-a_{1}}.

Passing to the limit leads to

\forall\lambda\in\mathtt{C}_{\infty},\quad\mathbf{F}\big(\rho_{\infty}(\lambda)\big)=0.

This concludes the proof of Corollary
7.14
.
∎

The following result is proved using standard argument, in similar way to
[
59
, Lem. 6.3]
.

Lemma 7.15
.

Under (
7.117
), we have the inclusion

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\infty}^{1}\cap\mathcal{C}_{\infty}^{2}\subset\mathtt{C}_{\infty},

(7.120)

where, for
k\in\{1,2\},

\mathcal{C}_{\infty}^{k}\triangleq\bigcap_{l\in\mathbb{Z}\atop|j|\geqslant k}\Big\{\lambda\in[\lambda_{*},\lambda^{*}]\quad\textnormal{s.t.}\quad|\varepsilon^{2}\omega(\lambda)l+\mu_{j,k}(\lambda,\rho_{\infty})|\geqslant\tfrac{2\gamma}{|j|^{\tau}}\Big\}

and

\displaystyle\mu_{j,k}(\lambda,\rho)

\displaystyle=j\mathtt{c}(\lambda,\rho)-\tfrac{k-1}{2}\tfrac{j}{|j|}\cdot

In addition, there exists
\varepsilon_{0}>0
and
C>0
such that for any
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})

|[\lambda_{*},\lambda^{*}]\setminus\mathtt{C}_{\infty}|\leqslant C\varepsilon^{\delta}.

Proof.

In view of (
7.65
), (
7.99
), (
7.118
) and (
7.119
) we have

\displaystyle\mathtt{C}_{\infty}=\mathcal{O}_{\infty,1}\cap\mathcal{O}_{\infty,2},

with

\mathcal{O}_{\infty,k}=\bigcap_{(l,j,n)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\times\mathbb{N}\atop k\leqslant|j|\leqslant N_{n}}\Big\{\lambda\in[\lambda_{*},\lambda^{*}]\quad\textnormal{s.t.}\quad|\varepsilon^{2}\omega(\lambda)\,l+\mu_{j,k}(\lambda,\rho_{n})|\geqslant\tfrac{\gamma}{|j|^{\tau}}\Big\}.

We shall prove that for
k\in\{1,2\},

\mathcal{C}_{\infty}^{k}\subset\mathcal{O}_{\infty,k},

(7.121)

which implies the inclusion (
7.120
). Let
\lambda\in\mathcal{C}_{\infty}^{k}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then, from the triangle inequality, we obtain for all
k\leqslant|j|\leqslant N_{n},

\displaystyle|\varepsilon^{2}\omega(\lambda)\,l+\mu_{j,k}(\lambda,\rho_{n})|

\displaystyle\geqslant|\varepsilon^{2}\omega(\lambda)\,l+\mu_{j,k}(\lambda,\rho_{\infty})|-|\mu_{j,k}(\lambda,\rho_{\infty})-\mu_{j,k}(\lambda,\rho_{n})|

\displaystyle\geqslant\tfrac{2\gamma}{|j|^{\tau}}-|\mu_{j,k}(\lambda,\rho_{\infty})-\mu_{j,k}(\lambda,\rho_{n})|.

(7.122)

Using (
7.68
), (
7.116
) and Corollary
7.14
we get for
\varepsilon
small enough

\displaystyle|\mu_{j,k}(\lambda,\rho_{\infty})-\mu_{j,k}(\lambda,\rho_{n})|

\displaystyle\leqslant C\varepsilon^{3}|j|\|\rho_{\infty}-\rho_{n}\|_{2s_{0}+2\tau+3}^{\textnormal{Lip}(\gamma)}

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{\ast}\varepsilon^{3+\mathtt{b}_{0}}\gamma^{-1}|j|N_{n}^{-a_{2}}

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{\ast}\gamma|j|^{-\tau}\varepsilon^{\mathtt{b}_{0}-1-2\delta}N_{n}^{1+\tau-a_{2}}.

From (
7.115
) and the assumption
1+\tau<a_{2}
and
\delta<\frac{2-\mu}{2}
(see (
7.117
)), we conclude that for small
\varepsilon
we have

\displaystyle|\mu_{j,k}(\lambda,\rho_{\infty})-\mu_{j,k}(\lambda,\rho_{n})|

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{\ast}\gamma|j|^{-\tau}\varepsilon^{2-\mu-2\delta}N_{n}^{1+\tau-a_{2}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\gamma|j|^{-\tau}.

Combining the last estimate with (
7.122
) we get, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
l\in\mathbb{Z}
and
k\leqslant|j|\leqslant N_{n}
we get

\displaystyle|\varepsilon^{2}\omega(\lambda)\,l+\mu_{j,k}(\lambda,\rho_{n})|

\displaystyle\geqslant\tfrac{\gamma}{|j|^{\tau}}\cdot

Hence, we deduce that
\lambda\in\mathcal{O}_{\infty,k}
, concluding the proof of (
7.121
). Consequently, one has

\big|[\lambda_{*},\lambda^{*}]\setminus\mathtt{C}_{\infty}\big|\leqslant\big|[\lambda_{*},\lambda^{*}]\setminus\mathcal{C}_{\infty}^{1}\big|+\big|[\lambda_{*},\lambda^{*}]\setminus\mathcal{C}_{\infty}^{2}\big|.

Thus, the measure estimate we only need to prove that

\displaystyle\big|[\lambda_{*},\lambda^{*}]\setminus\mathcal{C}_{\infty}^{k}\big|

\displaystyle\leqslant C\varepsilon^{\delta}.

(7.123)

For this aim we write

\mathcal{C}_{\infty}^{k}=\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\bigcap_{(l,j)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\atop k\leqslant|j|\leqslant N_{n}}\mathcal{U}_{l,j}^{k},

with
